17	**Residual-IA+ architecture:** Raw NLinear branch (primary predictor) + backbone additive residual with per-sample instance-adaptive gate. > AGENT

Here's my honest assessment, updated for April 21:

---

## Acceptance Probability: **55-65%**

I'm actually slightly more conservative than the 58-68% in the memory snapshot. Here's why:

### Strengths (what works for you)

1. **Clear, well-diagnosed phenomenon** — normalization-induced routing collapse is real, reproducible, and non-obvious. The 7 converging controls (especially Timer-XL opposite trajectory) make the causal claim strong.
2. **Massive experimental coverage** — 4,259+ runs, 6 backbones, 6 datasets, 107+ verify.py checks. This is well above the NeurIPS bar for empirical thoroughness.
3. **ρ=-0.88 predictive result** — the R(D) signal-ratio predicting where RR-MoA helps (and where it doesn't) is genuinely interesting and gives the paper scientific value beyond "method X beats method Y."
4. **Honest framing** — you count wins explicitly, acknowledge DLinear gap, use calibration-anchor language. This is what good reviewers reward.

### Vulnerabilities (what could sink it)

1. **Novelty concern** — R4 (theory-leaning reviewer) may score 4-5. The fix (bypass normalization for routing) is, at its core, simple. The MI proposition helps, but it's an application of chain rule, not a new bound. The tightness measurement partially neutralizes this but doesn't eliminate it.

2. **DLinear gap still partially open** — 62/72 is good but not dominant. A skeptical reviewer can say: "On MOMENT-small (your primary backbone), a linear model with no pretraining still wins 10/24 cells. Why should I use a 120M-param foundation model + your adapter?" The R(D) reframing is clever but it's fundamentally a concession.

3. **Scope vs. depth tradeoff** — 37+ pages of appendix signals thoroughness but also raises the "is this one idea stretched too thin?" question. The paper tries to be both a diagnostic paper AND a method paper, which creates tension.

4. **NeurIPS base rate** — acceptance is ~25-28%. Even strong papers get unlucky with reviewer assignments. A panel of {TSFM specialist, MoE specialist, TS generalist} is favorable; {theory, NAS, general ML} is unfavorable.

### Simulated reviewer panel

| Reviewer | Score | Rationale |
|---|---|---|
| R1 (TSFM/PEFT) | **7** | Appreciates backbone coverage, Timer-XL causal proof, practical relevance |
| R2 (MoE/routing) | **6-7** | Likes rescue sweep + collapse diagnosis; may want stronger rescue analysis |
| R3 (TS generalist) | **6-7** | Solid empirics, but may question DLinear gap |
| R4 (Theory) | **5-6** | MI proposition is chain-rule application; novelty insufficient for 7 |

**Mean: ~6.25.** NeurIPS typically needs mean ≥ 6.0 with no score below 5 for acceptance. You're in the accept-borderline zone where the AC's disposition matters a lot.

### What could move the needle

- **Upside risk (+5-10%)**: Cross-backbone expansion finishing strong (pushing 62/72 → 80+/120), favorable reviewer draw, or the H(E|S)≠Δ% finding landing as genuinely surprising.
- **Downside risk (-5-10%)**: Theory reviewer scoring 4, a second reviewer fixating on DLinear gap, or the "obvious fix" framing sticking despite mitigations.

### Bottom line

It's a competitive submission — well above the median NeurIPS paper in experimental rigor, and the diagnosis is real and useful. The main risk is that a reviewer sees the contribution as "skip one line of normalization" and scores accordingly, despite all the surrounding evidence. I'd put it at **coin-flip-plus** — more likely to get in than not, but not comfortably so.

      "text": "Grassmannian Mixture-of-Experts:\nConcentration-Controlled Routing on Subspace\nManifolds\nIbne Farabi Shihab∗\nDepartment of Computer Science\nIowa State University\nishihab@iastate.edu\nSanjeda Akter∗\nDepartment of Computer Science\nIowa State University\nsanjeda@iastate.edu\nAnuj Sharma\nDepartment of Civil, Construction and Environmental Engineering\nIowa State University\nanujs@iastate.edu\nAbstract\nMixture-of-Experts models rely on learned routers to assign tokens to experts, yet\nstandard softmax gating provides no principled mechanism to control the trade\u0002off between sparsity and utilization. We propose Grassmannian MoE (GrMoE),\na routing framework that operates on the Grassmannian manifold of subspaces,\nwhere gating weights arise from the concentration parameters of Matrix Bingham\ndistributions. This construction yields a single, interpretable knob—the concen\u0002tration matrix Λ—that continuously controls routing entropy, replacing discrete\ntop-k selection with a smooth, geometrically principled sparsity mechanism. We\nfurther develop an amortized variational inference procedure for posterior routing\ndistributions, enabling uncertainty-aware expert assignment that naturally resists\nexpert collapse. We formally prove tight bounds relating the Bingham concentra\u0002tion spectrum to routing entropy, expected top-k mass, and an exponential bound\non expert collapse, establishing the first formal theory of concentration-controlled\nsparsity. On synthetic routing tasks, a 350M-parameter MoE language model with\n8 experts, a 1.3B-parameter model with 16 experts, and a 2.7B-parameter model\nwith 32 experts, GrMoE achieves 0% routing collapse across all seeds, compara\u0002ble or better perplexity with 15–30% improved load balance, and a smooth mono\u0002tonic relationship between concentration and effective sparsity that enables post\u0002hoc sparsity tuning without retraining. Token-level analysis reveals that experts\nlearn heterogeneous concentration values that correlate with linguistic specializa\u0002tion, providing interpretable routing behavior.\n1 Introduction\nMixture-of-Experts architectures achieve favorable scaling by activating only a subset of parameters\nper input [Vaswani et al., 2017, Riquelme et al., 2021], with a learned router determining which ex\u0002perts process each token. Despite strong empirical results [Fedus et al., 2022, Lepikhin et al., 2021,\nZhou et al., 2022] and successful deployment at massive scale in systems like Mixtral [Jiang et al.,\n2024] and DeepSeek-V3 [DeepSeek-AI, 2024], current routing mechanisms suffer from fundamen\u0002tal limitations that hinder both training stability and deployment flexibility.\n∗Equal contribution.\nPreprint.\narXiv:2602.17798v1 [cs.LG] 19 Feb 2026\nStandard softmax gating computes router probabilities as g(x) = softmax(Wrx) and selects the\ntop-k experts. This formulation creates three persistent problems. First, expert collapse: a few ex\u0002perts dominate routing, leaving others undertrained and effectively wasted. Chi et al. [2022] formally\n"

      "text": "Load Balancing Mixture of Experts with Similarity\nPreserving Routers\nNabil Omi1,2˚ Siddhartha Sen2 Ali Farhadi1,3\n1University of Washington 2Microsoft Research 3Allen Institute for AI\nAbstract\nSparse Mixture of Experts (MoE) models offer a scalable and efficient architec\u0002ture for training large neural networks by activating only a subset of parameters\n(“experts”) for each input. A learned router computes a distribution over these\nexperts, and assigns input tokens to a small subset. However, without auxiliary\nbalancing mechanisms, routers often converge to using only a few experts, severely\nlimiting model capacity and degrading performance. Most current load balancing\nmechanisms encourage a distribution over experts that resembles a roughly uniform\ndistribution of experts per token. During training, this can result in inconsistent\nrouting behavior, resulting in the model spending its capacity to learn redundant\nknowledge. We address this by introducing a novel load balancing loss that pre\u0002serves token-wise relational structure, encouraging consistent expert choices for\nsimilar inputs during training. Our experimental results show that applying our loss\nto the router results in 36% faster convergence and lower redundancy compared to\na popular load balancing loss.\n1 Introduction\nAs the demand for larger and more capable neural networks continues to grow [Kaplan et al., 2020,\nBrown et al., 2020], the need for architectures that can scale efficiently—without incurring prohibitive\ncomputational costs—has become increasingly important. This is especially true in the context\nof large language models (LLMs), where state-of-the-art performance often requires billions of\nparameters and massive training datasets. One such approach, the Mixture of Experts (MoE) model\n[Shazeer et al., 2017], introduces sparsely activated sub-networks at certain layers, allowing for\nincreased model capacity while preserving computational efficiency.\nWhile MoE architectures offer improved parameter scalability, they often suffer from poor expert\nutilization during pretraining. Without mechanisms that encourage balanced routing, the model\nfrequently learns to rely on only a small subset of experts [Eigen et al., 2014, Bengio et al., 2016].\nTypically, routing decisions are made per token using a learned router that outputs a probability\ndistribution over experts—a paradigm known as Token Choice (TC) [Fedus et al., 2022]. To encourage\nbalanced expert usage, various strategies have been proposed, including sequence-level auxiliary\nlosses such as load balancing loss (LBL) [Fedus et al., 2022] or the Expert Choice (EC) routing\nvariant which generates a distribution over a sparse set of activated tokens for each expert [Zhou et al.,\n2022]. Section 5 covers additional strategies for load balancing.\nLoad balancing strategies often encourage a uniform distribution over experts to avoid collapse. This\napproach has proven to be useful to stabilize MoEs during training, and has been used in "

      "id": "https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.10971",
      "title": "[2511.10971] ERMoE: Eigen-Reparameterized Mixture-of-Experts for Stable Routing and Interpretable Specialization",
      "url": "https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.10971",
      "text": "[2511.10971] ERMoE: Eigen-Reparameterized Mixture-of-Experts for Stable Routing and Interpretable Specialization\n\n# Computer Science > Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition\n\narXiv:2511.10971 (cs)\n\n[Submitted on 14 Nov 2025 ([v1](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.10971v1)), last revised 26 Mar 2026 (this version, v2)]\n\n# Title:ERMoE: Eigen-Reparameterized Mixture-of-Experts for Stable Routing and Interpretable Specialization\n\n[View PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2511.10971) [HTML (experimental)](https://arxiv.org/html/2511.10971v2)\n\n> Abstract:Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures expand model capacity by sparsely activating experts but face two core challenges: misalignment between router logits and each expert's internal structure leads to unstable routing and expert underutilization, and load imbalances create straggler bottlenecks. Standard solutions, such as auxiliary load-balancing losses, can reduce load disparities but often weaken expert specialization and hurt downstream performance. To address these issues, we propose ERMoE, a sparse MoE transformer that reparameterizes each expert in a learned orthonormal eigenbasis and replaces learned gating logits with an \"Eigenbasis Score\", defined as the cosine similarity between input features and an expert's basis. This content-aware routing ties token assignments directly to experts' representation spaces, stabilizing utilization and promoting interpretable specialization without sacrificing sparsity. Crucially, ERMoE removes the need for explicit balancing losses and avoids the interfering gradients they introduce. We show that ERMoE achieves state-of-the-art accuracy on ImageNet classification and cross-modal image-text retrieval benchmarks (e.g., COCO, Flickr30K), while naturally producing flatter expert load distributions. Moreover, a 3D MRI variant (ERMoE-ba) improves brain age prediction accuracy by more than 7\\% and yields anatomically interpretable expert specializations. ERMoE thus introduces a new architectural principle for sparse expert models that directly addresses routing instabilities and enables improved performance with scalable, interpretable specialization.\n\narXiv-issued DOI via DataCite\n\n| Comments: |\n| --- |\n| Subjects: | Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition (cs.CV) |\n| Cite as: | [arXiv:2511.10971](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.10971) [cs.CV] |\n| (or [arXiv:2511.10971v2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.10971v2) [cs.CV] for this version) |\n\n## Submission history\n\nFrom: Anzhe Cheng [[view email](https://arxiv.org/show-email/bbaab269/2511.10971)] [[v1]](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.10971v1) Fri, 14 Nov 2025 05:31:37 UTC (2,564 KB) [v2] Thu, 26 Mar 2026 18:52:49 UTC (2,571 KB)\n\nFull-text links:\n\n## Access Paper:\n\nCurrent browse context:\n\ncs.CV\n\n[< prev](https://arxiv.org/prevnext?id=2511.10971&function=prev&context=cs.CV)| [next >](https://arxiv.org/prevnext?id=2511.10971&function=next&context=cs.CV)\n\nChange to browse by:\n\n### References & Citations\n\n- [Google Scholar](https://scholar.googl",

      "text": "Variational Routing: A Scalable Bayesian Framework for Calibrated\nMixture-of-Experts Transformers\nAlbus Yizhuo Li 1 Matthew Wicker 1\nAbstract\nFoundation models are increasingly being\ndeployed in contexts where understanding the un\u0002certainty of their outputs is critical to ensuring re\u0002sponsible deployment. While Bayesian methods\noffer a principled approach to uncertainty quantifi\u0002cation, their computational overhead renders their\nuse impractical for training or inference at founda\u0002tion model scale. State-of-the-art models achieve\nparameter counts in the trillions through carefully\nengineered sparsity including Mixture-of-Experts\n(MoE) layers. In this work, we demonstrate\ncalibrated uncertainty at scale by introducing Vari\u0002ational Mixture-of-Experts Routing (VMoER),\na structured Bayesian approach for modelling\nuncertainty in MoE layers. VMoER confines\nBayesian inference to the expert-selection stage\nwhich is typically done by a deterministic routing\nnetwork. We instantiate VMoER using two infer\u0002ence strategies: amortised variational inference\nover routing logits and inferring a temperature\nparameter for stochastic expert selection. Across\ntested foundation models, VMoER improves\nrouting stability under noise by 38%, reduces\ncalibration error by 94%, and increases out-of\u0002distribution AUROC by 12%, while incurring\nless than 1% additional FLOPs. These results\nsuggest VMoER offers a scalable path toward\nrobust and uncertainty-aware foundation models.\n1. Introduction\nFoundation models are increasingly deployed in open-world\nsettings characterised by distributional shift, partial ob\u0002servability, and high-stakes decision-making (Bommasani,\n2021; Szpruch et al., 2025). In such settings, the ability to\nquantify uncertainty is central to responsible deployment,\ngoverning when predictions should be trusted, deferred,\n1Department of Computing, Imperial College London. Corre\u0002spondence to: Matthew Wicker.\nPreprint. March 17, 2026.\nor interrogated (Kendall & Gal, 2017; Babbar et al., 2022;\nWicker et al., 2025). However, contemporary foundation\nmodels overwhelmingly rely on deterministic training and\ninference pipelines that obscure epistemic uncertainty and\nyield overconfident predictions outside their training support\n(Gal). While Bayesian methods provide a principled frame\u0002work for uncertainty quantification, their computational\nand memory costs have rendered them largely incompatible\nwith the scale at which modern foundation models operate\n(Wenzel et al., 2020). The tension between the necessity\nof uncertainty-aware reasoning and the engineering realities\nof trillion-parameter architectures has resulted in a critical\ngap between theory and practice. Bridging this gap requires\nuncertainty estimation mechanisms that are both statistically\ngrounded and natively compatible with the sparsity and\nmodularity that enable foundation model scaling.\nMixture-of-Experts (MoE) layers have become the\nstandard for scaling foundation models, replacing dense\nFeed-Forward Networ"

      "text": "Routing-Free Mixture-of-Experts\nYilun Liu∗,†,1,3, Jinru Han∗,2, Sikuan Yan1, Volker Tresp1,3, Yunpu Ma†,1,3\n1 Ludwig Maximilian University of Munich 2 University of California, Los Angeles\n3 Munich Center for Machine Learning ∗ Equal contribution.\n†\nyilun.liu@tum.de cognitive.yunpu@gmail.com\nAbstract\nStandard Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models rely on centralized routing mecha\u0002nisms that introduce rigid inductive biases. We propose Routing-Free MoE which\neliminates any hard-coded centralized designs including external routers, Soft\u0002max, TopK and load balancing, instead encapsulating all activation functionalities\nwithin individual experts and are directly optimized through continuous gradient\nflow, enabling each expert to determine its activation entirely on its own. We\nintroduce a unified adaptive load-balancing framework to simultaneously opti\u0002mize both expert-balancing and token-balancing objectives through a configurable\ninterpolation, allowing flexible and customizable resource allocation. Extensive\nexperiments show that Routing-Free MoE can consistently outperform baselines\nwith better scalability and robustness. We analyze its behavior in detail and offer\ninsights that may facilitate future MoE design and optimization. Code is available\nat https://github.com/liuyilun2000/RoutingFreeMoE/tree/release.\nFigure 1: Standard MoE relies on routing to orchestrate expert activations. Routing-Free MoE let\neach expert purely-independently determine its own activation. To efficiently\nexpand model capacity without proportional computation cost growth, Mixture-of-Experts (MoE)\ndesigns focus on activating subsets of parameters for each input (Shazeer et al., 2017; Fedus et al.,\n2022). This approach presents a fundamental challenge: how to optimally distribute inputs to experts\nwhile satisfying sparsity and balancing considerations.\nExisting MoE designs are hindered by structural limitations across multiple dimensions.       "id": "https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.23362",
      "title": "[2503.23362] Mixture of Routers",
      "url": "https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.23362",
      "publishedDate": "2025-03-30T00:00:00.000Z",
      "author": "[Submitted on 30 Mar 2025 (v1), last revised 16 May 2025 (this version, v2)]",
      "text": "[2503.23362] Mixture of Routers\n\n# Computer Science > Computation and Language\n\narXiv:2503.23362 (cs)\n\n[Submitted on 30 Mar 2025 ([v1](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.23362v1)), last revised 4 Nov 2025 (this version, v3)]\n\n# Title:Mixture of Routers\n\n[View PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2503.23362) [HTML (experimental)](https://arxiv.org/html/2503.23362v3)\n\n> Abstract:Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) is a milestone in aligning large language models with human instructions and adapting them to downstream tasks. In particular, Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) has gained widespread attention due to its parameter efficiency. However, its impact on improving the performance of large models remains limited. Recent studies suggest that combining LoRA with Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) can significantly enhance fine-tuning performance. MoE adapts to the diversity and complexity of datasets by dynamically selecting the most suitable experts, thereby improving task accuracy and efficiency. Despite impressive results, recent studies reveal issues in the MoE routing mechanism, such as incorrect assignments and imbalanced expert allocation. Inspired by the principles of Redundancy and Fault Tolerance Theory. We innovatively integrate the concept of Mixture of Experts into the routing mechanism and propose an efficient fine-tuning method called Mixture of Routers (MoR). It employs multiple sub-routers for joint selection and uses a learnable main router to determine the weights of the sub-routers. The results show that MoR outperforms baseline models on most tasks, achieving an average performance improvement of 1%. MoR can serve as a plug-and-play, parameter-efficient fine-tuning method suitable for a wide range of applications. Our code is available here: [this https URL](https://anonymous.4open.science/r/MoR-DFC6).\n\narXiv-issued DOI via DataCite\n\n| Comments: |\n| --- |\n| Subjects: | Computation and Language (cs.CL); Artificial Intelligence (cs.AI) |\n| Cite as: | [arXiv:2503.23362](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.23362) [cs.CL] |\n| (or [arXiv:2503.23362v3](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.23362v3) [cs.CL] for this version) |\n\n## Submission history\n\nFrom: Jia-Chen Zhang [[view email](https://arxiv.org/show-email/2b1a1f7b/2503.23362)] [[v1]](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.23362v1) Sun, 30 Mar 2025 08:39:09 UTC (197 KB) [[v2]](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.23362v2) Fri, 16 May 2025 14:18:06 UTC (197 KB) [v3] Tue, 4 Nov 2025 10:05:13 UTC (103 KB)\n\nFull-text links:\n\n## Access Paper:\n\nCurrent browse context:\n\ncs.CL\n\n[< prev](https://arxiv.org/prevnext?id=2503.23362&function=prev&context=cs.CL)| [next >](https://arxiv.org/prevnext?id=2503.23362&function=next&context=cs.CL)\n\nChange to browse by:\n\n### References & Citations\n\n- [Google Scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?arxiv_id=2503.23362)\n- [Semantic Scholar](https://api.semanticscholar.org/arXiv:2503.23362)\n\nexport BibTeX citation\n\n### Bookmark\n\nBibliographic Tools\n\n# Bibliographic and Citation Tools\n\nBibliographic Explorer Toggle\n\nBibliogra",

Under review.\nREXMOE: REUSING EXPERTS WITH MINIMAL\nOVERHEAD IN MIXTURE-OF-EXPERTS\nZheyue Tan1,∗ Zhiyuan Li2 Tao Yuan2 Dong Zhou2 Weilin Liu2 Yueqing Zhuang2\nYadong Li2 Guowei Niu2 Cheng Qin3 Zhuyu Yao2 Congyi Liu2 Haiyang Xu2\nBoxun Li2 Guohao Dai4,5,† Bo Zhao1,† Yu Wang6,†\n1 Aalto University 2\nInfinigence-AI 3 Yale University 4 Shanghai Jiao Tong University\n5 Shanghai Innovation Institute 6 Tsinghua University\nABSTRACT\nMixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures have emerged as a promising approach\nto scale Large Language Models (LLMs). MoE boosts the efficiency by activat\u0002ing a subset of experts per token. Recent works show that fine-grained experts\nsubstantially enriches the combinatorial flexibility of active experts and enhances\nmodel expressiveness. However, such a design is fundamentally limited by the\nlayer-local routing mechanism: each layer is restricted to its own expert pool.\nThis requires a careful trade-off between expert dimensionality and routing di\u0002versity given fixed parameter budgets. We describe REXMOE, a novel MoE ar\u0002chitecture that improves routing beyond the existing layer-local approaches by\nallowing routers to reuse experts across adjacent layers. REXMOE decouples ex\u0002pert dimensionality from per-layer budgets, enabling richer expert combinations\nwithout sacrificing individual expert capacity or inflating overall parameters. To\nthis end, we propose a new progressive scaling routing (PSR) strategy to grad\u0002ually increase the candidate expert pool during training. As a result, REXMOE\nimproves both language modeling and downstream task performance. Extensive\nexperiments on models ranging from 0.5B to 7B parameters across different ar\u0002chitectures demonstrate that REXMOE consistently improves performance under\nfixed architectural dimensions, confirming REXMOE as new design paradigm for\nparameter-efficient and scalable MoE-based LLMs.\n1 INTRODUCTION\nLarge Language Models (LLMs) have rapidly advanced in scale and capability, reaching hundreds\nof billions of parameters and demonstrating remarkable progress toward Artificial General Intelli\u0002gence (AGI). Recent foundation models (Achiam et al., 2023; OpenAI, 2025; Meta AI, 2025; Guo\net al., 2025; AI, 2025; Yang et al., 2025) have exhibited strong performance across complex tasks\nin multiple domains. This progress has been driven by massive investments in data and compute,\nbut such growth also intensifies the tension between model capacity and development practicality.\nGiven the substantial costs involved, Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures have become an in\u0002creasingly attractive alternative. By dynamically activating only a subset of specialized experts per\ninput, MoEs can match or even exceed the performance of dense counterparts while significantly\nreducing inference-time computational demands (Shazeer et al., 2017; Lepikhin et al., 2020; Fedus\net al., 2022; Du et al., 2022; Jiang et al., 2024; Liu et al., 2024a;b; Yang et al., 2025).\nComparing to the dense counterparts, a "

      "id": "http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16057v2",
      "title": "[2503.16057v2] Expert Race: A Flexible Routing Strategy for Scaling Diffusion Transformer with Mixture of Experts",
      "url": "http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16057v2",
      "text": "[2503.16057v2] Expert Race: A Flexible Routing Strategy for Scaling Diffusion Transformer with Mixture of Experts\n\n -->\n\n# Computer Science > Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition\n\narXiv:2503.16057v2 (cs)\n\n[Submitted on 20 Mar 2025 ([v1](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16057v1)), revised 25 Mar 2025 (this version, v2), latest version 12 Jun 2025 ([v3](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16057v3))]\n\n# Title:Expert Race: A Flexible Routing Strategy for Scaling Diffusion Transformer with Mixture of Experts\n\nAuthors: [Yike Yuan](https://arxiv.org/search/cs?searchtype=author&query=Yuan,+Y), [Ziyu Wang](https://arxiv.org/search/cs?searchtype=author&query=Wang,+Z), [Zihao Huang](https://arxiv.org/search/cs?searchtype=author&query=Huang,+Z), [Defa Zhu](https://arxiv.org/search/cs?searchtype=author&query=Zhu,+D), [Xun Zhou](https://arxiv.org/search/cs?searchtype=author&query=Zhou,+X), [Jingyi Yu](https://arxiv.org/search/cs?searchtype=author&query=Yu,+J), [Qiyang Min](https://arxiv.org/search/cs?searchtype=author&query=Min,+Q)\n\n[View PDF](http://arxiv.org/pdf/2503.16057v2) [HTML (experimental)](https://arxiv.org/html/2503.16057v2)\n\n> Abstract:Diffusion models have emerged as mainstream framework in visual generation. Building upon this success, the integration of Mixture of Experts (MoE) methods has shown promise in enhancing model scalability and performance. In this paper, we introduce Race-DiT, a novel MoE model for diffusion transformers with a flexible routing strategy, Expert Race. By allowing tokens and experts to compete together and select the top candidates, the model learns to dynamically assign experts to critical tokens. Additionally, we propose per-layer regularization to address challenges in shallow layer learning, and router similarity loss to prevent mode collapse, ensuring better expert utilization. Extensive experiments on ImageNet validate the effectiveness of our approach, showcasing significant performance gains while promising scaling properties.\n\n \n\n[https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2503.16057](https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2503.16057)\n\n \n\narXiv-issued DOI via DataCite\n\n| Subjects: | Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition (cs.CV); Artificial Intelligence (cs.AI); Machine Learning (cs.LG) |\n| --- | --- |\n| Cite as: | [arXiv:2503.16057](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16057) [cs.CV] |\n| (or [arXiv:2503.16057v2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16057v2) [cs.CV] for this version) |\n\n## Submission history\n\nFrom: Yike Yuan [[view email](http://arxiv.org/show-email/2ff45e21/2503.16057)] [[v1]](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16057v1) Thu, 20 Mar 2025 11:45:08 UTC (25,106 KB) [v2] Tue, 25 Mar 2025 08:56:54 UTC (30,442 KB) [[v3]](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.16057v3) Thu, 12 Jun 2025 07:41:53 UTC (12,808 KB)\n\n \n\nFull-text links:\n\n## Access Paper:\n\n- [View PDF](http://arxiv.org/pdf/2503.16057v2)\n- [HTML (experimental)](https://arxiv.org/html/2503.16057v2)\n- [TeX Source](http://arxiv.org/src/2503.16057v2)\n\n[view license](http://arxiv.org/licenses/nonexclusive-distrib/1.0/)",

      "title": "Adapting Time Series Foundation Models through Data Mixtures",
      "url": "https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.02840",
      "publishedDate": "2026-03-03T00:00:00.000Z",
      "text": "[2603.02840] Adapting Time Series Foundation Models through Data Mixtures\n\n# Computer Science > Machine Learning\n\narXiv:2603.02840 (cs)\n\n[Submitted on 3 Mar 2026]\n\n# Title:Adapting Time Series Foundation Models through Data Mixtures\n\nAuthors: [Thomas L. Lee](https://arxiv.org/search/cs?searchtype=author&query=Lee,+T+L), [Edoardo M. Ponti](https://arxiv.org/search/cs?searchtype=author&query=Ponti,+E+M), [Amos Storkey](https://arxiv.org/search/cs?searchtype=author&query=Storkey,+A)\n\n[View PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2603.02840) [HTML (experimental)](https://arxiv.org/html/2603.02840v1)\n\n> Abstract:Time series foundation models (TSFMs) have become increasingly popular for zero-shot forecasting. However, for a new time series domain not fully covered by the pretraining set, performance can suffer. Therefore, when a practitioner cares about a new domain and has access to a set of related datasets, the question arises: how best to fine-tune a TSFM to improve zero-shot forecasting? A typical approach to this type of problem is to fine-tune a LoRA module on all datasets or separately on each dataset. Tuning a separate module on each dataset allows for the specialisation of the TSFM to different types of data distribution, by selecting differing combinations of per-dataset modules for different time series contexts. However, we find that, using per-dataset modules might not be optimal, since a time series dataset can contain data from several types of distributions, i.e. sub-domains. This can be due to the distribution shifting or having differing distributions for different dimensions of the time series. Hence, we propose MixFT which re-divides the data using Bayesian mixtures into sets that best represent the sub-domains present in the data, and fine-tunes separately on each of these sets. This re-division of the data ensures that each set is more homogeneous, leading to fine-tuned modules focused on specific sub-domains. Our experiments show that MixFT performs better than per-dataset methods and when fine-tuning a single module on all the data. This suggests that by re-partitioning the data to represent sub-domains we can better specialise TSFMs to improve zero-shot forecasting.\n\narXiv-issued DOI via DataCite\n\n| Comments: |\n| --- |\n| Subjects: | Machine Learning (cs.LG); Machine Learning (stat.ML) |\n| Cite as: | [arXiv:2603.02840](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.02840) [cs.LG] |\n| (or [arXiv:2603.02840v1](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.02840v1) [cs.LG] for this version) |\n\n## Submission history\n\nFrom: Thomas Lee [[view email](https://arxiv.org/show-email/a306df86/2603.02840)] [v1] Tue, 3 Mar 2026 10:38:54 UTC (387 KB)\n\nFull-text links:\n\n## Access Paper:\n\nCurrent browse context:\n\ncs.LG\n\n[< prev](https://arxiv.org/prevnext?id=2603.02840&function=prev&context=cs.LG)| [next >](https://arxiv.org/prevnext?id=2603.02840&function=next&context=cs.LG)\n\nChange to browse by:\n\n### References & Citations\n\n- [Google Scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?arxiv_id=",

      "id": "https://arxiv.org/html/2603.02840v1",
      "title": "Adapting Time Series Foundation Models through Data Mixtures",
      "url": "https://arxiv.org/html/2603.02840v1",

      "text": "Adapting Time Series Foundation Models through Data Mixtures\n\nTitle:\n\nDescription:\n\n# Adapting Time Series Foundation Models through Data Mixtures\n\nThomas L. Lee Edoardo M. Ponti Amos Storkey\n\n###### Abstract\n\nTime series foundation models (TSFMs) have become increasingly popular for zero-shot forecasting. However, for a new time series domain not fully covered by the pretraining set, performance can suffer. Therefore, when a practitioner cares about a new domain and has access to a set of related datasets, the question arises: how best to fine-tune a TSFM to improve zero-shot forecasting? A typical approach to this type of problem is to fine-tune a LoRA module on all datasets or separately on each dataset. Tuning a separate module on each dataset allows for the specialisation of the TSFM to different types of data distribution, by selecting differing combinations of per-dataset modules for different time series contexts. However, we find that, using per-dataset modules might not be optimal, since a time series dataset can contain data from several types of distributions, i.e. sub-domains. This can be due to the distribution shifting or having differing distributions for different dimensions of the time series. Hence, we propose MixFT which re-divides the data using Bayesian mixtures into sets that best represent the sub-domains present in the data, and fine-tunes separately on each of these sets. This re-division of the data ensures that each set is more homogeneous, leading to fine-tuned modules focused on specific sub-domains. Our experiments show that MixFT performs better than per-dataset methods and when fine-tuning a single module on all the data. This suggests that by re-partitioning the data to represent sub-domains we can better specialise TSFMs to improve zero-shot forecasting.\n\nMachine Learning, ICML\n\n## 1 Introduction\n\nFigure 1: MixFT identifies and trains separate LoRA modules on different sub-domains given in the fine-tuning datasets. This is unlike previous approaches which either separately fine-tune LoRA modules using dataset boundaries (per-dataset methods) or trained a single LoRA module on all the data (Shared) (Ostapenko et al., 2024). By training separate LoRA modules on sub-domains, MixFT aims for the training data for each LoRA to be more homogeneous, leading to more specialized and consistent LoRA modules. This also should lead to better identifiability of what LoRA modules to use when zero-shot forecasting.\n\nTime series foundation models (TSFMs) have become increasingly popular for forecasting (Miller et al., 2024; Ye et al., 2024; Liang et al., 2024). A key reason for this is their zero-shot forecasting ability (Ansari et al., 2024; Woo et al., 2024; Darlow et al., 2024). This allows for the production of forecasts where there is little historical data, expertise or compute to fit a model on the particular time series of interest (Mercier et al., 2021). However, while there might not be much historical data for a parti",

      "text": "Adapting Time Series Foundation Models through Data Mixtures\nThomas L. Lee 1 Edoardo M. Ponti 1 Amos Storkey 1\nAbstract\nTime series foundation models (TSFMs) have be\u0002come increasingly popular for zero-shot forecast\u0002ing. However, for a new time series domain not\nfully covered by the pretraining set, performance\ncan suffer. Therefore, when a practitioner cares\nabout a new domain and has access to a set of\nrelated datasets, the question arises: how best to\nfine-tune a TSFM to improve zero-shot forecast\u0002ing? A typical approach to this type of problem is\nto fine-tune a LoRA module on all datasets or sep\u0002arately on each dataset. Tuning a separate module\non each dataset allows for the specialisation of the\nTSFM to different types of data distribution, by se\u0002lecting differing combinations of per-dataset mod\u0002ules for different time series contexts. However,\nwe find that, using per-dataset modules might not\nbe optimal, since a time series dataset can con\u0002tain data from several types of distributions, i.e.\nsub-domains. This can be due to the distribution\nshifting or having differing distributions for dif\u0002ferent dimensions of the time series. Hence, we\npropose MixFT which re-divides the data using\nBayesian mixtures into sets that best represent the\nsub-domains present in the data, and fine-tunes\nseparately on each of these sets. This re-division\nof the data ensures that each set is more homoge\u0002neous, leading to fine-tuned modules focused on\nspecific sub-domains. Our experiments show that\nMixFT performs better than per-dataset methods\nand when fine-tuning a single module on all the\ndata. This suggests that by re-partitioning the data\nto represent sub-domains we can better specialise\nTSFMs to improve zero-shot forecasting.\n1. Introduction\nTime series foundation models (TSFMs) have become in\u0002creasingly popular for forecasting (Miller et al., 2024; Ye\net al., 2024; Liang et al., 2024). A key reason for this is their\nzero-shot forecasting ability (Ansari et al., 2024; Woo et al.,\n1\nSchool of Informatics, University of Edinburgh. Correspon\u0002dence to: Thomas L. Lee.\nPreprint. March 4, 2026.\n2024; Darlow et al., 2024). This allows for the production\nof forecasts where there is little historical data, expertise\nor compute to fit a model on the particular time series of\ninterest (Mercier et al., 2021). However, while there might\nnot be much historical data for a particular time series, often\na practitioner will have access to other related time series.\nHence, a question arises of how to best adapt the TSFM\nwith these other related time series to improve zero-shot\nperformance on the time series of interest. This question is\nof particular importance when the time series of interest is\nfrom a domain outside of the pretraining set of the TSFM.\nThis is because, it has been observed that zero-shot perfor\u0002mance can suffer greatly for these time series (Toner et al.,\n2025; Diao et al., 2024; Aksu et al., 2024), requiring the\nneed for TSFM adaptation to obtain useful forecasts."

      "id": "http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17872",
      "title": "[2505.17872] Mixture of Low Rank Adaptation with Partial Parameter Sharing for Time Series Forecasting",
      "url": "http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17872",
      "text": "[2505.17872] Mixture of Low Rank Adaptation with Partial Parameter Sharing for Time Series Forecasting\n\n -->\n\n# Computer Science > Machine Learning\n\narXiv:2505.17872 (cs)\n\n[Submitted on 23 May 2025 ([v1](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17872v1)), last revised 27 May 2025 (this version, v2)]\n\n# Title:Mixture of Low Rank Adaptation with Partial Parameter Sharing for Time Series Forecasting\n\nAuthors: [Licheng Pan](https://arxiv.org/search/cs?searchtype=author&query=Pan,+L), [Zhichao Chen](https://arxiv.org/search/cs?searchtype=author&query=Chen,+Z), [Haoxuan Li](https://arxiv.org/search/cs?searchtype=author&query=Li,+H), [Guangyi Liu](https://arxiv.org/search/cs?searchtype=author&query=Liu,+G), [Zhijian Xu](https://arxiv.org/search/cs?searchtype=author&query=Xu,+Z), [Zhaoran Liu](https://arxiv.org/search/cs?searchtype=author&query=Liu,+Z), [Hao Wang](https://arxiv.org/search/cs?searchtype=author&query=Wang,+H), [Ying Wei](https://arxiv.org/search/cs?searchtype=author&query=Wei,+Y)\n\n[View PDF](http://arxiv.org/pdf/2505.17872) [HTML (experimental)](https://arxiv.org/html/2505.17872v2)\n\n> Abstract:Multi-task forecasting has become the standard approach for time-series forecasting (TSF). However, we show that it suffers from an Expressiveness Bottleneck, where predictions at different time steps share the same representation, leading to unavoidable errors even with optimal representations. To address this issue, we propose a two-stage framework: first, pre-train a foundation model for one-step-ahead prediction; then, adapt it using step-specific LoRA [this http URL](http://modules.this/) design enables the foundation model to handle any number of forecast steps while avoiding the expressiveness bottleneck. We further introduce the Mixture-of-LoRA (MoLA) model, which employs adaptively weighted LoRA experts to achieve partial parameter sharing across steps. This approach enhances both efficiency and forecasting performance by exploiting interdependencies between forecast steps. Experiments show that MoLA significantly improves model expressiveness and outperforms state-of-the-art time-series forecasting methods. Code is available at [this https URL](https://anonymous.4open.science/r/MoLA-BC92).\n\n \n\n[https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2505.17872](https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2505.17872)\n\n \n\narXiv-issued DOI via DataCite\n\n| Subjects: | Machine Learning (cs.LG); Artificial Intelligence (cs.AI) |\n| --- | --- |\n| Cite as: | [arXiv:2505.17872](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17872) [cs.LG] |\n| (or [arXiv:2505.17872v2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17872v2) [cs.LG] for this version) |\n\n## Submission history\n\nFrom: Licheng Pan [[view email](http://arxiv.org/show-email/a2bc1a96/2505.17872)] [[v1]](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17872v1) Fri, 23 May 2025 13:24:39 UTC (974 KB) [v2] Tue, 27 May 2025 07:23:28 UTC (966 KB)\n\n \n\nFull-text links:\n\n## Access Paper:\n\n- [View PDF](http://arxiv.org/pdf/2505.17872)\n- [HTML (experimental)](https://arxiv.org/html/2505.17872v2)\n- [TeX Source](http:",

      "text": "In-Context Fine-Tuning for Time-Series Foundation Models\nAbhimanyu Das∗ Matthew Faw† Rajat Sen∗ Yichen Zhou∗\nNovember 1, 2024\nAbstract\nMotivated by the recent success of time-series foundation models for zero-shot forecasting,\nwe present a methodology for in-context fine-tuning of a time-series foundation model. In\nparticular, we design a pretrained foundation model that can be prompted (at inference time)\nwith multiple time-series examples, in order to forecast a target time-series into the future. Our\nfoundation model is specifically trained to utilize examples from multiple related time-series in\nits context window (in addition to the history of the target time-series) to help it adapt to the\nspecific distribution of the target domain at inference time. We show that such a foundation\nmodel that uses in-context examples at inference time can obtain much better performance\non popular forecasting benchmarks compared to supervised deep learning methods, statistical\nmodels, as well as other time-series foundation models. Interestingly, our in-context fine-tuning\napproach even rivals the performance of a foundation model that is explicitly fine-tuned on the\ntarget domain.\n1 Introduction\nTime-series data is ubiquitous in several domains such as retail, finance, manufacturing, healthcare,\nand natural sciences. In many of these domains, time-series forecasting, i.e., predicting time-series\ninto the future, is a critical problem – for example, in applications like retail forecasting, climate and\nweather predictions, and traffic forecasting. In the last decade, deep learning approaches [SFGJ20;\nOCCB19; SYD19] have become popular in forecasting, often outperforming statistical approaches\nlike ARIMA [BJ68]. However, until recently, deep learning approaches for forecasting have adhered\nto the traditional supervised machine learning framework of having to train a forecasting model\non task-specific training data, before being able to perform forecasting for that task. On the other\nhand, in Natural Language Processing (NLP), Large Language Models (LLMs) [RWCLAS+19;\nBro+20] have shown the promise of foundation models: a single pretrained model can perform well\nand adapt to tasks like translation, code generation, text summarization during inference time in\na zero-shot or few-shot manner.\nMotivated by the success in NLP, there has been significant work in recent years on time\u0002series foundation models for forecasting, ranging from re-purposing LLMs directly for forecast\u0002ing [GFQW23] to fine-tuning pretrained LLMs on time-series data [ZNWSJ23; CPC23] to pre\u0002training time-series foundation models from scratch [DKSZ24; GSCCLD24; WLKXSS24; Ans+24;\nGM23]. The last approach in particular has been shown to obtain strong zero-shot accuracy, rivaling\nthe best supervised models trained specifically for the target datasets.\n∗Google Research.\n†The University of Texas at Austin. Correspondence to: {matthewfaw@utexas.edu}. This work was done while the\nauthor was a Student Rese"

      "text": "In-Context Fine-Tuning for Time-Series Foundation Models\nMatthew Faw 1 Rajat Sen 2 Yichen Zhou 2 Abhimanyu Das 2\nAbstract\nMotivated by the recent success of time-series\nfoundation models for zero-shot forecasting, we\npresent a methodology for in-context fine-tuning\nof a time-series foundation model. In particu\u0002lar, we design a pretrained foundation model that\ncan be prompted (at inference time) with multiple\ntime-series examples, in order to forecast a target\ntime-series into the future. Our foundation model\nis specifically trained to utilize examples from\nmultiple related time-series in its context window\n(in addition to the history of the target time-series)\nto help it adapt to the specific distribution of the\ntarget domain at inference time. We show that\nsuch a foundation model that uses in-context ex\u0002amples at inference time can obtain much better\nperformance on popular forecasting benchmarks\ncompared to supervised deep learning methods,\nstatistical models, and other time series founda\u0002tion models. Interestingly, our in-context fine\u0002tuning approach even matches the performance\nof a foundation model that is explicitly fine-tuned\non the target domain.\n1. In many of these domains, time-series forecasting,\ni.e., predicting time-series into the future, is a critical prob\u0002lem – for example, in applications like retail forecasting, cli\u0002mate and weather predictions, and traffic forecasting. In the\nlast decade, deep learning approaches (Salinas et al., 2020;\nOreshkin et al., 2020; Sen et al., 2019) have become popular\nin forecasting, often outperforming statistical approaches\nlike ARIMA (Box & Jenkins, 1968). However, until re\u0002cently, deep learning approaches for forecasting have ad\u00021Georgia Institute of Technology. Copyright 2025\nby the author(s).\nhered to the traditional supervised machine learning frame\u0002work of having to train a forecasting model on task-specific\ntraining data, before being able to perform forecasting for\nthat task. On the other hand, in Natural Language Process\u0002ing (NLP), Large Language Models (LLMs) (Radford et al.,\n2019; Brown et al., 2020) have shown the promise of foun\u0002dation models: a single pretrained model can perform well\nand adapt to tasks like translation, code generation during\ninference time in a zero-shot or few-shot manner.\nMotivated by the success in NLP, there has been significant\nwork in recent years on time-series foundation models for\nforecasting, ranging from re-purposing LLMs directly for\nforecasting (Gruver et al., 2023) to fine-tuning pretrained\nLLMs on time-series data (Zhou et al., 2023; Chang et al.,\n20"

      "id": "https://arxiv.org/pdf/2502.10235",
      "title": "",
      "url": "https://arxiv.org/pdf/2502.10235",
      "text": "AdaPTS: Adapting Univariate Foundation Models to Probabilistic Multivariate\nTime Series Forecasting\nAbdelhakim Benechehab 1 2 Vasilii Feofanov 1 Giuseppe Paolo 1 Albert Thomas 1 Maurizio Filippone 3\nBalazs K ´ egl ´\n1\nAbstract\nPre-trained foundation models (FMs) have shown\nexceptional performance in univariate time se\u0002ries forecasting tasks. However, several practi\u0002cal challenges persist, including managing intri\u0002cate dependencies among features and quantify\u0002ing uncertainty in predictions. This study aims\nto tackle these critical limitations by introducing\nadapters—feature-space transformations that fa\u0002cilitate the effective use of pre-trained univariate\ntime series FMs for multivariate tasks. Adapters\noperate by projecting multivariate inputs into a\nsuitable latent space and applying the FM inde\u0002pendently to each dimension. Inspired by the\nliterature on representation learning and partially\nstochastic Bayesian neural networks, we present\na range of adapters and optimization/inference\nstrategies. Experiments conducted on both syn\u0002thetic and real-world datasets confirm the effi\u0002cacy of adapters, demonstrating substantial en\u0002hancements in forecasting accuracy and uncer\u0002tainty quantification compared to baseline meth\u0002ods. Our framework, AdaPTS, positions adapters\nas a modular, scalable, and effective solution for\nleveraging time series FMs in multivariate con\u0002texts, thereby promoting their wider adoption in\nreal-world applications. We release the code at\nhttps://github.com/abenechehab/AdaPTS.\n1. Introduction\nTime series forecasting is a well-established machine learn\u0002ing problem that involves analyzing sequential data to pre\u0002dict future trends based on historical patterns. Two key\nchallenges frequently arise in this context: (a) time series\nare often multivariate, incorporating multiple descriptive\n1Huawei Noah’s Ark Lab, Paris, France 2Department\nof Data Science, EURECOM 3Statistics Program, KAUST.\nCorrespondence to: Abdelhakim Benechehab.\nPreprint, under review. (b) The AdaPTS frame\u0002work: The input time series is transformed through a feature\nspace transformation φ that maps into a stochastic latent\nspace. The prediction is then conducted using a pre-trained\nFM before transforming back the predicted, now distribu\u0002tion, to the original feature space. The fire symbol indicate\ntrainable weights while the snowflake implicates that the\nparameters of the FM are kept frozen.\nfeatures (Wei, 2019), and (b) estimating the uncertainty of a\nforecast is equally important, requiring probabilistic model\noutputs (Gneiting & Katzfuss, 2014). These challenges are\nparticularly relevant in real-world applications where risk\nassessment depends on reliable forecasts, such as health\u0002care "

      "id": "https://arxiv.org/pdf/2506.14087",
      "title": "",
      "url": "https://arxiv.org/pdf/2506.14087",
      "text": "Multi-Scale Finetuning for Encoder-based Time Series\nFoundation Models\nZhongzheng Qiao1,2,3 Chenghao Liu4 Yiming Zhang1 Ming Jin5 Quang Pham4\nQingsong Wen6 P.N.Suganthan7 Xudong Jiang1 Savitha Ramasamy2,3\nAbstract\nTime series foundation models (TSFMs) demonstrate impressive zero-shot per\u0002formance for time series forecasting. However, an important yet underexplored\nchallenge is how to effectively finetune TSFMs on specific downstream tasks.\nWhile naive finetuning can yield performance gains, we argue that it falls short\nof fully leveraging TSFMs’ capabilities, often resulting in overfitting and subop\u0002timal performance. Given the diverse temporal patterns across sampling scales\nand the inherent multi-scale forecasting capabilities of TSFMs, we adopt a causal\nperspective to analyze finetuning process, through which we highlight the critical\nimportance of explicitly modeling multiple scales and reveal the shortcomings of\nnaive approaches. Focusing on encoder-based TSFMs, we propose MultiScale\nFineTuning (MSFT), a simple yet general framework that explicitly integrates\nmulti-scale modeling into the finetuning process. Experimental results on three\ndifferent backbones (MOIRAI, MOMENT and UNITS) demonstrate that TSFMs\nfinetuned with MSFT not only outperform naive and typical parameter efficient\nfinetuning methods but also surpass state-of-the-art deep learning methods. Codes\nare available at https://github.com/zqiao11/MSFT.\n1 Introduction\nTime series foundation models (TSFMs) have emerged as a transformative direction within the time\nseries forecasting (TSF) community [2, 43, 8]. By pretraining on extensive time series datasets, these\nmodels possess universal knowledge, enabling them to achieve impressive zero-shot performance\non various forecasting tasks. Despite significant advancements in TSFM research, current studies\npredominantly focus on model pretraining and zero-shot evaluation, while paying limited attention to\nthe critical challenge of effectively finetuning these universal models for specific downstream tasks.\nIn contrast, finetuning pretrained models has become the standard pipeline for real-world applications\nin domains such as natural language processing (NLP) and computer vision (CV). Research in these\nfields has revealed key challenges in finetuning foundation models, including preserving pretrained\nknowledge [24], avoiding overfitting [15], and ensuring efficient adaptation [13, 48].\nExisting finetuning strategies for TSFMs often rely on naive approaches, such as full finetuning or\nlinear probing [2, 12, 11]. While these methods may offer performance gains, we argue that naive\nfinetuning is suboptimal for TSFMs as it fails to account for the intrinsic multi-scale properties of\nboth time series data and TSFMs. As a data modality generated from continuous real-world processes,\ntime series are inherently entangled and can be decomposed across multiple scales [23, 18]. A time\nseries can exhibit distinct temporal patterns at different sampl"

      "title": "Dual Adaptation of Time-Series Foundation Models for Financial Forecasting",
      "url": "https://openreview.net/pdf?id=SSdBpVNYxd",
      "author": "Fatemeh Chitsaz, Saman Heratizadeh",
      "text": "Dual Adaptation of Time-Series Foundation Models for Financial Forecasting\nFatemeh Chitsaz * 1 Saman Heratizadeh * 1\nAbstract\nRecent progress in time-series foundation mod\u0002els has expanded forecasting capabilities across\ndomains. However, their application to finance\nremains constrained by data scarcity, volatility,\nand overfitting. We present Dual Adaptation, a\nlightweight adaptation of the TimesFM founda\u0002tion model for financial forecasting, featuring a\ndual-module design: a Generalizer Adapter that\nlearns broad temporal patterns across assets and\nan Identity Signature module that captures asset\u0002specific signals, forming a lightweight layer tuned\non top of a frozen foundation model. The method\nis evaluated in both in-domain and zero-shot set\u0002tings, showing improved forecasting performance\ncompared to the frozen model and common tuning\nbaselines. To enhance generalization, the Iden\u0002tity Signature is removed during inference, allow\u0002ing the Generalizer Adapter to apply the shared\nknowledge it has learned to unseen assets. This\ndesign improves both stability and cross-asset gen\u0002eralization, offering a practical solution for adapt\u0002ing large models to noisy, low-resource financial\nforecasting tasks.\n1. Introduction\nTime-series forecasting is a major challenge in finance. Fi\u0002nancial data are often noisy, nonstationary, and limited\nin volume, which makes generalization across assets dif\u0002ficult. Both classical models and modern deep learning\napproaches frequently overfit and perform poorly on unseen\ninstruments(Han et al., 2019).\nFoundation models, which are large architectures pre\u0002trained on massive time-series corpora, have shown strong\nfew-shot and zero-shot capabilities on structured forecasting\n*Equal contribution 1\nSchool of Intelligent Systems, Univer\u0002sity of Tehran, Tehran, Iran. Correspondence to: Fatemeh Chit\u0002saz, Saman Heratizadeh.\nProceedings of the 1\nst ICML Workshop on Foundation Models for\nStructured Data, Vancouver, Canada. 2025. Copyright 2025 by\nthe author(s).\nbenchmarks(Cao et al., 2024). TimesFM, a decoder-style\nattention-based model, is one of the most prominent exam\u0002ples(Das et al., 2024). However, its training corpus contains\nlimited exposure to financial data, and its performance in\nfinancial settings remains largely untested. As a result, its\nability to model noisy and irregular price trajectories cannot\nbe assumed.\nFull fine-tuning of TimesFM on financial datasets such as\nthe SP100 can improve in-domain performance, but it is\ncomputationally intensive, sensitive to initialization, and\noffers only marginal improvements in generalization to un\u0002seen assets. Lightweight alternatives like LoRA, bias tuning,\nand layer-norm tuning are far more efficient and work well\nwithin the training domain, but they tend to exhibit reduced\nperformance when evaluated on out-of-domain financial\ninstruments.\nTo address these limitations, we introduce Dual Adaptation,\na lightweight dual-module adaptation strategy for finan\u0002cial forecasting. It consist"

      "id": "https://arxiv.org/pdf/2505.15151",
      "title": "",
      "url": "https://arxiv.org/pdf/2505.15151",
      "text": "arXiv:2505.15151v1 [cs.LG] 21 May 2025\nJOURNAL OF LATEX CLASS FILES, VOL. 18, NO. 9, SEPTEMBER 20201\nTime Tracker: Mixture-of-Experts-Enhanced Foundation Time Series\nForecasting Model with Decoupled Training Pipelines\nXiaohou Shi, Ke Li, Aobo Liang and Yan Sun\nAbstract—In the past few years, time series foundation models\nhave achieved superior predicting accuracy. However, real-world\ntime series often exhibit significant diversity in their temporal\npatterns across different time spans and domains, making it\nchallenging for a single model architecture to fit all complex\nscenarios. In addition, time series data may have multiple vari\u0002ables exhibiting complex correlations between each other. Recent\nmainstream works have focused on modeling times series in a\nchannel-independent manner in both pretraining and finetuning\nstages, overlooking the valuable inter-series dependencies. To\nthis end, we propose Time Tracker for better predictions on\nmultivariate time series data. Firstly, we leverage sparse mixture\nof experts (MoE) within Transformers to handle the modeling\nof diverse time series patterns, thereby alleviating the learning\ndifficulties of a single model while improving its generalization.\nBesides, we propose Any-variate Attention, enabling a unified\nmodel structure to seamlessly handle both univariate and mul\u0002tivariate time series, thereby supporting channel-independent\nmodeling during pretraining and channel-mixed modeling for\nfinetuning. Furthermore, we design a graph learning module that\nconstructs relations among sequences from frequency-domain\nfeatures, providing more precise guidance to capture inter-series\ndependencies in channel-mixed modeling. Based on these ad\u0002vancements, Time Tracker achieves state-of-the-art performance\nin predicting accuracy, model generalization and adaptability.\nIndex Terms—Time series forecasting, foundation model, mix\u0002ture of experts, graph learning\nI. INTRODUCTION\nR\nECENT advances in time series forecasting have demon\u0002strated the effectiveness of foundation models, with\nTransformers emerging as the pivotal architecture. With the\nauxiliary information of endogenous, exogenous variables and\ncross-domain features from historical contexts, time series\nfoundation models are designed to accommodate a wider\nspectrum of prediction scenarios. The concept of unified\nforecasting is gradually reshaping the conventional practice\nof task-specific training strategy.\nHowever, existing models face performance bottlenecks in\nmultivariate forecasting. Firstly, as shown in Fig. 1, real\u0002world time series are inherently heterogeneous: (a) within a\nsingle sequence, and across different variables from (b) the\nsame or (c) different scenarios. The complex temporal patterns\nand shifting data distributions pose significant challenges for\na single model to extract high-quality features. Meanwhile,\nrecent research has mainly focused on building encoder-only\narchitectures to model temporal dependencies across tokens.\nS. Xiao and K. Li are w"

      "text": "Routing-Free Mixture-of-Experts\n\nTitle:\n\nDescription:\n\n# Routing-Free Mixture-of-Experts\n\nYilun Liu∗,†,1,3, Jinru Han∗,2, Sikuan Yan1, Volker Tresp1,3, Yunpu Ma†,1,3 1 Ludwig Maximilian University of Munich 2 University of California, Los Angeles 3 Munich Center for Machine Learning ∗ Equal contribution. † yilun.liu@tum.de cognitive.yunpu@gmail.com\n\n###### Abstract\n\nStandard Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models rely on centralized routing mechanisms that introduce rigid inductive biases. We propose Routing-Free MoE which eliminates any hard-coded centralized designs including external routers, Softmax, TopK and load balancing, instead encapsulating all activation functionalities within individual experts and are directly optimized through continuous gradient flow, enabling each expert to determine its activation entirely on its own. We introduce a unified adaptive load-balancing framework to simultaneously optimize both expert-balancing and token-balancing objectives through a configurable interpolation, allowing flexible and customizable resource allocation. Extensive experiments show that Routing-Free MoE can consistently outperform baselines with better scalability and robustness. We analyze its behavior in detail and offer insights that may facilitate future MoE design and optimization. Code is available at [https://github.com/liuyilun2000/RoutingFreeMoE/tree/release](https://github.com/liuyilun2000/RoutingFreeMoE/tree/release).\n\nFigure 1: Standard MoE relies on routing to orchestrate expert activations. Routing-Free MoE let each expert purely-independently determine its own activation. Green indicates activated components; red for inactive components; yellow for trainable components.\n\n## 1 Introduction\n\nThe scalability of transformer-based Large Language Models (LLMs) (Vaswani et al., 2017) is constrained by the substantial computational resources required (Kaplan et al., 2020). To efficiently expand model capacity without proportional computation cost growth, Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) designs focus on activating subsets of parameters for each input (Shazeer et al., 2017; Fedus et al., 2022). This approach presents a fundamental challenge: how to optimally distribute inputs to experts while satisfying sparsity and balancing considerations.\n\nExisting MoE designs are hindered by structural limitations across multiple dimensions. Standard MoE relies on small, external routers that lack sufficient capacity for storing expert capabilities to determine expert preference for each input, forcing them to learn their prediction via indirect trial-and-error optimization, which inevitably leads to suboptimal routing and unstable early training dynamics (Lv et al., 2025). For computational efficiency, conventional MoE enforces rigid, global constraints on expert activation that ignore input-specific dynamics.       "text": "NeurIPS Poster $\\textit{Read-ME}$: Refactorizing LLMs as Router-Decoupled Mixture of Experts with System Co-Design NeurIPS 2024\n\nPoster\n\n# $\\textit{Read-ME}$: Refactorizing LLMs as Router-Decoupled Mixture of Experts with System Co-Design\n\nRuisi Cai ⋅ Yeonju Ro ⋅ Geon-Woo Kim ⋅ Peihao Wang ⋅ Babak Ehteshami Bejnordi ⋅ Aditya Akella ⋅ Zhangyang \"Atlas\" Wang\n\n2024 Poster\n\n[[Paper](https://proceedings.neurips.cc/paper_files/paper/2024/hash/d298cf34e4539f9134db7f38b42f69fe-Abstract-Conference.html)] [[Slides](https://neurips.cc/media/neurips-2024/Slides/94027_QHq3Vxt.pdf)] [[Poster](https://neurips.cc/media/PosterPDFs/NeurIPS%202024/94027.png?t=1731742141.6920156)] [[OpenReview](https://openreview.net/forum?id=i8JaxY7tDI)]\n\n### Abstract\n\nThe proliferation of large language models (LLMs) has led to the adoption of Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures that dynamically leverage specialized subnetworks for improved efficiency and performance. Despite their benefits, MoE models face significant challenges during inference, including inefficient memory management and suboptimal batching, due to misaligned design choices between the model architecture and the system policies. Furthermore, the conventional approach of training MoEs from scratch is increasingly prohibitive in terms of cost. In this paper, we propose a novel framework $\\textit{Read-ME}$ that transforms pre-trained dense LLMs into smaller MoE models (in contrast to ``upcycling\" generalist MoEs), avoiding the high costs of ground-up training. Our approach employs activation sparsity to extract experts. To compose experts, we examine the widely-adopted layer-wise router design and show its redundancy, and thus we introduce the pre-gating router decoupled from the MoE backbone that facilitates system-friendly pre-computing and lookahead scheduling, enhancing expert-aware batching and caching.Our codesign therefore addresses critical gaps on both the algorithmic and system fronts, establishing a scalable and efficient alternative for LLM inference in resource-constrained settings.$\\textit{Read-ME}$ outperforms other popular open-source dense models of similar scales, achieving improvements of up to 10.1\\% on MMLU, and improving mean end-to-end latency up to 6.1\\%. Codes are available at: \\url{https://github.com/VITA-Group/READ-ME}.\n\nShow more\n\n### Video\n\nChat is not available.\n\nSuccessful Page Load",

      "title": "[2412.14711] ReMoE: Fully Differentiable Mixture-of-Experts with ReLU Routing",
      "url": "https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.14711",
      "text": "[2412.14711] ReMoE: Fully Differentiable Mixture-of-Experts with ReLU Routing\n\n# Computer Science > Machine Learning\n\narXiv:2412.14711 (cs)\n\n[Submitted on 19 Dec 2024 ([v1](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.14711v1)), last revised 27 Feb 2025 (this version, v2)]\n\n# Title:ReMoE: Fully Differentiable Mixture-of-Experts with ReLU Routing\n\nAuthors: [Ziteng Wang](https://arxiv.org/search/cs?searchtype=author&query=Wang,+Z), [Jun Zhu](https://arxiv.org/search/cs?searchtype=author&query=Zhu,+J), [Jianfei Chen](https://arxiv.org/search/cs?searchtype=author&query=Chen,+J)\n\n[View PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2412.14711) [HTML (experimental)](https://arxiv.org/html/2412.14711v2)\n\n> Abstract:Sparsely activated Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models are widely adopted to scale up model capacity without increasing the computation budget. However, vanilla TopK routers are trained in a discontinuous, non-differentiable way, limiting their performance and scalability. To address this issue, we propose ReMoE, a fully differentiable MoE architecture that offers a simple yet effective drop-in replacement for the conventional TopK+Softmax routing, utilizing ReLU as the router instead. We further propose methods to regulate the router's sparsity while balancing the load among experts. ReMoE's continuous nature enables efficient dynamic allocation of computation across tokens and layers, while also exhibiting domain specialization. Our experiments demonstrate that ReMoE consistently outperforms vanilla TopK-routed MoE across various model sizes, expert counts, and levels of granularity. Furthermore, ReMoE exhibits superior scalability with respect to the number of experts, surpassing traditional MoE architectures. The implementation based on Megatron-LM is available at [this https URL](https://github.com/thu-ml/ReMoE).\n\narXiv-issued DOI via DataCite\n\n| Comments: |\n| --- |\n| Subjects: | Machine Learning (cs.LG) |\n| Cite as: | [arXiv:2412.14711](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.14711) [cs.LG] |\n| (or [arXiv:2412.14711v2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.14711v2) [cs.LG] for this version) |\n\n## Submission history\n\nFrom: Ziteng Wang [[view email](https://arxiv.org/show-email/9e3d0509/2412.14711)] [[v1]](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.14711v1) Thu, 19 Dec 2024 10:21:20 UTC (792 KB) [v2] Thu, 27 Feb 2025 16:33:09 UTC (795 KB)\n\nFull-text links:\n\n## Access Paper:\n\nCurrent browse context:\n\ncs.LG\n\n[< prev](https://arxiv.org/prevnext?id=2412.14711&function=prev&context=cs.LG)| [next >](https://arxiv.org/prevnext?id=2412.14711&function=next&context=cs.LG)\n\nChange to browse by:\n\n### References & Citations\n\n- [Google Scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?arxiv_id=2412.14711)\n- [Semantic Scholar](https://api.semanticscholar.org/arXiv:2412.14711)\n\nexport BibTeX citation\n\n### Bookmark\n\nBibliographic Tools\n\n# Bibliographic and Citation Tools\n\nBibliographic Explorer Toggle\n\nBibliographic Explorer ([What is the Explorer?](https://info.arxiv.org/labs/showcase.html#arxiv-bibliographic-explorer))\n\nConnected Pa",

      "id": "https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14900",
      "title": "[2509.14900] FURINA: Free from Unmergeable Router via LINear Aggregation of mixed experts",
      "url": "https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14900",
      "text": "[2509.14900] FURINA: Free from Unmergeable Router via LINear Aggregation of mixed experts\n\n -->\n\n# Computer Science > Computation and Language\n\narXiv:2509.14900 (cs)\n\n[Submitted on 18 Sep 2025 ([v1](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14900v1)), last revised 25 Sep 2025 (this version, v2)]\n\n# Title:FURINA: Free from Unmergeable Router via LINear Aggregation of mixed experts\n\nAuthors: [Jiayi Han](https://arxiv.org/search/cs?searchtype=author&query=Han,+J), [Liang Du](https://arxiv.org/search/cs?searchtype=author&query=Du,+L), [Yinda Chen](https://arxiv.org/search/cs?searchtype=author&query=Chen,+Y), [Xiao Kang](https://arxiv.org/search/cs?searchtype=author&query=Kang,+X), [Weiyang Ding](https://arxiv.org/search/cs?searchtype=author&query=Ding,+W), [Donghong Han](https://arxiv.org/search/cs?searchtype=author&query=Han,+D)\n\n[View PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2509.14900) [HTML (experimental)](https://arxiv.org/html/2509.14900v2)\n\n> Abstract:The Mixture of Experts (MoE) paradigm has been successfully integrated into Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) for parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT), delivering performance gains with minimal parameter overhead. However, a key limitation of existing MoE-LoRA methods is their reliance on a discrete router, which prevents the integration of the MoE components into the backbone model. To overcome this, we propose FURINA, a novel Free from Unmergeable Router framework based on the LINear Aggregation of experts. FURINA eliminates the router by introducing a Self-Routing mechanism. This is achieved through three core innovations: (1) decoupled learning of the direction and magnitude for LoRA adapters, (2) a shared learnable magnitude vector for consistent activation scaling, and (3) expert selection loss that encourages divergent expert activation. The proposed mechanism leverages the angular similarity between the input and each adapter's directional component to activate experts, which are then scaled by the shared magnitude vector. This design allows the output norm to naturally reflect the importance of each expert, thereby enabling dynamic, router-free routing. The expert selection loss further sharpens this behavior by encouraging sparsity and aligning it with standard MoE activation patterns. We also introduce a shared expert within the MoE-LoRA block that provides stable, foundational knowledge. To the best of our knowledge, FURINA is the first router-free, MoE-enhanced LoRA method that can be fully merged into the backbone model, introducing zero additional inference-time cost or complexity. Extensive experiments demonstrate that FURINA not only significantly outperforms standard LoRA but also matches or surpasses the performance of existing MoE-LoRA methods, while eliminating the extra inference-time overhead of MoE.\n\n \n\n[https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2509.14900](https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2509.14900)\n\n \n\narXiv-issued DOI via DataCite\n\n| Comments: |\n| --- |\n| Subjects: | Computation and Language (cs.CL) |\n| Cite as: | ",

      "id": "https://arxiv.org/html/2601.21349v1",
      "title": "L2R: Low-Rank and Lipschitz-Controlled Routing for Mixture-of-Experts",
      "url": "https://arxiv.org/html/2601.21349v1",
      "text": "L2R: Low-Rank and Lipschitz-Controlled Routing for Mixture-of-Experts\n\n# L2R: Low-Rank and Lipschitz-Controlled Routing for Mixture-of-Experts\n\nMinghao Yang Ren Togo Guang Li Takahiro Ogawa Miki Haseyama\n\n###### Abstract\n\nMixture-of-Experts (MoE) models scale neural networks by conditionally activating a small subset of experts, where the router plays a central role in determining expert specialization and overall model performance. However, many modern MoE systems still adopt linear routers in raw high-dimensional representation spaces, where representation mismatch, angular concentration, and scale-sensitive scoring can jointly undermine routing discriminability and stable expert specialization. In this work, we propose Low-rank & Lipschitz-controlled Routing (L2R), a unified routing framework that reshapes both the routing space and scoring geometry. L2R performs expert assignment in a shared low-rank latent routing space and introduces Saturated Inner-Product Scoring (SIPS) to explicitly control the Lipschitz behavior of routing functions, yielding smoother and more stable routing geometry. In addition, L2R incorporates a parameter-efficient multi-anchor routing mechanism to enhance expert expressiveness. Extensive experiments on a large-scale language MoE model and a vision MoE setting on ImageNet demonstrate that L2R consistently improves routing stability, expert specialization, and overall model performance.\n\nDeep Learning\n\nEmail: {yang, togo, guang, ogawa, mhaseyama}@lmd.ist.hokudai.ac.jp\n\n## 1 Introduction\n\nMixture-of-Experts (MoE) has emerged as a leading paradigm for scaling deep neural networks across a wide range of domains, including large language models (LLMs) (Muennighoff et al., [2025](https://arxiv.org/html/2601.21349v1#bib.bib1); Dai et al., [2024](https://arxiv.org/html/2601.21349v1#bib.bib2); Jiang et al., [2024](https://arxiv.org/html/2601.21349v1#bib.bib8); Yang et al., [2024](https://arxiv.org/html/2601.21349v1#bib.bib13)) and vision backbones (Riquelme et al., [2021](https://arxiv.org/html/2601.21349v1#bib.bib3); Chen et al., [2023](https://arxiv.org/html/2601.21349v1#bib.bib9); Yang et al., [2025](https://arxiv.org/html/2601.21349v1#bib.bib10)). By enabling conditional computation, MoE decouples model capacity from inference cost and is designed to realize meaningful expert specialization (Cai et al., [2025](https://arxiv.org/html/2601.21349v1#bib.bib12)). Central to achieving this objective is the router. As the mechanism that assigns tokens to experts under sparse and dynamic activation, the router effectively defines the functional topology of the model, governing how capacity is allocated and coordinated. Consequently, the quality of routing is a fundamental factor that determines the expressiveness, stability, and upper performance bound of MoE models (Dikkala et al., [2023](https://arxiv.org/html/2601.21349v1#bib.bib5)).\n\nFigure 1: Comparison between the linear router (left) and the proposed L2R framework (right)",

      "text": "RASA: Routing-Aware Safety Alignment for Mixture-of-Experts Models\nJiacheng Liang 1 Yuhui Wang 1 Tanqiu Jiang 1 Ting Wang 1\nAbstract\nMixture-of-Experts (MoE) language models in\u0002troduce unique challenges for safety alignment\ndue to their sparse routing mechanisms, which\ncan enable degenerate optimization behaviors un\u0002der standard full-parameter fine-tuning. In our\npreliminary experiments, we observe that naively\napplying full-parameter safety fine-tuning to MoE\nmodels can reduce attack success rates through\nrouting or expert dominance effects, rather than by\ndirectly repairing Safety-Critical Experts. To ad\u0002dress this challenge, we propose RASA, a routing\u0002aware expert-level alignment framework that ex\u0002plicitly repairs Safety-Critical Experts while pre\u0002venting routing-based bypasses. RASA identifies\nexperts disproportionately activated by success\u0002ful jailbreaks, selectively fine-tunes only these\nexperts under fixed routing, and subsequently\nenforces routing consistency with safety-aligned\ncontexts. Across two representative MoE architec\u0002tures and a diverse set of jailbreak attacks, RASA\nachieves near-perfect robustness, strong cross\u0002attack generalization, and substantially reduced\nover-refusal, while preserving general capabili\u0002ties on benchmarks such as MMLU, GSM8K,\nand TruthfulQA. Our results suggest that ro\u0002bust MoE safety alignment benefits from tar\u0002geted expert repair rather than global parame\u0002ter updates, offering a practical and architecture\u0002preserving alternative to prior approaches. Correspondence to: Ting Wang\n.\nPreprint.       "id": "https://www.arxiv.org/pdf/2509.25913v2",
      "title": "",
      "url": "https://www.arxiv.org/pdf/2509.25913v2",
      "text": "Preprint\nUNDERSTANDING THE MIXTURE-OF-EXPERTS WITH\nNADARAYA-WATSON KERNEL\nChuanyang Zheng1∗ Jiankai Sun2 Yihang Gao3 Enze Xie4 Yuehao Wang5\nPeihao Wang 5 Ting Xu6 Matthew Chang7 Liliang Ren8 Jingyao Li6\nJing Xiong9 Kashif Rasul1 Mac Schwager2 Anderson Schneider1 Atlas Wang5\nYuriy Nevmyvaka1\n1Morgan Stanley 2Stanford 3NUS 4Nvidia 5UT Austin\n6CUHK 7Meta 8Microsoft Research 9HKU\nABSTRACT\nMixture-of-Experts (MoE) has become a cornerstone in recent state-of-the-art large\nlanguage models (LLMs). Traditionally, MoE relies on Softmax as the router\nscore function to aggregate expert output, a designed choice that has persisted from\nthe earliest MoE models to modern LLMs, and is now widely regarded as standard\npractice. However, the necessity of using Softmax to project router weights into\na probability simplex remains an unchallenged assumption rather than a princi\u0002pled design choice. In this work, we first revisit the classical Nadaraya–Watson\nregression and observe that MoE shares the same mathematical formulation as\nNadaraya–Watson regression. Furthermore, we show that both feed-forward neural\nnetwork (FFN) and MoE can be interpreted as a special case of Nadaraya–Watson\nregression, where the kernel function corresponds to the input neurons of the\noutput layer. Motivated by these insights, we propose the zero-additional-cost\nKernel Inspired Router with Normalization (KERN), an FFN-style router func\u0002tion, as an alternative to Softmax. We demonstrate that this router generalizes\nboth Sigmoid- and Softmax-based routers. Based on empirical observations and\nestablished practices in FFN implementation, we recommend the use of ReLU\nactivation and ℓ2-normalization in KERN router function. Comprehensive\nexperiments in MoE and LLM validate the effectiveness of the proposed FFN-style\nrouter function KERN.\n1 INTRODUCTION\nRecent years have witnessed remarkable progress in Large Language Models (LLMs) (Brown et al.,\n2020; Ouyang et al., 2022; Touvron et al., 2023), driven primarily by the exponential growth of\ntraining data and model parameters. With the mixture of experts (MoE), there is great progress in\nlanguage modeling (Fedus et al., 2022; Puigcerver et al., 2024; Jiang et al., 2024; Meta; Liu et al.,\n2024a; Team et al., 2025) and computer vision (Riquelme et al., 2021; Lin et al., 2024b). The MoE\narchitecture (Jacobs et al., 1991; Shazeer et al., 2017; Roller et al., 2021) has emerged as an efficient\nalternative that allows parameter scaling while maintaining manageable computational requirements.\nThe successful integration of MoE with Transformer architectures (Vaswani et al., 2017) has led to\nthe development of exceptionally large yet efficient language models (Dai et al., 2024; Jiang et al.,\n2024; Shen et al., 2024; Wei et al., 2024), demonstrating the tremendous potential of this approach.\nA critical and widely adopted design choice in modern MoE architectures is the use of the Softmax\nfunction as the core routing mechanism. This approach, prominently featu"
