    {
      "id": "https://arxiv.org/html/2508.12801v1",
      "title": "Maximum Score Routing For Mixture-of-Experts",
      "url": "https://arxiv.org/html/2508.12801v1",
      "text": "Maximum Score Routing For Mixture-of-Experts \n\n# Maximum Score Routing For Mixture-of-Experts\n\nBowen Dong1 Yilong Fan2 Yutao Sun1 Zhenyu Li1 Tengyu Pan1 Xun Zhou3†\\dagger Jianyong Wang1†\\dagger 1Tsinghua University 2Tianjin University 3Seed-Foundation-Model Team ByteDance dbw22@mails.tsinghua.edu.cn\n\n###### Abstract\n\nRouting networks in sparsely activated mixture-of-experts (MoE) dynamically allocate input tokens to top-k experts through differentiable sparse transformations, enabling scalable model capacity while preserving computational efficiency. Traditional MoE networks impose an expert capacity constraint to ensure GPU-friendly computation. However, this leads to token dropping when capacity is saturated and results in low hardware efficiency due to padding in underutilized experts. Removing the capacity constraint, in turn, compromises load balancing and computational efficiency. To address these issues, we propose Maximum Score Routing (MaxScore), a novel MoE routing paradigm that models routing as a minimum-cost maximum-flow problem and integrates a SoftTopk\\mathrm{SoftTopk} operator. MaxScore resolves the fundamental limitations of iterative rerouting and optimal transport formulations, achieving lower training losses and higher evaluation scores at equivalent FLOPs compared to both constrained and unconstrained baselines. Implementation details and experimental configurations can be obtained from [https://github.com/dongbw18/MaxScore.git](https://github.com/dongbw18/MaxScore.git).\n\nMaximum Score Routing For Mixture-of-Experts\n\n††footnotetext: † indicates corresponding authors.\n\n## 1 INTRODUCTION\n\nThe Mixture of Experts (MoE) paradigm has emerged as a compelling architectural strategy for scaling neural networks while maintaining computational efficiency. This approach dynamically combines multiple subsets of parameters (experts) by a learnable routing network, aiming to improve model capacity and computational efficiency. The routing network of sparsely activated MoE Shazeer et al. ([2017](https://arxiv.org/html/2508.12801v1#bib.bib33)) dynamically allocates input tokens to top-k experts through differentiable sparse transformations, enabling conditional computation that scales model parameters without proportionally increasing FLOPs.\n\nSoftmax is conventionally employed to compute token-expert affinity coefficients in MoE routing networks, which promotes inter-expert competition. To mitigate winner-takes-all and preserve load balance, both hard constraints using expert capacity Eigen et al. ([2014](https://arxiv.org/html/2508.12801v1#bib.bib13)), and soft constraints using auxiliary losses Bengio et al. ([2016](https://arxiv.org/html/2508.12801v1#bib.bib3)), are incorporated into the routing network Shazeer et al. ([2017](https://arxiv.org/html/2508.12801v1#bib.bib33)). GShard Lepikhin et al. ([2020](https://arxiv.org/html/2508.12801v1#bib.bib23)) pioneers the integration of MoE with Transformer architectures Vaswani et al. ([2017](htt",

We propose Routing-Free MoE which\neliminates any hard-coded centralized designs including external routers, Soft\u0002max, TopK and load balancing, instead encapsulating all activation functionalities\nwithin individual experts and are directly optimized through continuous gradient\nflow, enabling each expert to determine its activation entirely on its own. We\nintroduce a unified adaptive load-balancing framework to simultaneously opti\u0002mize both expert-balancing and token-balancing objectives through a configurable\ninterpolation, allowing flexible and customizable resource allocation. Extensive\nexperiments show that Routing-Free MoE can consistently outperform baselines\nwith better scalability and robustness. We analyze its behavior in detail and offer\ninsights that may facilitate future MoE design and optimization. To efficiently\nexpand model capacity without proportional computation cost growth, Mixture-of-Experts (MoE)\ndesigns focus on activating subsets of parameters for each input (Shazeer et al., 2017; Fedus et al.,\n2022). This approach presents a fundamental challenge: how to optimally distribute inputs to experts\nwhile satisfying sparsity and balancing considerations.\nExisting MoE designs are hindered by structural limitations across multiple dimensions.       "text": "[2504.21190] TT-LoRA MoE: Unifying Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning and Sparse Mixture-of-Experts\n\n# Computer Science > Machine Learning\n\narXiv:2504.21190 (cs)\n\n[Submitted on 29 Apr 2025]\n\n# Title:TT-LoRA MoE: Unifying Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning and Sparse Mixture-of-Experts\n\n[View PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2504.21190) [HTML (experimental)](https://arxiv.org/html/2504.21190v1)\n\n> Abstract:We propose Tensor-Trained Low-Rank Adaptation Mixture of Experts (TT-LoRA MoE), a novel computational framework integrating Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) with sparse MoE routing to address scalability challenges in large model deployments. Unlike traditional MoE approaches, which face substantial computational overhead as expert counts grow, TT-LoRA MoE decomposes training into two distinct, optimized stages. First, we independently train lightweight, tensorized low-rank adapters (TT-LoRA experts), each specialized for specific tasks. Subsequently, these expert adapters remain frozen, eliminating inter-task interference and catastrophic forgetting in multi-task setting. A sparse MoE router, trained separately, dynamically leverages base model representations to select exactly one specialized adapter per input at inference time, automating expert selection without explicit task specification. Comprehensive experiments confirm our architecture retains the memory efficiency of low-rank adapters, seamlessly scales to large expert pools, and achieves robust task-level optimization. This structured decoupling significantly enhances computational efficiency and flexibility: uses only 2% of LoRA, 0.3% of Adapters and 0.03% of AdapterFusion parameters and outperforms AdapterFusion by 4 value in multi-tasking, enabling practical and scalable multi-task inference deployments.\n\narXiv-issued DOI via DataCite\n\nDOI(s) linking to related resources\n\n| Subjects: | Machine Learning (cs.LG); Artificial Intelligence (cs.AI) |\n| --- | --- |\n| Report number: | 10.1145/3712285 |\n| Cite as: | [arXiv:2504.21190](https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.21190) [cs.LG] |\n| (or [arXiv:2504.21190v1](https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.21190v1) [cs.LG] for this version) |\n| Journal reference: | In Proceedings of the International Conference for High Performance Computing, Networking, Storage and Analysis (SC 2025), ACM, 2025 |\n| Related DOI: |\n\n## Submission history\n\nFrom: Pradip Kunwar [[view email](https://arxiv.org/show-email/5187d0b4/2504.21190)] [v1] Tue, 29 Apr 2025 21:46:43 UTC (1,571 KB)\n\nFull-text links:\n\n## Access Paper:\n\nCurrent browse context:\n\ncs.LG\n\n[< prev](https://arxiv.org/prevnext?id=2504.21190&function=prev&context=cs.LG)| [next >](https://arxiv.org/prevnext?id=2504.21190&function=next&context=cs.LG)\n\nChange to browse by:\n\n### References & Citations\n\n- [Google Scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?arxiv_id=2504.21190)\n- [Semantic Scholar](https://api.semanticscholar.org/arXiv:2504.21190)\n\nexport BibTeX citation\n\n### Bookmark\n\nBibliographic Tools\n\n# Bibliographic and Cit",

      "id": "https://neurips.cc/virtual/2025/poster/117357",
      "title": "PT-MoE: An Efficient Finetuning Framework for Integrating Mixture-of ...",
      "url": "https://neurips.cc/virtual/2025/poster/117357",
      "publishedDate": "2025-09-19T10:20:14.000Z",
      "text": "NeurIPS Poster PT-MoE: An Efficient Finetuning Framework for Integrating Mixture-of-Experts into Prompt Tuning NeurIPS 2025\n\nPoster Thu, Dec 4, 2025 • 11:00 AM – 2:00 PM PST\n\n# PT-MoE: An Efficient Finetuning Framework for Integrating Mixture-of-Experts into Prompt Tuning\n\nZongqian Li ⋅ Yixuan Su ⋅ Nigel Collier\n\n[[Poster](https://neurips.cc/media/PosterPDFs/NeurIPS%202025/117357.png?t=1762538539.1911392)] [[OpenReview](https://openreview.net/forum?id=ZSAWYtIwGg)]\n\n### Abstract\n\nParameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) methods have shown promise in adapting large language models, yet existing approaches exhibit counter-intuitive phenomena: integrating either matrix decomposition or mixture-of-experts (MoE) individually decreases performance across tasks, though decomposition improves results on specific domains despite reducing parameters, while MoE increases parameter count without corresponding decrease in training efficiency. Motivated by these observations and the modular nature of PT, we propose PT-MoE, a novel framework that integrates matrix decomposition with MoE routing for efficient PT. Evaluation results across 17 datasets demonstrate that PT-MoE achieves state-of-the-art performance in both question answering (QA) and mathematical problem solving tasks, improving F1 score by 1.49 points over PT and 2.13 points over LoRA in QA tasks, while improving mathematical accuracy by 10.75 points over PT and 0.44 points over LoRA, all while using 25% fewer parameters than LoRA. Our analysis reveals that while PT methods generally excel in QA tasks and LoRA-based methods in math datasets, the integration of matrix decomposition and MoE in PT-MoE yields complementary benefits: decomposition enables efficient parameter sharing across experts while MoE provides dynamic adaptation, collectively enabling PT-MoE to demonstrate cross-task consistency and generalization abilities. These findings, along with ablation studies on routing mechanisms and architectural components, provide insights for future PEFT methods.\n\nShow more\n\n### Video\n\nChat is not available.\n\nSuccessful Page Load",

Comprehensive experiments confirm our architecture retains\nthe memory efficiency of low-rank adapters, seamlessly scales to\nlarge expert pools, and achieves robust task-level optimization. This\nstructured decoupling significantly enhances computational effi\u0002ciency and flexibility: uses only 2% of LoRA, 0.3% of Adapters and\n0.03% of AdapterFusion parameters and outperforms AdapterFu\u0002sion by 4 value in multi-tasking, enabling practical and scalable\nmulti-task inference deployments.\nCCS Concepts\n• Computing methodologies → Supervised learning; Transfer\nlearning; Natural language processing; Ensemble methods.\nKeywords\nParameter Efficient Fine Tuning (PEFT), Mixture of Experts (MoE),\nTTLoRA, Scalable Inference, Incremental Learning, Multi-Tasking,\nLLM, NLP\n1 Introduction\nLarge language models (LLMs) have driven remarkable progress\nin natural language processing (NLP), yet their deployment re\u0002mains challenging due to the high computational costs and mem\u0002ory demands of full fine-tuning.       "text": "MiLoRA: Efficient Mixture of Low-Rank Adaptation for Large Language\nModels Fine-tuning\nJingfan Zhang1∗ Yi Zhao2∗ Dan Chen3† Xing Tian4 Huanran Zheng5 Wei Zhu5†\n1\niFLYTEK Co., Ltd, China\n2 University of Pennsylvania, USA, zhaoyi3@seas.upenn.edu\n3 Lenovo Connect Co., Ltd, China\n4 Niuxin Network Technology Co., Ltd, China\n5 East China Normal University, China\nAbstract\nLow-rank adaptation (LoRA) and its mixture\u0002of-experts (MOE) variants are highly effective\nparameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) meth\u0002ods. However, they introduce significant la\u0002tency in multi-tenant settings due to the LoRA\nmodules and MOE routers added to multiple\nlinear modules in the Transformer layer. To\naddress this issue, we propose Mixture of Low\u0002Rank Adaptation (MiLoRA), a novel and ef\u0002ficient LoRA variant. MiLoRA differs from\nprevious MOE-style LoRA methods by con\u0002sidering each LoRA module as an expert and\nemploying a prompt-aware routing mechanism.\nThis mechanism calculates expert routing re\u0002sults once before generating the first new to\u0002ken and reuses these results for subsequent\ntokens, reducing latency. Extensive experi\u0002ments and analysis on commonsense reason\u0002ing tasks, math reasoning tasks, and widely\nused LLM evaluation benchmarks demonstrate\nthat MiLoRA consistently outperforms strong\nPEFT baselines with comparable tunable pa\u0002rameter budgets. Additionally, MiLoRA signif\u0002icantly reduces latency in multi-tenant settings\ncompared to previous LoRA-based methods.\n1 Introduction\nLarge language models (LLMs) have been\nachieving state-of-the-art (SOTA) results not only\nin various natural language processing tasks (Qin\net al., 2023; Zhu et al., 2023; Zhu et al., 2023a,b,\n2021a; Li et al., 2023b; Zhu et al., 2023c; Zhang\net al., 2023a; Zhu et al., 2023e; Guo et al., 2021;\nZhu et al., 2021b; Zheng et al., 2023; Sun et al.,\n2020; Zhang et al., 2023c,d; Wang et al., 2023; Zhu\net al., 2019a; Zhu, 2021c; Zhang et al., 2021; Wang\net al., 2020), but also many challenging evaluation\ntasks (Huang et al., 2023; Li et al., 2023a; Cui et al.,\n2023; Wang et al., 2024; Wenjing Yue and Wang,\n∗Equal contributions.\n†Corresponding author. For any inquiries, please contact:\nmichaelwzhu91@gmail.com;\n2023) but also in numerous challenging evaluation\ntasks (Huang et al., 2023; Li et al., 2023a), such as\nquestion answering, reasoning, math, safety, and in\u0002struction following. Although LLMs are evolving\ninto general task solvers, fine-tuning remains essen\u0002tial for efficient LLM inference and for controlling\nthe style of the generated content (Xin et al., 2024;\nDing et al., 2022). Full-parameter fine-tuning of\nsuch large models is impractical due to the signif\u0002icant GPU memory and computational resources\nrequired. Consequently, parameter-efficient fine\u0002tuning (PEFT) (Zhang et al., 2023e; Zhao et al.,\n2023) has garnered considerable attention in the\nresearch community, as it typically involves tun\u0002ing less than 1% of the LLMs’ parameters, thereby\nsubstantially reducing computational costs.\nAmong many PEF"

      "text": "MIXLORA: Enhancing Large Language Models\nFine-Tuning with LoRA-based Mixture of Experts\nDengchun Li*, Yingzi Ma*, Naizheng Wang*, Zhengmao Ye*, Zhiyuan Cheng1, Yinghao Tang*, Yan\nZhang3, Lei Duan*, Jie Zuo*, Cal Yang2, and Mingjie Tang*\n*Sichuan University, Chengdu, China\n1Purdue University, West Lafayette, USA\n2Emory University, Atlanta, USA\n3Nanyang Technological University, Singapore\nmikecovlee@163.com, {g19myz, pherenice1125, yezhengmaolove}@gmail.com,\ncheng443@purdue.edu„ {yinghaotang2001, yanzhang.jlu}@gmail.com„ {leiduan,\nzuojie}@scu.edu.cn„ j.carlyang@emory.edu, tangrock@gmail.com\nAbstract\nFine-tuning Large Language Models (LLMs) is a common practice to adapt pre\u0002trained models for specific applications. While methods like LoRA have effectively\naddressed GPU memory constraints during fine-tuning, their performance often\nfalls short, especially in multi-task scenarios. However, the\nresource requirements of these MoEs remain challenging, particularly for consumer\u0002grade GPUs with less than 24GB memory. To tackle these challenges, we propose\nMIXLORA, an approach to construct a resource-efficient sparse MoE model based\non LoRA. MIXLORA inserts multiple LoRA-based experts within the feed-forward\nnetwork block of a frozen pre-trained dense model and employs a commonly used\ntop-k router. Unlike other LoRA-based MoE methods, MIXLORA enhances model\nperformance by utilizing independent attention-layer LoRA adapters. Additionally,\nan auxiliary load balance loss is employed to address the imbalance problem of\nthe router. Our evaluations show that MIXLORA improves about 9% accuracy\ncompared to state-of-the-art PEFT methods in multi-task learning scenarios. We\nalso propose a new high-throughput framework to alleviate the computation and\nmemory bottlenecks during the training and inference of MOE models. This\nframework reduces GPU memory consumption by 40% and token computation\nlatency by 30% during both training and inference.\n1 Introduction\nInstruction fine-tuning of Large Language Models (LLMs) [1; 2; 3; 4; 5] for various downstream\ntasks has achieved impressive proficiency in Natural Language Processing (NLP) [6; 7; 8]. As the\nscale of parameters increases, LLMs have been demonstrated to be able to identify complex linguistic\npatterns, thereby enabling the emergence of powerful cross-task generalization capabilities [9]. The\nparadigm of instruction tuning leads to a trade-off between the computational resources required and\nthe performance achieved on downstream tasks, which has been a valuable facet.\nTo substantially reduce the computational and memory resources required by full parameter fine\u0002tuning processes, Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) methods have emerged [10; 11; 12; 13;\n14; 15]. Among these methods, Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) [15], a popular PEFT approach,"

      "text": "Published as a conference paper at ICLR 2026\nLD-MOLE: LEARNABLE DYNAMIC ROUTING FOR\nMIXTURE OF LORA EXPERTS\nYuan Zhuang1∗, Yi Shen2∗, Yuexin Bian3, Qing Su1,\nShihao Ji1, Yuanyuan Shi3, Fei Miao1†\n1University of Connecticut, 2University of Pennsylvania, 3University of California San Diego\n{yuan.2.zhuang, qing.2.su, shihao.ji, fei.miao}@uconn.edu,\neshen6959@gmail.com,\n{yubian, yyshi}@ucsd.edu\nABSTRACT\nRecent studies have shown that combining parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT)\nwith mixture-of-experts (MoE) is an effective strategy for adapting large language\nmodels (LLMs) to the downstream tasks. However, most existing approaches rely\non conventional TopK routing, which requires careful hyperparameter tuning and\nassigns a fixed number of experts to each token. In this work, we propose LD\u0002MoLE, a Learnable Dynamic routing mechanism for Mixture of LoRA Experts\nthat enables adaptive, token-dependent, and layer-wise expert allocation. Our\nmethod replaces the non-differentiable TopK selection with a differentiable rout\u0002ing function and a closed-form solution. Moreover, our design allows the model\nto adaptively determine the number of experts to activate for each token at dif\u0002ferent layers. In addition, we introduce an analytical sparsity control objective to\nregularize the number of activated experts. Extensive experiments on the Qwen3-\n1.7B and Llama-3.2-3B models show that LD-MoLE achieves the highest average\nscores compared to state-of-the-art baselines, across a diverse set of benchmarks.\nOur method not only achieves superior performance, but also demonstrates the\nability to learn token-dependent and layer-wise expert allocation. The code is\navailable at: https://github.com/eshentw/LD-MoLE.\n1 INTRODUCTION\nLarge language models (LLMs) have demonstrated impressive capabilities across a wide range of\nnatural language processing (NLP) tasks. However, their growing size requires significant compu\u0002tational resources for full-parameter fine-tuning.       "text": "Published as a conference paper at ICLR 2025\nHMORA: MAKING LLMS MORE EFFECTIVE WITH HI\u0002ERARCHICAL MIXTURE OF LORA EXPERTS\nMengqi Liao, Wei Chen, Junfeng Shen, Shengnan Guo & Huaiyu Wan∗\nDepartment of Computer Science\nBeijing Jiaotong University\n{mqliao,w chen,jfshen,guoshn,hywan}@bjtu.edu.cn\nABSTRACT\nRecent studies have combined Mixture of Experts (MoE) and Parameter-Efficient\nFine-tuning (PEFT) to fine-tune large language models (LLMs), holding excellent\nperformance in multi-task scenarios while remaining resource-efficient. However,\nexisting MoE approaches still exhibit the following limitations: (1) Current meth\u0002ods fail to consider that different LLM layers capture features at varying levels\nof granularity, leading to suboptimal performance. (2) Task-level routing meth\u0002ods lack generalizability to unseen tasks. (3) The uncertainty introduced by load\nimbalance loss undermines the effective specialization of the experts. To address\nthese challenges, we propose HMoRA, a Hierarchical fine-tuning method that\ncombines MoE and LoRA, employing hybrid routing that integrates token-level\nand task-level routing in a hierarchical manner. This hierarchical hybrid routing\nallows the model to more efficiently capture both fine-grained token information\nand broader task contexts. To improve the certainty of expert selection, a novel\nrouting auxiliary loss is introduced. This auxiliary function also enhances the task\nrouter’s ability to differentiate tasks and its generalization to unseen tasks. Addi\u0002tionally, several optional lightweight designs have been proposed to significantly\nreduce both the number of trainable parameters and computational costs. Ex\u0002perimental results demonstrate that HMoRA outperforms full fine-tuning across\nmultiple NLP benchmarks, while fine-tuning only 3.9% of the parameters. The\ncode is available on: https://github.com/LiaoMengqi/HMoRA.\n1 INTRODUCTION\nLarge language models (LLMs) have made significant strides and achieved impressive capabilities\nin various natural language processing (NLP) (Liu et al., 2023; Touvron et al., 2023; Team et al.,\n2024) tasks, such as machine translation, text generation, and question answering. However, with\nmodels reaching tens or hundreds of billions of parameters, the computational and memory costs\nfor training have increased substantially (Kaplan et al., 2020). Therefore, reducing these costs while\nmaintaining performance has become a critical challenge.\nIn recent years, Mixture of Experts (MoE) (Jacobs et al., 1991; Cai et al., 2024) has attracted growing\ninterest in the research community. The MoE is a neural network architecture that activates a subset\nof expert modules for each input, utilizing a routing mechanism to determine which experts are en\u0002gaged. MoE models enable the expansion of model capacity without significantly increasing compu\u0002tational costs.       "text": "PT-MoE: An Efficient Finetuning Framework for\nIntegrating Mixture-of-Experts into Prompt Tuning\nZongqian Li\nUniversity of Cambridge\nzl510@cam.ac.uk\nYixuan Su\nUniversity of Cambridge\nys484@cam.ac.uk\nNigel Collier\nUniversity of Cambridge\nnhc30@cam.ac.uk\nAbstract\nParameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) methods have shown promise in adapting\nlarge language models, yet existing approaches exhibit counter-intuitive phenom\u0002ena: integrating either matrix decomposition or mixture-of-experts (MoE) individ\u0002ually decreases performance across tasks, though decomposition improves results\non specific domains despite reducing parameters, while MoE increases parameter\ncount without corresponding decrease in training efficiency. Motivated by these\nobservations and the modular nature of PT, we propose PT-MoE, a novel framework\nthat integrates matrix decomposition with MoE routing for efficient PT. Evaluation\nresults across 17 datasets demonstrate that PT-MoE achieves state-of-the-art perfor\u0002mance in both question answering (QA) and mathematical problem solving tasks,\nimproving F1 score by 1.49 points over PT and 2.13 points over LoRA in QA tasks,\nwhile improving mathematical accuracy by 10.75 points over PT and 0.44 points\nover LoRA, all while using 25% fewer parameters than LoRA. Our analysis reveals\nthat while PT methods generally excel in QA tasks and LoRA-based methods in\nmath datasets, the integration of matrix decomposition and MoE in PT-MoE yields\ncomplementary benefits: decomposition enables efficient parameter sharing across\nexperts while MoE provides dynamic adaptation, collectively enabling PT-MoE\nto demonstrate cross-task consistency and generalization abilities. These findings,\nalong with ablation studies on routing mechanisms and architectural components,\nprovide insights for future PEFT methods. 1\n1 Introduction\nBackground. Large language models (LLMs) have shown remarkable capabilities but require\nresources for fine-tuning [41, 18, 19]. PEFT methods address this challenge by updating only a small\nsubset of parameters [8, 21, 22]. Prompt tuning (PT) stands out among PEFT approaches with its\nunique advantages: minimizing trainable parameters through soft prompt optimization, enabling\nmodular deployment through task-specific prompts without model modifications, and supporting\nflexible knowledge composition [14, 20]. These properties make it particularly effective for low\u0002resource and multi-task applications where efficient adaptation is essential [17].\nMotivation.       "text": "ERMoE: Eigen-Reparameterized Mixture-of-Experts for Stable Routing and Interpretable Specialization\n\nTitle:\n\nDescription:\n\n# ERMoE: Eigen-Reparameterized Mixture-of-Experts for Stable Routing and Interpretable Specialization\n\nAnzhe Cheng Shukai Duan11footnotemark: 1 Shixuan Li Chenzhong Yin Mingxi Cheng Heng Ping Tamoghna Chattopadhyay Sophia I. Thomopoulos Shahin Nazarian Paul Thompson Paul Bogdan University of Southern California {anzheche,shukaidu,sli97750,chenzhoy,mingxic}@usc.edu {hping,tchattop,sthomopo,shahin.nazarian,pthomp,pbogdan}@usc.edu Equal contribution.\n\n###### Abstract\n\nMixture-of-Experts (MoE) models expand capacity via sparse expert activation, but routing logits can misalign with expert structure (unstable routing, underutilization) and load imbalance can create stragglers. Auxiliary load-balancing losses reduce disparity but often weaken specialization and downstream accuracy. We propose ERMoE, a sparse MoE transformer that reparameterizes each expert in a learned orthonormal eigenbasis and routes with an Eigenbasis Score (cosine similarity between token features and an expert basis) instead of learned gating logits. By tying assignments to each expert’s representation space, ERMoE stabilizes utilization, improves interpretability, and removes explicit balancing losses and their gradient interference. ERMoE reaches state-of-the-art results on ImageNet and image-text retrieval (COCO, Flickr30K) with flatter expert loads. A 3D MRI variant (ERMoE-ba) improves brain age prediction by over 7% and yields anatomically interpretable expert specializations.\n\n## 1 Introduction\n\nDeep learning has emerged as a cornerstone of modern artificial intelligence, delivering striking advances across core vision problems [4, 25, 18]. A dominant driver of this progress has been a straightforward scaling rule: train larger models on more data to steadily reduce error [19]. Yet the same paradigm carries clear limits: ever-larger dense networks imply escalating compute, memory, and energy budgets that are difficult to reconcile with practical training and deployment constraints [39, 38]. To respond to this tension, Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) [40] architectures were introduced. By activating only a small subset of experts per input, MoE layers decouple parameter count from per-token FLOPs, preserving throughput while expanding capacity. Despite these potential, MoE systems repeatedly encounter structural drawbacks.\n\nThe most significant issue is the misalignment between the router and the experts. With noisy or imbalanced gating, routers may route tokens to suboptimal experts, leading to incorrect predictions and weakening expert specialization [50, 22]. Direct evidence comes from R2-T2 [27], which shows that test-time re-routing, without updating experts, recovers accuracy by moving tokens away from the originally chosen experts toward better ones, implying the initial router frequently selects the wrong expert.       "title": "[2601.12137] EMoE: Eigenbasis-Guided Routing for Mixture-of-Experts",

      "text": "[2601.12137] EMoE: Eigenbasis-Guided Routing for Mixture-of-Experts\n\n# Computer Science > Machine Learning\n\narXiv:2601.12137 (cs)\n\n[Submitted on 17 Jan 2026]\n\n# Title:EMoE: Eigenbasis-Guided Routing for Mixture-of-Experts\n\n[View PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2601.12137) [HTML (experimental)](https://arxiv.org/html/2601.12137v1)\n\n> Abstract:The relentless scaling of deep learning models has led to unsustainable computational demands, positioning Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures as a promising path towards greater efficiency. However, MoE models are plagued by two fundamental challenges: 1) a load imbalance problem known as the``rich get richer\" phenomenon, where a few experts are over-utilized, and 2) an expert homogeneity problem, where experts learn redundant representations, negating their purpose. Current solutions typically employ an auxiliary load-balancing loss that, while mitigating imbalance, often exacerbates homogeneity by enforcing uniform routing at the expense of specialization. To resolve this, we introduce the Eigen-Mixture-of-Experts (EMoE), a novel architecture that leverages a routing mechanism based on a learned orthonormal eigenbasis. EMoE projects input tokens onto this shared eigenbasis and routes them based on their alignment with the principal components of the feature space. This principled, geometric partitioning of data intrinsically promotes both balanced expert utilization and the development of diverse, specialized experts, all without the need for a conflicting auxiliary loss function. Our code is publicly available at [this https URL](https://github.com/Belis0811/EMoE).\n\narXiv-issued DOI via DataCite\n\n| Comments: |\n| --- |\n| Subjects: | Machine Learning (cs.LG); Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition (cs.CV) |\n| Cite as: | [arXiv:2601.12137](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.12137) [cs.LG] |\n| (or [arXiv:2601.12137v1](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.12137v1) [cs.LG] for this version) |\n\n## Submission history\n\nFrom: Anzhe Cheng [[view email](https://arxiv.org/show-email/543cdb9d/2601.12137)] [v1] Sat, 17 Jan 2026 18:49:25 UTC (3,859 KB)\n\nFull-text links:\n\n## Access Paper:\n\nCurrent browse context:\n\ncs.LG\n\n[< prev](https://arxiv.org/prevnext?id=2601.12137&function=prev&context=cs.LG)| [next >](https://arxiv.org/prevnext?id=2601.12137&function=next&context=cs.LG)\n\nChange to browse by:\n\n### References & Citations\n\n- [Google Scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?arxiv_id=2601.12137)\n- [Semantic Scholar](https://api.semanticscholar.org/arXiv:2601.12137)\n\nexport BibTeX citation\n\n### Bookmark\n\nBibliographic Tools\n\n# Bibliographic and Citation Tools\n\nBibliographic Explorer Toggle\n\nBibliographic Explorer ([What is the Explorer?](https://info.arxiv.org/labs/showcase.html#arxiv-bibliographic-explorer))\n\nConnected Papers Toggle\n\nConnected Papers ([What is Connected Papers?](https://www.connectedpapers.com/about))\n\nLitmaps Toggle\n\nLitmaps ([What is Litmaps?](https://www.litmaps.co/))\n\nscite.ai Toggle\n\nscite Smart Citations (",

Standard solutions, such as auxiliary load-balancing losses, can reduce load disparities but often weaken expert specialization and hurt downstream performance. To address these issues, we propose ERMoE, a sparse MoE transformer that reparameterizes each expert in a learned orthonormal eigenbasis and replaces learned gating logits with an \"Eigenbasis Score\", defined as the cosine similarity between input features and an expert's basis. This content-aware routing ties token assignments directly to experts' representation spaces, stabilizing utilization and promoting interpretable specialization without sacrificing sparsity. Crucially, ERMoE removes the need for explicit balancing losses and avoids the interfering gradients they introduce. We show that ERMoE achieves state-of-the-art accuracy on ImageNet classification and cross-modal image-text retrieval benchmarks (e.g., COCO, Flickr30K), while naturally producing flatter expert load distributions. Moreover, a 3D MRI variant (ERMoE-ba) improves brain age prediction accuracy by more than 7\\% and yields anatomically interpretable expert specializations. ERMoE thus introduces a new architectural principle for sparse expert models that directly addresses routing instabilities and enables improved performance with scalable, interpretable specialization.\n\nAuthors (11)\n\n1.       "text": "[2511.10971] ERMoE: Eigen-Reparameterized Mixture-of-Experts for Stable Routing and Interpretable Specialization\n\n# Computer Science > Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition\n\narXiv:2511.10971 (cs)\n\n[Submitted on 14 Nov 2025 ([v1](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.10971v1)), last revised 26 Mar 2026 (this version, v2)]\n\n# Title:ERMoE: Eigen-Reparameterized Mixture-of-Experts for Stable Routing and Interpretable Specialization\n\n[View PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2511.10971) [HTML (experimental)](https://arxiv.org/html/2511.10971v2)\n\n> Abstract:Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures expand model capacity by sparsely activating experts but face two core challenges: misalignment between router logits and each expert's internal structure leads to unstable routing and expert underutilization, and load imbalances create straggler bottlenecks. Standard solutions, such as auxiliary load-balancing losses, can reduce load disparities but often weaken expert specialization and hurt downstream performance. To address these issues, we propose ERMoE, a sparse MoE transformer that reparameterizes each expert in a learned orthonormal eigenbasis and replaces learned gating logits with an \"Eigenbasis Score\", defined as the cosine similarity between input features and an expert's basis. This content-aware routing ties token assignments directly to experts' representation spaces, stabilizing utilization and promoting interpretable specialization without sacrificing sparsity. Crucially, ERMoE removes the need for explicit balancing losses and avoids the interfering gradients they introduce. We show that ERMoE achieves state-of-the-art accuracy on ImageNet classification and cross-modal image-text retrieval benchmarks (e.g., COCO, Flickr30K), while naturally producing flatter expert load distributions. Moreover, a 3D MRI variant (ERMoE-ba) improves brain age prediction accuracy by more than 7\\% and yields anatomically interpretable expert specializations. ERMoE thus introduces a new architectural principle for sparse expert models that directly addresses routing instabilities and enables improved performance with scalable, interpretable specialization.\n\narXiv-issued DOI via DataCite\n\n| Comments: |\n| --- |\n| Subjects: | Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition (cs.CV) |\n| Cite as: | [arXiv:2511.10971](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.10971) [cs.CV] |\n| (or [arXiv:2511.10971v2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.10971v2) [cs.CV] for this version) |\n\n## Submission history\n\nFrom: Anzhe Cheng [[view email](https://arxiv.org/show-email/bbaab269/2511.10971)] [[v1]](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.10971v1) Fri, 14 Nov 2025 05:31:37 UTC (2,564 KB) [v2] Thu, 26 Mar 2026 18:52:49 UTC (2,571 KB)\n\nFull-text links:\n\n## Access Paper:\n\n### Current browse context:\n\ncs.CV\n\n[< prev](https://arxiv.org/prevnext?id=2511.10971&function=prev&context=cs.CV)| [next >](https://arxiv.org/prevnext?id=2511.10971&function=next&context=cs.CV)\n\nChange to browse by:\n\n### References & Citations\n\n- [Google Scholar](https://scholar.g",

      "title": "[2604.00801] Routing-Free Mixture-of-Experts - arXiv",

      "publishedDate": "2026-04-01T00:00:00.000Z",
      "text": "[2604.00801] Routing-Free Mixture-of-Experts\n\n# Computer Science > Machine Learning\n\narXiv:2604.00801 (cs)\n\n[Submitted on 1 Apr 2026]\n\n# Title:Routing-Free Mixture-of-Experts\n\n[View PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2604.00801) [HTML (experimental)](https://arxiv.org/html/2604.00801v1)\n\n> Abstract:Standard Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models rely on centralized routing mechanisms that introduce rigid inductive biases. We propose Routing-Free MoE which eliminates any hard-coded centralized designs including external routers, Softmax, Top-K and load balancing, instead encapsulating all activation functionalities within individual experts and directly optimized through continuous gradient flow, enabling each expert to determine its activation entirely on its own. We introduce a unified adaptive load-balancing framework to simultaneously optimize both expert-balancing and token-balancing objectives through a configurable interpolation, allowing flexible and customizable resource allocation. Extensive experiments show that Routing-Free MoE can consistently outperform baselines with better scalability and robustness. We analyze its behavior in detail and offer insights that may facilitate future MoE design ad optimization.\n\narXiv-issued DOI via DataCite\n\n| Comments: |\n| --- |\n| Subjects: | Machine Learning (cs.LG); Artificial Intelligence (cs.AI); Computation and Language (cs.CL) |\n| Cite as: | [arXiv:2604.00801](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.00801) [cs.LG] |\n| (or [arXiv:2604.00801v1](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.00801v1) [cs.LG] for this version) |\n\n## Submission history\n\nFrom: Yilun Liu [[view email](https://arxiv.org/show-email/83b8c41f/2604.00801)] [v1] Wed, 1 Apr 2026 12:07:20 UTC (1,245 KB)\n\nFull-text links:\n\n## Access Paper:\n\n### Current browse context:\n\ncs.LG\n\n[< prev](https://arxiv.org/prevnext?id=2604.00801&function=prev&context=cs.LG)| [next >](https://arxiv.org/prevnext?id=2604.00801&function=next&context=cs.LG)\n\nChange to browse by:\n\n### References & Citations\n\n- [Google Scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?arxiv_id=2604.00801)\n- [Semantic Scholar](https://api.semanticscholar.org/arXiv:2604.00801)\n\nexport BibTeX citation\n\n### Bookmark\n\nBibliographic Tools\n\n# Bibliographic and Citation Tools\n\nBibliographic Explorer Toggle\n\nBibliographic Explorer ([What is the Explorer?](https://info.arxiv.org/labs/showcase.html#arxiv-bibliographic-explorer))\n\nConnected Papers Toggle\n\nConnected Papers ([What is Connected Papers?](https://www.connectedpapers.com/about))\n\nLitmaps Toggle\n\nLitmaps ([What is Litmaps?](https://www.litmaps.co/))\n\nscite.ai Toggle\n\nscite Smart Citations ([What are Smart Citations?](https://www.scite.ai/))\n\nCode, Data, Media\n\n# Code, Data and Media Associated with this Article\n\nalphaXiv Toggle\n\nalphaXiv ([What is alphaXiv?](https://alphaxiv.org/))\n\nLinks to Code Toggle\n\nCatalyzeX Code Finder for Papers ([What is CatalyzeX?](https://www.catalyzex.com/))\n\nDagsHub Toggle\n\nDagsHub ([What is DagsHub?](https://dagshub.com/))\n\nGotitPub Toggle",

    {
      "id": "https://openreview.net/forum?id=Ksgiup7ZNZ",
      "title": "Union-of-Experts: Experts in Mixture-of-Experts are Secretly Routers",
      "url": "https://openreview.net/forum?id=Ksgiup7ZNZ",
      "publishedDate": "2025-12-03T00:00:00.000Z",
      "text": "Union-of-Experts: Experts in Mixture-of-Experts are Secretly Routers | OpenReview\n\n## Union-of-Experts: Experts in Mixture-of-Experts are Secretly Routers\n\n### Songhao Wu, Ang Lv, Ruobing Xie, Xingwu Sun, Di Wang, Rui Yan, Yankai Lin \n\nICLR 2026 Conference Withdrawn SubmissionEveryone [Revisions](https://openreview.net/revisions?id=Ksgiup7ZNZ) BibTeX [CC BY 4.0](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/)\n\nKeywords: Mixture-of-Experts, Language models\n\nAbstract: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) is a foundational architecture in modern large language models (LLMs). However, a structural limitation has been overlooked: the router is external to the experts, rendering it unaware of their internal capabilities. This gap between routing decisions and expert capabilities limits model performance. In this paper, we demonstrate that the activations of a small subset of “routing neurons” within each routed expert’s own parameters can faithfully capture the match between the expert’s capabilities and input tokens. Collectively, these distributed routing neurons within each routed experts compose an implicit, capabilities-aware “router”, where the norm of the routing neurons’ activations suggests its corresponding expert’s weight. A straightforward implementation of this design requires activating all experts to compute these routing signals, where the unselected experts’ routing neurons are abandoned. To avoid the computational waste from activating unselected experts, we introduce another novel design: we unify the routing neurons of all routed experts to form a virtual shared expert, replacing the standard shared expert in MoE. In this virtual shared expert, activations are not wasted, as they serve not only for routing but also contribute to the final outputs of both the shared expert and partial of routed experts. We name this new MoE variant Union-of-Experts (UoE), drawing an analogy where the routing neuron acts as each expert’s representative, and the virtual shared expert is their union, enabling the experts’ autonomous selection and joint statement. We pre-train language models ranging from 1B to 3B parameters, showing that UoE consistently outperforms strong MoE baselines with comparable efficiency.\n\nSupplementary Material: [ zip](https://openreview.net/attachment?id=Ksgiup7ZNZ&name=supplementary_material)\n\nPrimary Area: foundation or frontier models, including LLMs\n\nSubmission Number: 20138\n\nLoading",

      "favicon": "https://openreview.net/favicon.ico"

We argue that the\nseparation between the router’s decision-making\nand the experts’ execution is a critical yet over\u0002looked issue, leading to suboptimal expert selec\u0002tion and ineffective learning. To address this,\nwe propose Autonomy-of-Experts (AoE), a novel\nMoE paradigm in which experts autonomously\nselect themselves to process inputs. AoE is based\non the insight that an expert is aware of its own\ncapacity to effectively process a token, an aware\u0002ness reflected in the scale of its internal acti\u0002vations. In AoE, routers are removed; instead,\nexperts pre-compute internal activations for in\u0002puts and are ranked based on their activation\nnorms. Only the top-ranking experts proceed\nwith the forward pass, while the others abort.\nThe overhead of pre-computing activations is re\u0002duced through a low-rank weight factorization.\nThis self-evaluating-then-partner-comparing ap\u0002proach ensures improved expert selection and ef\u0002fective learning. We pre-train language models\nhaving 700M up to 4B parameters, demonstrat\u0002ing that AoE outperforms traditional MoE models\nwith comparable efficiency. The code is avail\u0002able at https://github.com/trestad/\nAutonomy-of-Experts\n1Gaoling School of Artificial Intelligence, Renmin University\nof China 2Large Language Model Department, Tencent 3Southeast\nUniversity, China 4University of Macau 5Engineering Research\nCenter of Next-Generation Intelligent Search and Recommenda\u0002tion, MoE 6\nSchool of Artificial Intelligence, Wuhan University.\nCorrespondence to: Ruobing Xie, Rui\nYan.\nProceedings of the 42 nd International Conference on Machine\nLearning, Vancouver, Canada. Comparison between traditional MoE and AoE. Arrows\nindicate data flow, while shadowed modules represent unused pa\u0002rameters or variables. (a) Traditional MoE models use a router\nto assign tokens to specific experts. This separation between the\nrouter‘s decision-making and the experts’ execution leads to sub\u0002optimal expert selection and ineffective learning. (b) In an AoE\nmodel, experts operate autonomously. They are ranked based on\ntheir internal activation norms, and only the top-activated experts\ncontinue processing, while the others are terminated. The AoE\nexpert architecture is modified to maintain efficiency.\n1. Introduction\nLarge language models (LLM) built on Mixture-of-Experts\ntechniques (MoE, Shazeer et al., 2017; Lepikhin et al., 2021;\nFedus et al., 2022) have gained increasing research"

      "id": "https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.14711",
      "title": "[2412.14711] ReMoE: Fully Differentiable Mixture-of-Experts with ReLU Routing",
      "url": "https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.14711",
      "text": "[2412.14711] ReMoE: Fully Differentiable Mixture-of-Experts with ReLU Routing\n\n# Computer Science > Machine Learning\n\narXiv:2412.14711 (cs)\n\n[Submitted on 19 Dec 2024 ([v1](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.14711v1)), last revised 27 Feb 2025 (this version, v2)]\n\n# Title:ReMoE: Fully Differentiable Mixture-of-Experts with ReLU Routing\n\nAuthors: [Ziteng Wang](https://arxiv.org/search/cs?searchtype=author&query=Wang,+Z), [Jun Zhu](https://arxiv.org/search/cs?searchtype=author&query=Zhu,+J), [Jianfei Chen](https://arxiv.org/search/cs?searchtype=author&query=Chen,+J)\n\n[View PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2412.14711) [HTML (experimental)](https://arxiv.org/html/2412.14711v2)\n\n> Abstract:Sparsely activated Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models are widely adopted to scale up model capacity without increasing the computation budget. However, vanilla TopK routers are trained in a discontinuous, non-differentiable way, limiting their performance and scalability. To address this issue, we propose ReMoE, a fully differentiable MoE architecture that offers a simple yet effective drop-in replacement for the conventional TopK+Softmax routing, utilizing ReLU as the router instead. We further propose methods to regulate the router's sparsity while balancing the load among experts. ReMoE's continuous nature enables efficient dynamic allocation of computation across tokens and layers, while also exhibiting domain specialization. Our experiments demonstrate that ReMoE consistently outperforms vanilla TopK-routed MoE across various model sizes, expert counts, and levels of granularity. Furthermore, ReMoE exhibits superior scalability with respect to the number of experts, surpassing traditional MoE architectures. The implementation based on Megatron-LM is available at [this https URL](https://github.com/thu-ml/ReMoE).\n\narXiv-issued DOI via DataCite\n\n| Comments: |\n| --- |\n| Subjects: | Machine Learning (cs.LG) |\n| Cite as: | [arXiv:2412.14711](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.14711) [cs.LG] |\n| (or [arXiv:2412.14711v2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.14711v2) [cs.LG] for this version) |\n\n## Submission history\n\nFrom: Ziteng Wang [[view email](https://arxiv.org/show-email/9e3d0509/2412.14711)] [[v1]](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.14711v1) Thu, 19 Dec 2024 10:21:20 UTC (792 KB) [v2] Thu, 27 Feb 2025 16:33:09 UTC (795 KB)\n\nFull-text links:\n\n## Access Paper:\n\nCurrent browse context:\n\ncs.LG\n\n[< prev](https://arxiv.org/prevnext?id=2412.14711&function=prev&context=cs.LG)| [next >](https://arxiv.org/prevnext?id=2412.14711&function=next&context=cs.LG)\n\nChange to browse by:\n\n### References & Citations\n\n- [Google Scholar](https://scholar.google.com/scholar_lookup?arxiv_id=2412.14711)\n- [Semantic Scholar](https://api.semanticscholar.org/arXiv:2412.14711)\n\nexport BibTeX citation\n\n### Bookmark\n\nBibliographic Tools\n\n# Bibliographic and Citation Tools\n\nBibliographic Explorer Toggle\n\nBibliographic Explorer ([What is the Explorer?](https://info.arxiv.org/labs/showcase.html#arxiv-bibliographic-explorer))\n\nConnected Pa",

{

  "results": [

      "id": "http://paperreading.club/page?id=308704",
      "title": "Mixture of Low Rank Adaptation with Partial Parameter Sharing for Time Series Forecasting",
      "url": "http://paperreading.club/page?id=308704",
      "text": "Mixture of Low Rank Adaptation with Partial Parameter Sharing for Time Series Forecasting\n\n-- '+tag+'')-- --\n\n# Mixture of Low Rank Adaptation with Partial Parameter Sharing for Time Series Forecasting\n\n2025-05-23 13:24:39\n\nLicheng Pan, Zhichao Chen, Haoxuan Li, Guangyi Liu, Zhijian Xu, Zhaoran Liu, Hao Wang, Ying Wei\n\n• --\n\n• -- [arXiv_AI](http://paperreading.club/category?cate=arXiv_AI) [Prediction](http://paperreading.club/category?cate=Prediction) [Pose](http://paperreading.club/category?cate=Pose) [Time_Series](http://paperreading.club/category?cate=Time_Series)\n\n---\n\n- PDF\n- URL\n- Abstract (translated)\n- Abstract\n\n##### Abstract\n\nMulti-task forecasting has become the standard approach for time-series forecasting (TSF). However, we show that it suffers from an Expressiveness Bottleneck, where predictions at different time steps share the same representation, leading to unavoidable errors even with optimal representations. To address this issue, we propose a two-stage framework: first, pre-train a foundation model for one-step-ahead prediction; then, adapt it using step-specific LoRA this http URL design enables the foundation model to handle any number of forecast steps while avoiding the expressiveness bottleneck. We further introduce the Mixture-of-LoRA (MoLA) model, which employs adaptively weighted LoRA experts to achieve partial parameter sharing across steps. This approach enhances both efficiency and forecasting performance by exploiting interdependencies between forecast steps. Experiments show that MoLA significantly improves model expressiveness and outperforms state-of-the-art time-series forecasting methods. Code is available at this https URL.\n\n##### Abstract (translated)\n\n多任务预测已成为时间序列预测（TSF）的标准方法。然而，我们发现它存在表达力瓶颈问题：不同时间步的预测共享相同的表示形式，即使使用最优的表示也无法避免错误的发生。为解决这一问题，我们提出了一种两阶段框架：首先对基础模型进行预训练，使其能够执行一步前瞻预测；然后采用特定步骤的LoRA（低秩适应）对其进行调整。这种设计使基础模型能够在处理任意数量的预测步长的同时避开表达力瓶颈。此外，我们还引入了混合LoRA（MoLA）模型，该模型利用自适应加权的LoRA专家来实现在各时间步之间的部分参数共享，从而通过挖掘预测步骤间的相互依赖性来提高效率和预测性能。实验表明，MoLA显著增强了模型的表现力，并且在时间序列预测方法中超越了现有最佳结果。代码可在提供的链接处获取。\n\n##### URL\n\n[https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17872](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17872)\n\n##### PDF Copy\n\n[https://arxiv.org/pdf/2505.17872.pdf](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2505.17872.pdf)\n\n---\n\nSimilar Posts-- -- -- Machine Learning Automation Toolbox-- -- -- -- -- -- 上一篇 On-- -- -- -- 下一篇 A Spatial-temporal-- -- -- --\n\n-- -- -- -- -- Similar Posts-- Comments-- -- -- bbbb –>-- --\n\nTags\n\n[3D](http://paperreading.club/category?cate=3D) [Action](http://paperreading.club/category?cate=Action) [Action_Localization](http://paperreading.club/category?cate=Action_Localization) [Action_Recognition](http://paperreading.club/category?cate=Action_Recognition) [Activity](http://paperreading.club/category?cate=Activity) [Adversarial](http://paperreading.club/category?cate=Adversarial) [Agent](http://paperreading.club/category?cate=Agent) [Attention](http://paperreading.club/category?cate=Attention) [Autonomous](http://paperreading.club/category?cate=Autonomous)",

However, it is often difficult to apply these large models in downstream tasks, due to massive fine-tuning cost. Recently, Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) has been applied for efficient tuning of diffusion models. Unfortunately, the capabilities of LoRA-tuned diffusion models are limited, since the same LoRA is used for different timesteps of the diffusion process. To tackle this problem, we introduce a general and concise TimeStep Master (TSM) paradigm with two key fine-tuning stages. In the fostering stage (1-stage), we apply different LoRAs to fine-tune the diffusion model at different timestep intervals. This results in different TimeStep LoRA experts that can effectively capture different noise levels. In the assembling stage (2-stage), we design a novel asymmetrical mixture of TimeStep LoRA experts, via core-context collaboration of experts at multi-scale intervals. For each timestep, we leverage TimeStep LoRA expert within the smallest interval as the core expert without gating, and use experts within the bigger intervals as the context experts with time-dependent gating. Consequently, our TSM can effectively model the noise level via the expert in the finest interval, and adaptively integrate contexts from the experts of other scales, boosting the versatility of diffusion models. To show the effectiveness of our TSM paradigm, we conduct extensive experiments on three typical and popular LoRA-related tasks of diffusion models, including domain adaptation, post-pretraining, and model distillation. Our TSM achieves the state-of-the-art results on all these tasks, throughout various model structures (UNet, DiT and MM-DiT) and visual data modalities (Image, Video), showing its remarkable generalization capacity.\n\nShow more\n\n### Lay Summary\n\nDiffusion models create pictures and videos by gently turning noise into clear scenes. Fine-tuning these large models for new jobs usually costs lots of time and computer power. We introduce TimeStep Master, a lightweight add-on that lets the model learn small, specialized tweaks for different moments in the creation process, then smartly combines them. This approach keeps quality high while cutting training effort and works for many kinds of images, videos, and model types. ## RACE VM Experiment Orchestrator Patterns - Summary Report

Based on my analysis of your scripts, here are the key patterns for creating new experiment scripts and orchestrators:

### 1. **Worker Sharding Pattern** (from `run_rescue_baseline_race.sh`)

```bash
# Mode parsing (single worker or distributed)
MODE="${1:-single}"          # Default: "single" (run all)
WORKER_ID="${2:-1}"          # Worker index (1-indexed)
NUM_WORKERS="${3:-1}"        # Total workers

# Worker distribution logic
RUN_IDX=0
for ... do
  if [ "$MODE" = "worker" ] && [ $((RUN_IDX % NUM_WORKERS)) -ne $((WORKER_ID - 1)) ]; then
    SKIPPED_WORKER=$((SKIPPED_WORKER + 1))
    RUN_IDX=$((RUN_IDX + 1))
    continue
  fi
  # Run experiment...
  RUN_IDX=$((RUN_IDX + 1))
done
```

**Key points:**
- Invocation: `bash scripts/run_rescue_baseline_race.sh` (single) OR `bash scripts/run_rescue_baseline_race.sh worker K N` (worker K of N)
- Worker K only executes runs where `(RUN_IDX % N) == (K-1)` (0-indexed internally)
- Typical tmux launch for N=4:
  ```bash
  tmux new-session -d -s w1 'cd ~/neuralips26 && bash scripts/run_rescue_baseline_race.sh worker 1 4 2>&1 | tee results/w1.log'
  ```

### 2. **Existing Result Check & Skip** (double-check pattern from rescue script)

```bash
OUTFILE="${RESULTS_DIR}/${ds}_H96_K5_${freeze}_${seed}_rtr${router}_...json"
OUTFILE_ALT="${RESULTS_DIR}/${ds}_..._lb$(printf %g $lbc)_...json"  # float formatting variant

if [ -f "$OUTFILE" ] || [ -f "$OUTFILE_ALT" ]; then
  SKIPPED_EXISTING=$((SKIPPED_EXISTING + 1))
  RUN_IDX=$((RUN_IDX + 1))
  continue
fi
```

**Key points:**
- Always check for existence before running
- Handle both exact and printf-formatted float variants (bash %g and Python %g differ)
- Track skipped runs for summary reporting

### 3. **Error Handling & Logging**

```bash
set +e
$PYTHON scripts/run_example.py \
  --arg1 value1 \
  --arg2 value2 \
  > /tmp/work_${WORKER_ID}.out 2>&1
RC=$?
set -e

if [ $RC -ne 0 ]; then
  FAILED=$((FAILED + 1))
  echo "  FAILED (rc=$RC, last 5 lines):"
  tail -5 /tmp/work_${WORKER_ID}.out | sed 's/^/    /'
else
  LAUNCHED=$((LAUNCHED + 1))
  # Optional: grep for specific result lines for live monitoring
  grep "pattern" /tmp/work_${WORKER_ID}.out | tail -2 | sed 's/^/    /'
fi
```

### 4. **Standard CLI Interface Arguments**

Common across all experiments (from `run_adamix.py`, `run_rr_moa.py`, `run_raw_mlp_moe.py`):

```python
parser.add_argument("--dataset", default="ETTh1")           # Dataset name
parser.add_argument("--horizon", type=int, default=96)      # Forecast horizon
parser.add_argument("--K", type=int, default=5)             # Number of experts
parser.add_argument("--epochs", type=int, default=15)       # Training epochs
parser.add_argument("--seed", type=int, default=42)         # Random seed
parser.add_argument("--device", default="cuda")             # GPU/CPU device
parser.add_argument("--results-dir", default="results/xxx") # Output directory
parser.add_argument("--unfreeze", choices=["frozen","last2","last4","all"]) # Backbone unfreezing
parser.add_argument("--backbone", default="AutonLab/MOMENT-1-small")  # Model backbone
parser.add_argument("--batch-size", type=int, default=128)  # Batch size
```

**Dataset standards:** `DATASETS=(ETTh1 ETTh2 ETTm1 ETTm2 Weather Electricity)`

### 5. **JSON Result File Structure**

All results saved as JSON with this structure:

```json
{
  "dataset": "ETTh1",
  "horizon": 96,
  "seed": 42,
  "K": 5,
  "top_k": 2,
  "unfreeze": "frozen",
  "router_input_mode": "raw",
  "disable_revin": false,
  "backbone_trainable_params": 0,
  
  "rr_moa": {                          // Main experiment results
    "mse": 0.211,
    "mae": 0.305,
    "param_count": 819830,
    "routing": {                        // Per-expert routing weights
      "mean": 0.163,
      "last": 0.226,
      "max": 0.178,
      "attention": 0.203,
      "conv1d": 0.23
    },
    "routing_entropy": 1.510,           // Shannon entropy of routing dist
    "routing_max_weight": 0.334,        // Max routing weight (collapse indicator)
    "routing_per_sample_std": 0.0802,
    "routing_cross_sample_var": 0.0072,
    "mse_denorm": 10654670.0,           // Denormalized MSE (original units)
    "mae_denorm": 299.63
  },
  
  "elapsed": 28.66,                    // Wallclock time in seconds
  "baselines": {},                     // Optional baseline results
  "winner": "N/A",                     // Which method won
  "delta_pct": 0.0,                    // Percentage improvement
  
  "scaler": {                          // StandardScaler for denormalization
    "scale_": [...],                   // Per-channel scales
    "mean_": [...],                    // Per-channel means
    "n_features_in_": 321,
    "mean_scale_sq": 77023693.73
  }
}
```

### 6. **Filename Naming Convention**

**Simple baseline:** `{DATASET}_H{HORIZON}_K{K}_{TOP_K_LABEL}_{UNFREEZE}_{SEED}.json`
- Example: `ETTh1_H96_K5_top2_frozen_42.json`

**With suffixes (for non-defaults):**
- Router mode: `_router-{mode}` (raw, revin, shuffled, etc.)
- Expert pool: `_pool-{pool}` (canonical, macro, identical-mean, etc.)
- Backbone: `_bb-{name}` (moirai, timer, chronos, etc.)
- Entropy reg: `_entreg-{coef}`
- Router arch: `_rarch-{arch}` (conv, ssr, fft, etc.)
- Temperature: `_temp-{val}`

**AdaMix rescue configs:** `{DATASET}_H{H}_K{K}_{UNFREEZE}_{SEED}_rtr{router}_lb{coef}_lv{variant}_ent{entropy}_z{zloss}_l1{l1}_cf{cf}.json`
- Uses `printf %g` for float formatting to match Python's %g behavior

### 7. **Summary Reporting Pattern**

```bash
echo ""
echo "================================================================"
echo "Experiment DONE at $(date)"
echo "  Launched:          $LAUNCHED"
echo "  Skipped (existed): $SKIPPED_EXISTING"
echo "  Skipped (worker):  $SKIPPED_WORKER"
echo "  Failed:            $FAILED"
echo "  Total scanned:     $RUN_IDX"
echo "================================================================"
```

### 8. **Phase-based Orchestration** (from `run_broader_scope_race.sh`)

For multi-phase experiments:
1. Define each phase clearly in comments
2. Report time estimate per phase
3. Create dedicated result directories per phase
4. Can check partial results between phases for early termination
5. Independent phases can be run in parallel (different result dirs)

---

**Key takeaway:** These scripts follow a **minimal-but-robust** pattern that prioritizes:
- **Reproducibility** via explicit random seeds
- **Resumability** via existence checks with careful filename handling
- **Parallelism** via simple modulo-based worker sharding
- **Observability** via detailed per-run logging and summary statistics
- **Extensibility** via standard JSON output with comprehensive metadata

Files analyzed:
- `/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/scripts/run_rescue_baseline_race.sh` (complex multi-phase, worker-sharded)
- `/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/scripts/run_raw_mlp_moe_race.sh` (simpler single-phase example)
- `/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/scripts/run_adamix.py` (Python experiment runner with argument spec)
- `/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/scripts/run_rr_moa.py` (RR-MoA variant with suffix system)
- `/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/scripts/run_raw_mlp_moe.py` (simplest Python baseline)
- `/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/scripts/run_broader_scope_race.sh` (multi-phase orchestrator example)
- Example result files from `/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/results/rr_moa/`

## Comprehensive Architecture Summary: RR-MoA and AdaMix Routing Implementation

Based on my analysis of the codebase, here is the complete architecture breakdown:

---

## 1. RR-MoA Router Architecture (Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters)

**File**: `/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/scripts/run_rr_moa.py`

**Main Class**: `RawRoutedMoA` (lines 103-350)

### Router Input Modes (`--router-input-mode`)
The router processes one of five input signals:

1. **`raw`** (default, line 222): Uses raw unnormalized time series directly
2. **`revin`** (lines 211-220): Applies per-window RevIN (zero mean, unit variance) normalization
3. **`uniform`** (lines 188-197): Forces constant uniform logits (0 gradient, soft ensemble)
4. **`partial`** (lines 198-205): Linear interpolation between raw and RevIN with alpha parameter
5. **`shuffled`** (lines 206-210): Temporally permuted raw signal (tests temporal ordering importance)

### Router Architecture Options (`--router-arch`)

| Architecture | Code Lines | Input Features | Output Dimension | Purpose |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **`conv`** (default) | 168-174 | Conv1d(1→16, k=32, s=16) + GELU + AdaptiveAvgPool1d | 64 features → K logits | Standard temporal feature extraction |
| **`linear`** | 162-166 | Direct linear projection | Input_len (512) → K logits | V1 ablation: raw signal suffices without conv |
| **`ssr`** | 141-143, 224-228 | Hand-crafted: μ, σ only | 2 scalars → K logits | Sufficient Statistic Router (Prop 2 empirical) |
| **`stats`** | 144-146, 230-243 | Hand-crafted: μ, σ, range, slope, autocorr | 5 scalars → K logits | Five manual statistics features |
| **`multiscale`** | 147-152, 245-250 | Three parallel Conv1d (k=4,16,64) | 24 features (3×8) → K logits | Multi-scale temporal patterns |
| **`fft`** | 153-161, 252-258 | Time branch + FFT frequency branch | 64+32=96 features → K logits | Spectral-Temporal Router (time + frequency) |

### Expert Architecture

**Expert Pool Options** (lines 93-100):

1. **`canonical`** (default): 5 simple pooling heads
   - **MeanPoolHead** (lines 47-52): Mean pool over sequence, Linear(d_model→hidden→output)
   - **LastTokenHead** (lines 54-59): Last token, Linear(d_model→hidden→output)
   - **MaxPoolHead** (lines 61-66): Max pool, Linear(d_model→hidden→output)
   - **AttentionPoolHead** (lines 68-75): Learned attention weights, Linear(d_model→hidden→output)
   - **Conv1dPoolHead** (lines 77-84): Conv1d(d_model, hidden, k=8, s=4) + mean pool, Linear

   All canonical experts have signature: `__init__(d_model, output_dim, hidden=64)` and `forward(hidden_states: (B,T,d))`

2. **`macro`**: 5 AAS-discovered architectures (from `/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/feasibility/rrmoa_macro_experts.py`):
   - **BNMeanLinearExpert** (lines 32-52): BatchNorm1d(d_model) → mean pool → Linear
   - **MultiScaleConvResidualExpert** (lines 55-83): 3 parallel Conv1d (k=3,5,7), averaged, residual BN
   - **Conv1dBNResidualExpert** (lines 86-109): Conv1d + BatchNorm + 1×1 residual (ResNet-style)
   - **DepthwiseSeparableExpert** (lines 112-134): Depthwise separable Conv (MobileNet motif, lowest params)
   - **GatedConvResidualExpert** (lines 137-162): Sigmoid-gated Conv + residual (highway/GLU motif)

3. **`identical-{mean|conv1d|attn}`**: 5 identical experts with different random initialization

### Routing Weight Computation (Forward Pass)

**Lines 268-305**:

```
Input: hidden_states (B, T, d_model), raw_input (B, input_len)
↓
1. _compute_logits(raw_input) → (B, K) logits based on router_arch
2. Apply temperature scaling (line 274-275): logits /= router_temp
3. Expert dropout (lines 278-283): randomly zero expert weights during training
4. Dense mode (top_k >= K, lines 285-289):
   - weights = softmax(logits) → (B, K)
   - expert_outputs = stack([adapters[i](hidden_states) for i in K]) → (B, K, output_dim)
   - result = (weights.unsqueeze(-1) * outputs).sum(dim=1) → (B, output_dim)
5. Sparse Top-K mode (lines 291-305):
   - topk_vals, topk_idx = logits.topk(top_k) → (B, top_k) values and indices
   - weights = softmax(topk_vals) over selected experts only
   - For each sample, only top_k experts execute via masked computation
```

### Auxiliary Loss Terms

| Loss | Lines | Formula | Purpose |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Load Balance Loss** | 313-318 | K * Σ(f_i * P_i) | Switch Transformer load balancing |
| **Entropy Regularization** | 323-329 | -H(p) where H = -(p*log(p)).sum() | Encourages diverse routing |
| **SAIB Loss** | 331-349 | MSE(pred_stats, true_stats) | Forces router latent to encode μ, σ |

---

## 2. AdaMix Router Architecture (Comparison)

**File**: `/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/scripts/run_adamix.py`

**Main Class**: `AdaMix` (lines 109-340)

### Key Differences from RR-MoA

| Aspect | RR-MoA | AdaMix |
|---|---|---|
| **Router Input** | RAW time series (unnormalized) | Mean-pooled hidden states OR raw |
| **Routing Decision** | Per-sample softmax over K experts | Configurable: softmax, ReLU, expert-choice |
| **Expert Pool** | 5 canonical or macro experts | Same 5 canonical experts |
| **Router Architecture** | 6 options (conv, linear, ssr, stats, multiscale, fft) | 2 options: 2-layer MLP or Conv1d (if raw input) |
| **Sparse Routing** | Top-K with sparse execution | No sparse mode |
| **Top-K Selection** | Expert-level selection | Via softmax only |

### AdaMix Router Configs

**Router Input Options** (lines 159-172):
- **`hidden`** (default): 2-layer MLP on mean-pooled hidden states
  - Layer 1: Linear(d_model → router_hidden=32) + GELU
  - Layer 2: Linear(router_hidden → K)
- **`raw`**: Conv1d path identical to RR-MoA default
  - Conv1d(1→16, k=32, s=16) + GELU + AdaptiveAvgPool1d(4) → (B, 64)
  - Linear(64 → K)

### AdaMix Routing Weight Schemes

**_compute_weights()** (lines 188-234) dispatches to:

1. **`softmax`** (default, line 193): F.softmax(logits, dim=-1)
2. **`relu`** (lines 195-209): ReMoE-style ReLU gating
   - raw = ReLU(logits)
   - Renormalize: raw / raw.sum(dim=-1, keepdim=True)
   - Fallback to softmax if all logits ≤ 0
3. **`expert-choice`** (lines 211-232): Zhou et al. 2022 per-expert top-k
   - Each expert selects top k samples (k = B * capacity_factor / K)
   - Weighted by softmax probability * expert selection mask

---

## 3. Feature Extraction Pipeline

**File**: `/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/feasibility/finetune.py`

### _extract_features_batch() (lines 102-138)

```
Input flow:
  Raw input (B, 512) → unsqueeze(1) → (B, 1, 512) for backbone
  ↓
  Backbone forward pass (MOMENT, Chronos, Moirai, or Timer)
  ↓
  Forward hook captures last encoder block output
  ↓
  hidden_states (B, T, d_model) returned to caller
```

For MOMENT backbone specifically (default):
- `model(x_enc=batch_x, input_mask=input_mask)` where batch_x is (B, 1, input_len)
- Hidden dimension: discovered via `_get_hidden_dim()` (line 322-342 in model.py)
  - Checks model.config.d_model or model.d_model
  - Fallback: inspect first Linear layer's in_features
  - For MOMENT-small: d_model = 384

### Adapter Attachment Pattern

1. Load frozen backbone: `model = load_backbone(args.backbone, device)`
2. Get encoder blocks: `blocks = _get_encoder_blocks(model)` (lines 291-319 in model.py)
3. Create RR-MoA/AdaMix module separately (NOT attached as forward hook)
4. Both receive features in training loop:
   - Backbone → _extract_features_batch() → hidden_states (B, T, d_model)
   - RawRoutedMoA(hidden_states, raw_input) → prediction (B, output_dim)

---

## 4. Training Flow: Data → Router → Experts → Output

**train_rr_moa()** (lines 352-476) execution order:

```
1. INITIALIZATION
   adapter = RawRoutedMoA(d_model=384, output_dim=96, K=5, ...)
   Load K experts from pool (canonical or macro)
   Initialize router based on router_arch choice
   Collect trainable params: adapter params + unfrozen backbone blocks

2. TRAINING LOOP (line 390-419)
   for epoch in range(n_epochs=15):
     for batch in train_loader:
       bx_raw = bx.to(device)  # (B, 512) shape
       bx_enc = bx.unsqueeze(1)  # (B, 1, 512) for backbone
       by = by.to(device)  # (B, 96) targets
       
       # Forward backbone
       feat = _extract_features_batch(model, blocks, bx_enc, mask)  # (B, T, 384)
       
       # RR-MoA routing & mixing
       pred = adapter(feat, bx_raw)  # (B, 96)
       
       # Loss computation
       loss = MSE(pred, by)
       loss += 0.01 * load_balance_loss(raw_input)  # Switch loss
       loss += entropy_reg_coef * entropy_regularization(raw_input)
       loss += saib_coef * saib_loss(raw_input)
       
       optimizer.step()

3. EVALUATION (lines 422-468)
   adapter.eval()
   for batch in test_loader:
     feat = _extract_features_batch(...)  # (B, T, 384)
     preds = adapter(feat, bx_raw)  # (B, 96)
     routing = adapter.get_routing_stats(bx_raw)  # (B, K) softmax
     
     Compute: MSE, MAE, routing entropy, per-sample variance
```

## 6. Router Input Signal Flow in Detail

### RR-MoA _compute_logits() (lines 187-266)

```
raw_input (B, input_len=512)
↓
[Based on router_input_mode]
- raw: x = raw_input (line 222)
- revin: x = (raw_input - μ) / σ (lines 218-220)
- uniform: return zeros (line 197) [no routing learned]
- partial: x = (1-α)*raw + α*revin (lines 202-205)
- shuffled: x = raw_input[:, shuffle_perm] (line 210)
↓
[Based on router_arch]
↓
CONV (default):
  x.unsqueeze(1) → (B, 1, 512)
  Conv1d(1→16, k=32, s=16, p=8) → (B, 16, ~33)
  GELU activation
  AdaptiveAvgPool1d(4) → (B, 16, 4)
  flatten(1) → (B, 64)
  Linear(64 → K) → (B, K) logits
↓
SOFTMAX:
  logits /= router_temp (if temp != 1.0)
  weights = softmax(logits) → (B, K) mixing weights
```

### Expert Pooling in Canonical Heads
All canonical heads follow pattern: (B, T, d_model) → (B, output_dim)
```
MeanPoolHead: h.mean(dim=1) → (B, d_model) → Linear(d→hidden) → GELU → Linear(hidden→output)
LastTokenHead: h[:,-1,:] → (B, d_model) → same processing
MaxPoolHead: h.max(dim=1).values → same
AttentionPoolHead: softmax(Linear(h)) weighted sum → (B, d_model) → same
Conv1dPoolHead: h.permute(0,2,1) → (B, d_model, T)
                Conv1d(d→hidden) → GELU → mean(dim=2) → Linear
```

## 7. Key Configuration Parameters

**Command-line Arguments** (from argparse blocks):

```python
# Router configuration
--router-input-mode {raw|revin|uniform|partial|shuffled}
--router-arch {conv|linear|ssr|stats|multiscale|fft}
--router-temp <float>  # softmax temperature
--alpha <float>  # for partial mode: 0=raw, 1=revin
--entropy-reg <float>  # entropy regularization coefficient
--saib-coef <float>  # SAIB auxiliary loss coefficient
--expert-dropout <float>  # probability of zeroing expert weight

# Expert configuration
--expert-pool {canonical|macro|identical-mean|identical-conv1d|identical-attn}
--K <int>  # number of experts (default 5)
--hidden <int>  # expert hidden dimension (default 64)

# Routing strategy
--top-k <int>  # sparse top-k, None means dense (all K)
--freeze-router  # V1 ablation: freeze router at random init
--rdgf  # Router-Detached Gradient Flow

# Backbone
--backbone <str>  # default: AutonLab/MOMENT-1-small
--unfreeze {frozen|last2|last4|all}  # which blocks to train
--disable-revin  # remove RevIN from backbone
```

---

## 8. Design Differences Summary

| Aspect | RR-MoA | AdaMix |
|---|---|---|
| **Diagnosis** | Routes on RAW (avoids RevIN collapse) | Routes on hidden states (collapsed in practice) |
| **Router Input** | Always raw time series | Hidden states (with optional raw ablation) |
| **Router Params** | Conv/multi-scale on raw signal | 2-layer MLP on hidden states |
| **Sparse Routing** | Full Top-K support with efficient sparse execution | No sparse mode |
| **Routing Schemes** | Single softmax-based | Softmax, ReLU (ReMoE), Expert-Choice |
| **Expert Pool** | Canonical + Macro + Identical variants | Canonical only |
| **Auxiliary Losses** | Load balance, entropy, SAIB | Load balance (argmax variant), entropy, z-loss, ReLU-L1 |
| **Trajectory Logging** | Not supported | Full gradient/routing trajectory (T2.A experiments) |

---

This comprehensive breakdown provides all exact class names, line numbers, router architectures, feature extraction flow, and training dynamics needed to design a new routing variant. The key architectural insight is that RR-MoA separates the **deep semantic processing (backbone)** from the **per-sample routing decision (raw signal)**, avoiding the normalization cascade collapse that defeats standard hidden-state routers.
