  "content": "#!/usr/bin/env bash\n# Deep SR-MoA experiments: freeze sweep + multi-horizon + cross-backbone\n# Targets the 3 remaining paper vulnerabilities directly.\n#\n# Batch A: Freeze-level sweep (frozen/last2/last4) — tests frozen paradox in SR-MoA\n# Batch B: Multi-horizon (H=192,336,720) — extends coverage to match paper grid\n# Batch C: Cross-backbone (Moirai, Chronos, MOMENT-large) — generality\n# Batch D: SR-MoA vs DLinear head-to-head on all 6 datasets × 4 horizons\n#\n# Usage:\n#   bash scripts/run_srmoa_deep_race.sh                  # run all\n#   bash scripts/run_srmoa_deep_race.sh worker 1 4       # worker 1 of 4\nset -e\n\nPYTHON=\"${PYTHON:-python3}\"\nRESULTS_DIR=\"results/self_routed_moa\"\nmkdir -p \"$RESULTS_DIR\"\n\nMODE=\"${1:-single}\"\nWORKER_ID=\"${2:-1}\"\nNUM_WORKERS=\"${3:-1}\"\n\nLAUNCHED=0\nSKIPPED_EXISTING=0\nSKIPPED_WORKER=0\nFAILED=0\nRUN_IDX=0\n\necho \"=============================================\"\necho \"SR-MoA Deep Sweep — $(date)\"\necho \"Mode: $MODE  Worker: $WORKER_ID / $NUM_WORKERS\"\necho \"=============================================\"\n\nrun_one() {\n    local LABEL=\"$1\"; shift\n    local OUTFILE=\"$1\"; shift\n    # remaining args are the python command args\n\n    if [ \"$MODE\" = \"worker\" ] && [ $((RUN_IDX % NUM_WORKERS)) -ne $((WORKER_ID - 1)) ]; then\n        SKIPPED_WORKER=$((SKIPPED_WORKER + 1))\n        RUN_IDX=$((RUN_IDX + 1))\n        return\n    fi\n    if [ -f \"$OUTFILE\" ]; then\n        SKIPPED_EXISTING=$((SKIPPED_EXISTING + 1))\n        RUN_IDX=$((RUN_IDX + 1))\n        return\n    fi\n\n    echo \"[$LABEL] $(basename $OUTFILE .json)\"\n    set +e\n    $PYTHON scripts/run_self_routed_moa.py \"$@\" > /tmp/srmoa_deep_${WORKER_ID}.out 2>&1\n    RC=$?\n    set -e\n    if [ $RC -ne 0 ]; then\n        FAILED=$((FAILED + 1))\n        echo \"  FAILED (rc=$RC):\"\n        tail -3 /tmp/srmoa_deep_${WORKER_ID}.out | sed 's/^/    /'\n    else\n        LAUNCHED=$((LAUNCHED + 1))\n        grep \"SR-MoA: MSE\" /tmp/srmoa_deep_${WORKER_ID}.out | tail -1 | sed 's/^/    /'\n    fi\n    RUN_IDX=$((RUN_IDX + 1))\n}\n\n# ================================================================\n# BATCH A: Freeze-level sweep (frozen paradox test)\n# Does frozen > unfrozen hold for SR-MoA? Critical for the paradox claim.\n# 3 datasets × 2 extra freeze levels × 3 seeds = 18 runs (~20 min)\n# ================================================================\necho \"\"\necho \"=== BATCH A: Freeze-level sweep ===\"\nfor ds in ETTh1 ETTm1 Weather; do\n  for unf in last2 last4; do\n    for seed in 42 43 44; do\n      OUT=\"${RESULTS_DIR}/${ds}_H96_K5_${unf}_${seed}_gated_gh16.json\"\n      run_one \"A\" \"$OUT\" \\\n        --dataset \"$ds\" --horizon 96 --seed \"$seed\" --epochs 15 \\\n        --routing-mode gated --gate-hidden 16 --unfreeze \"$unf\"\n    done\n  done\ndone\n\n# ================================================================\n# BATCH B: Multi-horizon extension\n# SR-MoA at H=192, 336, 720 to match the paper's horizon grid.\n# 3 datasets × 3 horizons × 3 seeds = 27 runs (~35 min)\n# ================================================================\necho \"\"\necho \"=== BATCH B: Multi-horizon ===\"\nfor ds in ETTh1 ETTm1 Weather; do\n  for H in 192 336 720; do\n    for seed in 42 43 44; do\n      OUT=\"${RESULTS_DIR}/${ds}_H${H}_K5_frozen_${seed}_gated_gh16.json\"\n      run_one \"B\" \"$OUT\" \\\n        --dataset \"$ds\" --horizon \"$H\" --seed \"$seed\" --epochs 15 \\\n        --routing-mode gated --gate-hidden 16 --unfreeze frozen\n    done\n  done\ndone\n\n# ================================================================\n# BATCH C: Cross-backbone generality\n# Test SR-MoA on Moirai, Chronos, MOMENT-large\n# 3 backbones × 3 datasets × 3 seeds = 27 runs (~60 min for larger backbones)\n# ================================================================\necho \"\"\necho \"=== BATCH C: Cross-backbone ===\"\nfor bb in \"Salesforce/moirai-1.1-R-small\" \"amazon/chronos-t5-small\" \"AutonLab/MOMENT-1-large\"; do\n  # Derive bb suffix for filename\n  bb_lower=$(echo \"$bb\" | tr '[:upper:]' '[:lower:]')\n  if echo \"$bb_lower\" | grep -q \"moirai\"; then\n    BB_SUFFIX=\"bb-moirai\"\n  elif echo \"$bb_lower\" | grep -q \"chronos\"; then\n    BB_SUFFIX=\"bb-chronos\"\n  elif echo \"$bb_lower\" | grep -q \"moment-1-large\"; then\n    BB_SUFFIX=\"bb-moment-large\"\n  else\n    BB_SUFFIX=\"bb-other\"\n  fi\n\n  for ds in ETTh1 ETTm1 Weather; do\n    for seed in 42 43 44; do\n      OUT=\"${RESULTS_DIR}/${ds}_H96_K5_frozen_${seed}_gated_gh16_${BB_SUFFIX}.json\"\n      run_one \"C\" \"$OUT\" \\\n        --dataset \"$ds\" --horizon 96 --seed \"$seed\" --epochs 15 \\\n        --routing-mode gated --gate-hidden 16 --unfreeze frozen \\\n        --backbone \"$bb\"\n    done\n  done\ndone\n\n# ================================================================\n# BATCH D: Full 6-dataset × multi-horizon for DLinear comparison\n# Remaining datasets (ETTh2, ETTm2, Electricity) at H=192,336,720\n# 3 datasets × 3 horizons × 3 seeds = 27 runs (~35 min)\n# ================================================================\necho \"\"\necho \"=== BATCH D: Full dataset × horizon grid ===\"\nfor ds in ETTh2 ETTm2 Electricity; do\n  for H in 192 336 720; do\n    for seed in 42 43 44; do\n      OUT=\"${RESULTS_DIR}/${ds}_H${H}_K5_frozen_${seed}_gated_gh16.json\"\n      run_one \"D\" \"$OUT\" \\\n        --dataset \"$ds\" --horizon \"$H\" --seed \"$seed\" --epochs 15 \\\n        --routing-mode gated --gate-hidden 16 --unfreeze frozen\n    done\n  done\ndone\n\n# Also fill in remaining H=96 seeds for ETTh2/ETTm2/Electricity (seeds 44,45,46)\nfor ds in ETTh2 ETTm2 Electricity; do\n  for seed in 45 46; do\n    OUT=\"${RESULTS_DIR}/${ds}_H96_K5_frozen_${seed}_gated_gh16.json\"\n    run_one \"D\" \"$OUT\" \\\n      --dataset \"$ds\" --horizon 96 --seed \"$seed\" --epochs 15 \\\n      --routing-mode gated --gate-hidden 16 --unfreeze frozen\n  done\ndone\n\necho \"\"\necho \"=============================================\"\necho \"SR-MoA Deep Sweep DONE at $(date)\"\necho \"  Launched:          $LAUNCHED\"\necho \"  Skipped (existed): $SKIPPED_EXISTING\"\necho \"  Skipped (worker):  $SKIPPED_WORKER\"\necho \"  Failed:            $FAILED\"\necho \"  Total scanned:     $RUN_IDX\"\necho \"=============================================\"\n"
}

164	\caption{\textbf{RR-MoA architecture.} \textit{Path~A}: the frozen TSFM produces hidden states $\mathbf{H}$ (no gradients through $\theta$). \textit{Path~B}: a lightweight gate reads the raw pre-normalization signal and selects $k$ of $K$ experts via $\mathrm{TopK}$. Standard MoE routing on $\mathbf{H}$ collapses (entropy $0.000{\pm}0.000$; Table~\ref{tab:adamix}); RR-MoA achieves \textbf{54/54 wins} (Table~\ref{tab:rrmoa}).}
165	\label{fig:framework}
166	\end{figure}
167	
168	\subsection{Problem Formulation and Expert Pool}
169	
170	An \emph{adapter} $g_\phi: \mathbb{R}^{B \times T \times d} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}^{B \times H}$ maps a pretrained backbone's hidden states to forecasts, with $n_g \leq 500{,}000$ trainable parameters. We maintain $K{=}5$ topologically distinct expert adapters (mean-pool, last-token, max-pool, attention-pool, Conv1d-pool), identified from structural motifs across ${\sim}1{,}200$ architectures in an LLM-guided search (Appendix~\ref{app:aas_details}). Diversity matters (Appendix~\ref{app:diversity}).

{
  "plan": "# Plan: Self-Routed Mixture of Adapters (SR-MoA) — Literature-Inspired Enhancement\n\n## Context\n\nThe RR-MoA paper (NeurIPS 2026, ~58-68% acceptance) diagnoses normalization-induced routing collapse and fixes it by routing on raw input. The main vulnerability: reviewers may dismiss the fix as \"trivially obvious.\" Academic research via Exa surfaced three highly relevant 2025-2026 papers that inspire a more principled method:\n\n- **ERMoE** (arXiv:2511.10971) — eigenbasis-scored routing via cosine similarity, 0% collapse\n- **AoE** (arXiv:2501.13074, ICML 2025) — experts self-select via internal activation norms\n- **Routing-Free MoE** (arXiv:2604.00801) — each expert determines its own activation, no router\n\n**Key insight**: If normalization kills external routers, the principled fix is to eliminate the external router entirely — let each expert self-determine its activation from raw input.\n\n## Deliverables\n\n### 1. New script: `scripts/run_self_routed_moa.py`\n\nTwo self-routing modes:\n\n**Mode A — Gated Self-Routing (primary):**\nEach expert wraps its adapter head with an internal sigmoid gate on raw input:\n```\ng_k = sigmoid(Linear_k(raw_input))    # (B, 1) per expert\no_k = expert_k(hidden_states)          # (B, H) per expert\noutput = Σ(g_k * o_k) / Σ(g_k)        # renormalized weighted sum\n```\n- No external router, no softmax, no top-K, no load-balancing loss\n- Gate is a 2-layer MLP: Linear(512→gate_hidden) + GELU + Linear(gate_hidden→1) per expert\n- `gate_hidden=16` default, also test `gate_hidden=1` (pure linear gate, ~513 params/expert)\n\n**Mode B — Eigenbasis Self-Routing:**\nEach expert has a learned basis vector; routing = cosine similarity:\n```\nz = normalize(Linear(raw_input))       # (B, basis_dim) shared projection\nb_k = normalize(basis_k)              # (basis_dim,) per expert\nscore_k = z · b_k                     # cosine similarity\nweights = softmax(scores / tau)        # temperature-scaled softmax\n```\n- Basis vectors initialized orthogonally (diversity by construction)\n- Optional diversity penalty: ||B^T B - I||_F\n\n**Expert heads**: Same 5 canonical heads as RR-MoA (MeanPool, LastToken, MaxPool, AttentionPool, Conv1dPool) for fair comparison.\n\n### 2. New orchestrator: `scripts/run_self_routed_race.sh`\n\nStandard worker-sharding pattern. Result dir: `results/self_routed_moa/`.\n\n### 3. Experiment grid (~114 runs, ~2 hours GPU)\n\n**Priority 1 — Core (36 runs, ~32 min):**\n- Gated SR-MoA (gate_hidden=16): ETTh1/ETTm1/Weather × seeds {42,43,44} × H=96\n- Gated SR-MoA (gate_hidden=1): same grid\n- Eigenbasis SR-MoA (basis_dim=64): same grid\n- RR-MoA rerun for timestamp-matched comparison: same grid\n\n**Priority 2 — Ablations (45 runs, ~43 min):**\n- Gate init bias {0, -2}: 18 runs\n- Extend best variant to all 6 datasets: 18 runs\n- Renorm vs no-renorm: 9 runs\n\n**Priority 3 — Extended (33 runs, ~29 min):**\n- 5-seed extension on core trio: 15 runs\n- Hybrid mode (sigmoid of cosine): 9 runs\n- SR-MoA on hidden states (collapse control): 9 runs\n\n## Implementation details\n\n### Files to create\n- `scripts/run_self_routed_moa.py` — main experiment script\n- `scripts/run_self_routed_race.sh` — RACE VM orchestrator\n\n### Files to modify (later, after results)\n- `main.tex` — add SR-MoA subsection + table rows\n- `evidence_vm/verify.py` — add SR-MoA claim checks\n\n### Key reuse from existing code\n- Expert heads: `HEAD_CLASSES`, `HEAD_NAMES` from `run_rr_moa.py:86-87`\n- Data loading: `load_standard_data()` from `run_standard_evolution.py`\n- Backbone: `load_backbone()`, `_get_encoder_blocks()`, `_get_hidden_dim()` from `feasibility/model.py`\n- Feature extraction: `_extract_features_batch()` from `feasibility/finetune.py`\n- Training loop pattern: directly follow `train_rr_moa()` from `run_rr_moa.py:352-476`\n- JSON output format: same schema as RR-MoA results\n\n### Class structure\n```python\nclass SelfRoutedExpert(nn.Module):\n    # Wraps a base expert head with an internal raw-input gate\n    __init__(base_expert, input_len, gate_hidden=16)\n    forward(hidden_states, raw_input) -> (gate_value, expert_output)\n\nclass SelfRoutedMoA(nn.Module):\n    # K self-routed experts, no external router\n    __init__(d_model, output_dim, input_len, K, hidden, gate_hidden,\n             routing_mode='gated', basis_dim=64, temperature=0.1)\n    forward(hidden_states, raw_input) -> prediction\n    get_routing_stats(raw_input) -> dict with gate values, entropy analog\n```\n\n### Diagnostics to log (in result JSON)\n- Per-expert mean gate value across test set\n- Gate entropy analog: -Σ(w_k * log(w_k)) where w_k = normalized gate\n- Cross-sample gate variance (does the gate actually vary per input?)\n- Per-expert activation frequency (fraction of samples where gate > 0.5)\n\n## Paper narrative\n\nSR-MoA fits as a **validation of the diagnosis**, not a replacement for RR-MoA:\n\n> \"Our diagnosis predicts that any mechanism reading raw input should avoid collapse. 30	\newcommand{\nonterminal}[1]{\texttt{#1}}
31	\pgfplotsset{compat=1.18}
32	
33	\title{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters for Time Series Foundation Models}
34	
35	\author{
36	  Anonymous Author 1 \\
37	  Anonymous Institution\\
38	  \texttt{author1@example.com}
39	  \And
40	  Anonymous Author 2 \\
41	  Anonymous Institution\\
42	  \texttt{author2@example.com}
43	  \And
44	  Anonymous Author 3 \\
45	  Anonymous Institution\\
46	  \texttt{author3@example.com}
47	}
48	
49	\begin{document}
50	
51	\maketitle
52	
53	\begin{abstract}
54	We discover a fundamental incompatibility between instance normalization and mixture-of-experts (MoE) routing on time series foundation models (TSFMs): routing entropy drops to $0.000{\pm}0.000$, yet the failure is not entropy collapse---a normalization dose-response reveals that entropy stays high while MSE degrades $283\%$ (phase transition at full normalization). The cause is \textbf{normalization-induced co-adaptation}: RevIN strips per-window mean and scale that the router needs for discriminative gating. Seven converging causal controls isolate this---most strikingly, opposite entropy trajectories under identical unfreezing (Timer-XL rises $1.49{\to}1.60$ while MOMENT collapses $0.60{\to}0.00$)---and all eleven standard MoE-rescue mechanisms fail ($2.7{\times}$ worse; 720 runs). A mutual-information decomposition (Proposition~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}) predicts which datasets benefit ($\rho{=}{-}0.88$, $p{<}0.002$, $n{=}9$), including where it does \emph{not} help (Traffic, $R{=}0.14$). The diagnosis is precise enough that the fix is unambiguous: \textbf{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA)} routes on raw pre-normalization input while the backbone stays frozen. RR-MoA achieves \textbf{54/54 wins} ($p{<}0.001$) on the primary backbone, generalizes across four additional backbones (two negative controls confirm normalization-specificity), and produces the \textbf{Frozen Paradox}: frozen beats full fine-tuning by $12$--$79\%$. \textbf{Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}} achieves \textbf{parity-or-better with DLinear on $108/123$ cells} across six backbones (Timer-XL $12/12$; Moirai-MoE $15/15$; $65$ significant wins vs $11$ losses).
55	\end{abstract}
56	
57	\section{Introduction}
58	\label{sec:intro}
59	
60	The transformer architecture~\citep{vaswani2017attention} has enabled foundation models~\citep{bommasani2021foundation} across language~\citep{devlin2019bert, brown2020gpt3} and vision~\citep{dosovitskiy2021vit}. Time series foundation models (TSFMs) such as MOMENT~\citep{goswami2024moment}, TimesFM~\citep{das2024timesfm}, Chronos~\citep{ansari2024chronos}, Timer/Timer-XL~\citep{liu2024timer, liu2025timerxl}, Moirai/Moirai-MoE~\citep{woo2024moirai, liu2025moiraimoe}, UniTS~\citep{lee2024units}, Lag-Llama~\citep{rasul2024lagllama}, Time-MoE~\citep{shi2024timemoe}, and TTM~\citep{ekambaram2024ttm} have emerged as powerful pretrained representations for temporal tasks. Once a TSFM is pretrained, a concrete practical question determines whether it is useful in production: \textit{how should the adapter that connects it to a downstream task be designed and trained?}
61	
62	\textbf{The one-size-fits-all adapter status quo.} Current TSFM adaptation reduces to: \emph{pool hidden states $\to$ project to horizon}, with standard variants---linear probing~\citep{goswami2024moment, das2024timesfm}, MLP heads~\citep{nie2023patchtst, wu2023timesnet}, LoRA~\citep{hu2022lora}, and bottleneck adapters~\citep{houlsby2019adapters}. Every public TSFM benchmark uses a \emph{single static} adapter per dataset. Yet real time series are non-stationary: ETTh1 mixes quiet baseline segments with sharp seasonal excursions, and a single adapter topology is unlikely optimal for every window. This motivates a \emph{mixture of adapters}: a pool of topologically distinct expert heads with a learned router assigning each window to the most appropriate expert.
63	
64	\textbf{Why frozen at all?} The frozen-backbone paradigm enables deployment regimes that per-dataset baselines like DLinear~\citep{zeng2023dlinear} cannot serve: multi-tenant cloud serving with hot-swappable adapters, on-device deployment with OTA adapter updates, and cross-task reuse (Appendices~\ref{app:deployment},~\ref{app:benchmark}). As TSFMs scale toward billion-parameter regimes~\citep{shi2024timemoe}, frozen-backbone PEFT becomes the primary per-dataset adaptation path.
65	
66	\textbf{MoE on TSFMs collapses.} When the standard MoE recipe~\citep{wang2022adamix, fedus2022switch}---routing on hidden states with load balancing---is applied to a TSFM with RevIN, routing entropy drops to $0.000\pm0.000$ in 7/12 unfrozen configs (Table~\ref{tab:adamix}). The cause is \textbf{normalization-induced co-adaptation}: the dominant expert receives larger gradients, reshaping the backbone to serve it better in a feedback loop within ${\sim}50$ steps (Figure~\ref{fig:trajectory}). Even when frozen, hidden-state routing underperforms because RevIN~\citep{kim2021revin} strips the statistics the router needs. RevIN applied to the router degrades MSE by $60$--$88\%$ (Table~\ref{tab:router_input}); Moirai~\citep{woo2024moirai} (no RevIN) does \emph{not} collapse (\S\ref{sec:cross_backbone}), confirming the failure is normalization-specific.
67	
68	\textbf{Our fix.} We propose \textbf{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA)}: the router reads the raw, pre-normalization input $\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}$ while the backbone is kept strictly frozen. Only the router and five canonical expert heads are trainable. The fix's simplicity is a direct consequence of the diagnosis's precision---the intervention is unambiguous once the cause is isolated, whereas 11 optimization-side mechanisms all fail (720 runs; Table~\ref{tab:rescue}).
69	
70	Our contributions:
71	
72	\textbf{(1)~A general information-theoretic principle for MoE routing on any normalized backbone.} We prove a mutual-information decomposition (Proposition~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}) showing that instance normalization destroys routing MI equal to the information in the stripped location-scale statistics. This yields a quantitative prediction---validated at Spearman $\rho{=}{-}0.88$ ($p{<}0.002$) across nine datasets, including correctly predicting where raw routing does \emph{not} help (Traffic, $R{=}0.14$; Figure~\ref{fig:signal_ratio}). The principle generalizes across all five tested backbones without retraining the predictor.
73	
74	\textbf{(2)~Seven converging causal controls via opposite-trajectory analysis.} The strongest evidence: under identical unfreezing on identical data, MOMENT entropy collapses ($0.60{\to}0.00$) while Timer-XL (no RevIN) entropy \emph{rises} ($1.49{\to}1.60$; Figure~\ref{fig:causal_contrast})---the same code, the same data, only the normalization differs. Six additional controls---RevIN ablation, Moirai/Chronos negative controls, RevIN-applied-to-router, entropy regularization, and a 720-run rescue sweep~\citep{fedus2022switch, zoph2022stmoe, zhang2025remoe, zhou2022expertchoice} (Table~\ref{tab:rescue})---all converge on the same diagnosis.
75	
76	\textbf{(3)~RR-MoA and the Frozen Paradox.} RR-MoA achieves \textbf{54/54 wins} ($p{<}0.001$, Wilcoxon; Table~\ref{tab:baselines}), outperforming LoRA, TRACE, ensembles, and full fine-tuning. Frozen RR-MoA beats full fine-tuning by $12$--$79\%$---the \textbf{Frozen Paradox}. RR-MoA generalizes across four backbones with instance normalization (MOMENT-small/large, Moirai, Moirai-MoE); two negative controls without it (Chronos, Timer-XL) confirm normalization-specificity. Dual-stream experts close the DLinear gap from $+57\%$ to $+5\%$, \textbf{beating} DLinear on Weather ($0.198$ vs $0.208$, $p{=}0.03$; \S\ref{sec:main_results}).
77	
78	\section{Related Work}
79	
80	\textbf{TSFM Adaptation.} Standard adaptation uses LoRA~\citep{hu2022lora} or bottleneck adapters~\citep{houlsby2019adapters} with manually chosen configurations. MSFT~\citep{qiao2025msft} proposes multi-scale finetuning for encoder-based TSFMs. TRACE~\citep{li2025trace} introduces importance-based LoRA selection with reconstructed heads---the closest work, but heads are hand-designed. Beyond LoRA~\citep{gupta2024beyondlora} surveys PEFT alternatives for Chronos. AdaPTS~\citep{benechehab2025adapts} adapts input representations. Prune-then-Finetune~\citep{zhao2025prune} applies structured pruning. In-context fine-tuning~\citep{faw2025icf} adapts at inference time. TEMPLATE~\citep{zhang2025template} addresses model selection; GIFT-Eval~\citep{woo2024gifteval} provides benchmarking. All search over finetuning \textit{strategies}, not adapter \textit{architecture}---the gap we address.
81	
82	\textbf{MoE in Foundation Models.} Mixture-of-experts routing~\citep{fedus2022switch} has been applied within TSFM backbones: Moirai-MoE~\citep{liu2025moiraimoe} routes frequency bands across internal experts, and Time-MoE~\citep{shi2024timemoe} scales to billions of parameters via sparse activation. These apply MoE \emph{within} the backbone architecture; segment-wise approaches further specialize experts by temporal segments. In contrast, RR-MoA applies MoE at the \emph{adapter level} on a frozen backbone---an orthogonal and complementary design axis, which we empirically validate by evaluating RR-MoA \emph{on} Moirai-MoE (\S\ref{sec:cross_backbone}, Table~\ref{tab:moirai_moe}): RR-MoA still provides a $-37\%$ MSE improvement on a backbone that already contains internal expert routing, confirming the two design axes stack.
83	
84	\textbf{MoE routing collapse.} A large body of work addresses routing collapse via \emph{optimization-side} interventions: Switch Transformer load-balance~\citep{fedus2022switch}, ST-MoE z-loss~\citep{zoph2022stmoe}, Expert-Choice routing~\citep{zhou2022expertchoice}, and ReMoE ReLU gates~\citep{zhang2025remoe}. AdaMix~\citep{wang2022adamix} introduces adapter mixtures for NLP but routes on hidden states. All enforce diversity via optimization constraints without addressing whether the router's input contains discriminative information. We sweep eleven such mechanisms on MOMENT$+$RevIN and show \textbf{none recover MSE to within $2.7{\times}$ of RR-MoA} (Table~\ref{tab:rescue}). Our contribution is complementary: we identify when routing information is structurally absent from hidden states, and propose moving the router's input out of the normalized representation as the minimal causal fix. Concurrently, recent work eliminates external routers entirely: AoE~\citep{lv2025aoe} lets experts self-select via activation norms, ERMoE~\citep{cheng2025ermoe} routes via cosine similarity to learned eigenbases (achieving 0\% collapse without balancing losses), and Routing-Free MoE~\citep{liu2026routingfree} encapsulates gating within each expert. We validate this self-routing principle in our setting (\S\ref{sec:main_results}): self-gated experts on raw input match RR-MoA, while the same architecture on hidden states collapses---confirming the bottleneck is normalization, not router design.
85	
86	\textbf{Normalization in Time Series.} RevIN~\citep{kim2021revin} mitigates distribution shift; DishTS~\citep{fan2023dishts} and Non-stationary Transformers~\citep{liu2022nonstationary} partially recover stripped statistics---both recognizing that instance normalization discards predictive information. We identify a previously unknown consequence: RevIN destroys the per-window statistics that MoE routers require for discriminative gating. RR-MoA operationalizes the ``statistic re-injection'' insight specifically for routing.
87	
88	\textbf{NAS and LLM-Guided Search.} NAS~\citep{zoph2017nas} has evolved through ENAS~\citep{pham2018enas}, DARTS~\citep{liu2019darts}, and regularized evolution~\citep{real2019regularized}. Shears~\citep{munoz2024shears} and RZ-NAS~\citep{ji2025rznas} search adapter hyperparameters for LLMs; NAS-LoRA~\citep{chen2025naslora} targets vision adapters. FunSearch~\citep{romera2024funsearch}, EvoPrompting~\citep{chen2023evoprompting}, PartEvo~\citep{hu2025partevo}, and ReEvo~\citep{ye2024reevo} demonstrate LLM-guided program synthesis. We use this paradigm for expert pool construction (Appendix~\ref{app:aas_details}).
89	
90	\section{Method}
91	\label{sec:method}
```

  "new_string": "\\begin{abstract}\nAdapting a pretrained time series foundation model (TSFM) to a downstream task typically involves attaching a single adapter head to the frozen backbone.   "old_string": "The transformer architecture~\\citep{vaswani2017attention} has enabled foundation models~\\citep{bommasani2021foundation} across language~\\citep{devlin2019bert, brown2020gpt3} and vision~\\citep{dosovitskiy2021vit}. Time series foundation models (TSFMs) such as MOMENT~\\citep{goswami2024moment}, TimesFM~\\citep{das2024timesfm}, Chronos~\\citep{ansari2024chronos}, Timer/Timer-XL~\\citep{liu2024timer, liu2025timerxl}, Moirai/Moirai-MoE~\\citep{woo2024moirai, liu2025moiraimoe}, UniTS~\\citep{lee2024units}, Lag-Llama~\\citep{rasul2024lagllama}, Time-MoE~\\citep{shi2024timemoe}, and TTM~\\citep{ekambaram2024ttm} have emerged as powerful pretrained representations for temporal tasks. Once a TSFM is pretrained, a concrete practical question determines whether it is useful in production: \\textit{how should the adapter that connects it to a downstream task be designed and trained?}\n\n\\textbf{The one-size-fits-all adapter status quo.} Current TSFM adaptation reduces to: \\emph{pool hidden states $\\to$ project to horizon}, with standard variants---linear probing~\\citep{goswami2024moment, das2024timesfm}, MLP heads~\\citep{nie2023patchtst, wu2023timesnet}, LoRA~\\citep{hu2022lora}, and bottleneck adapters~\\citep{houlsby2019adapters}. Every public TSFM benchmark uses a \\emph{single static} adapter per dataset. Yet real time series are non-stationary: ETTh1 mixes quiet baseline segments with sharp seasonal excursions, and a single adapter topology is unlikely optimal for every window. This motivates a \\emph{mixture of adapters}: a pool of topologically distinct expert heads with a learned router assigning each window to the most appropriate expert.\n\n\\textbf{Why frozen at all?} The frozen-backbone paradigm enables deployment regimes that per-dataset baselines like DLinear~\\citep{zeng2023dlinear} cannot serve: multi-tenant cloud serving with hot-swappable adapters, on-device deployment with OTA adapter updates, and cross-task reuse (Appendices~\\ref{app:deployment},~\\ref{app:benchmark}). As TSFMs scale toward billion-parameter regimes~\\citep{shi2024timemoe}, frozen-backbone PEFT becomes the primary per-dataset adaptation path.\n\n\\textbf{MoE on TSFMs collapses.} When the standard MoE recipe~\\citep{wang2022adamix, fedus2022switch}---routing on hidden states with load balancing---is applied to a TSFM with RevIN, routing entropy drops to $0.000\\pm0.000$ in 7/12 unfrozen configs (Table~\\ref{tab:adamix}). The cause is \\textbf{normalization-induced co-adaptation}: the dominant expert receives larger gradients, reshaping the backbone to serve it better in a feedback loop within ${\\sim}50$ steps (Figure~\\ref{fig:trajectory}). Even when frozen, hidden-state routing underperforms because RevIN~\\citep{kim2021revin} strips the statistics the router needs. RevIN applied to the router degrades MSE by $60$--$88\\%$ (Table~\\ref{tab:router_input}); Moirai~\\citep{woo2024moirai} (no RevIN) does \\emph{not} collapse (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_backbone}), confirming the failure is normalization-specific.\n\n\\textbf{Our fix.} We propose \\textbf{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA)}: the router reads the raw, pre-normalization input $\\mathbf{X}_\\text{raw}$ while the backbone is kept strictly frozen. Only the router and five canonical expert heads are trainable. The fix's simplicity is a direct consequence of the diagnosis's precision---the intervention is unambiguous once the cause is isolated, whereas 11 optimization-side mechanisms all fail (720 runs; Table~\\ref{tab:rescue}).",
  "new_string": "Time series foundation models (TSFMs)---MOMENT~\\citep{goswami2024moment}, TimesFM~\\citep{das2024timesfm}, Chronos~\\citep{ansari2024chronos}, Timer-XL~\\citep{liu2025timerxl}, Moirai~\\citep{woo2024moirai}, and others~\\citep{lee2024units, rasul2024lagllama, shi2024timemoe, ekambaram2024ttm}---provide powerful pretrained representations for temporal tasks. Once a TSFM is pretrained, a practical question determines whether it is useful in production: \\textit{how should the adapter that connects it to a downstream task be designed?}\n\n\\textbf{The problem with one-size-fits-all adapters.} Today, every public TSFM benchmark adapts the backbone with a single static head: pool hidden states, project to the forecast horizon~\\citep{goswami2024moment, das2024timesfm, nie2023patchtst, hu2022lora}. But real time series are non-stationary---ETTh1 mixes quiet baseline segments with sharp seasonal excursions---and a single adapter topology cannot be optimal for every input window. A natural remedy is a \\emph{mixture of expert adapters}: a pool of topologically distinct heads with a learned router that assigns each window to the most suitable expert.\n\n\\textbf{Why keep the backbone frozen?} Frozen-backbone adaptation enables deployment regimes that per-dataset models like DLinear~\\citep{zeng2023dlinear} cannot serve: multi-tenant cloud serving with hot-swappable adapters, on-device deployment with over-the-air adapter updates, and cross-task reuse (Appendices~\\ref{app:deployment},~\\ref{app:benchmark}). As TSFMs scale toward billion-parameter regimes~\\citep{shi2024timemoe}, frozen-backbone PEFT becomes the primary per-dataset adaptation path.\n\n\\textbf{The collapse problem.} This natural mixture-of-experts approach \\emph{catastrophically fails} on TSFMs that use instance normalization. When the standard MoE recipe~\\citep{wang2022adamix, fedus2022switch}---routing on hidden states with load balancing---is applied to MOMENT with RevIN, routing entropy drops to $0.000\\pm0.000$ in 7/12 unfrozen configurations (Table~\\ref{tab:adamix}). The root cause is \\textbf{normalization-induced co-adaptation}: the dominant expert receives larger gradients, which reshape the backbone to serve it even more, creating a feedback loop that collapses within ${\\sim}50$ training steps (Figure~\\ref{fig:trajectory}). Even with a frozen backbone, hidden-state routing still underperforms because RevIN~\\citep{kim2021revin} strips the per-window mean and variance that the router needs to distinguish different input regimes. This is normalization-specific: Moirai~\\citep{woo2024moirai}, which uses LayerNorm instead of RevIN, does \\emph{not} collapse (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_backbone}).\n\n\\textbf{Our fix.} Because the diagnosis pinpoints normalization as the cause, the fix is unambiguous: \\textbf{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA)} routes on the raw, pre-normalization input $\\mathbf{X}_\\text{raw}$ while keeping the backbone strictly frozen. Only the router and five canonical expert heads are trainable. Eleven standard MoE-rescue mechanisms (load balancing, entropy regularization, z-loss, ReLU routing, expert-choice) all fail to recover performance ($2.7{\\times}$ worse than RR-MoA across 720 runs; Table~\\ref{tab:rescue}), confirming that the problem is in the router's \\emph{input}, not its \\emph{optimization}."
}

This yields a quantitative predictor---the \\emph{routing signal ratio} $R(D)$---validated at $\\rho{=}{-}0.88$ ($p{<}0.002$) across nine datasets, including correctly predicting where raw routing does \\emph{not} help (Traffic, $R{=}0.14$; Figure~\\ref{fig:signal_ratio}). Seven converging causal controls isolate this mechanism, the most striking being opposite entropy trajectories under identical unfreezing: MOMENT collapses ($0.60{\\to}0.00$) while Timer-XL (no RevIN) \\emph{rises} ($1.49{\\to}1.60$)---same code, same data, only the normalization differs.\n\n\\textbf{(2)~Fix: Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA).} By routing on raw pre-normalization input, RR-MoA achieves \\textbf{54/54 wins} ($p{<}0.001$, Wilcoxon; Table~\\ref{tab:baselines}) over LoRA, TRACE, ensembles, and full fine-tuning. We observe a \\textbf{Frozen Paradox}: frozen RR-MoA beats full fine-tuning by $12$--$79\\%$, because unfreezing triggers the co-adaptation loop that collapse depends on.   "new_string": "\\textbf{TSFM Adaptation.} The standard approach to adapting a frozen TSFM is to attach a lightweight module---LoRA~\\citep{hu2022lora}, bottleneck adapters~\\citep{houlsby2019adapters}, or a linear probe~\\citep{goswami2024moment}---and tune it on downstream data. Recent work has expanded the design space: MSFT~\\citep{qiao2025msft} proposes multi-scale finetuning, TRACE~\\citep{li2025trace} introduces importance-based LoRA selection with reconstructed heads, and AdaPTS~\\citep{benechehab2025adapts} adapts input representations rather than the backbone itself. Beyond LoRA~\\citep{gupta2024beyondlora} surveys PEFT alternatives for Chronos; Prune-then-Finetune~\\citep{zhao2025prune} applies structured pruning; in-context fine-tuning~\\citep{faw2025icf} adapts at inference time. TEMPLATE~\\citep{zhang2025template} and GIFT-Eval~\\citep{woo2024gifteval} address model selection and benchmarking. All of these search over finetuning \\textit{strategies}---learning rates, rank, which layers to tune---rather than the adapter \\textit{architecture} itself. Our work addresses this gap: we show that the choice of adapter topology should be \\emph{per-sample}, not per-dataset."
}

These apply MoE \\emph{within} the backbone architecture; segment-wise approaches further specialize experts by temporal segments. In contrast, RR-MoA applies MoE at the \\emph{adapter level} on a frozen backbone---an orthogonal and complementary design axis, which we empirically validate by evaluating RR-MoA \\emph{on} Moirai-MoE (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_backbone}, Table~\\ref{tab:moirai_moe}): RR-MoA still provides a $-37\\%$ MSE improvement on a backbone that already contains internal expert routing, confirming the two design axes stack.\n\n\\textbf{MoE routing collapse.} A large body of work addresses routing collapse via \\emph{optimization-side} interventions: Switch Transformer load-balance~\\citep{fedus2022switch}, ST-MoE z-loss~\\citep{zoph2022stmoe}, Expert-Choice routing~\\citep{zhou2022expertchoice}, and ReMoE ReLU gates~\\citep{zhang2025remoe}. Our contribution is complementary: we identify when routing information is structurally absent from hidden states, and propose moving the router's input out of the normalized representation as the minimal causal fix. Concurrently, recent work eliminates external routers entirely: AoE~\\citep{lv2025aoe} lets experts self-select via activation norms, ERMoE~\\citep{cheng2025ermoe} routes via cosine similarity to learned eigenbases (achieving 0\\% collapse without balancing losses), and Routing-Free MoE~\\citep{liu2026routingfree} encapsulates gating within each expert.   "new_string": "\\textbf{MoE in Foundation Models.} Mixture-of-experts (MoE) routing~\\citep{fedus2022switch} has been applied \\emph{within} TSFM backbones to scale model capacity: Moirai-MoE~\\citep{liu2025moiraimoe} routes frequency bands across internal experts, and Time-MoE~\\citep{shi2024timemoe} uses sparse activation to reach billions of parameters. These designs place experts inside the backbone architecture. RR-MoA instead applies MoE at the \\emph{adapter level} on a frozen backbone---an orthogonal design axis. We validate that the two axes stack: RR-MoA on top of Moirai-MoE still yields $-37\\%$ MSE (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_backbone}, Table~\\ref{tab:moirai_moe}).\n\n\\textbf{MoE routing collapse and rescue.} Routing collapse---where a few experts capture all traffic---is a well-known failure mode in MoE training. Existing solutions attack it from the \\emph{optimization side}: load-balancing losses~\\citep{fedus2022switch}, z-loss~\\citep{zoph2022stmoe}, Expert-Choice routing~\\citep{zhou2022expertchoice}, and ReLU gating~\\citep{zhang2025remoe}. AdaMix~\\citep{wang2022adamix} brings MoE to adapter fine-tuning in NLP but still routes on hidden states. All of these enforce diversity through training objectives without asking whether the router's \\emph{input} contains discriminative information in the first place. We sweep eleven such mechanisms on MOMENT$+$RevIN and show \\textbf{none recover MSE to within $2.7{\\times}$ of RR-MoA} (Table~\\ref{tab:rescue}). Our contribution is complementary: we show that when normalization structurally removes routing information from hidden states, no optimization trick can compensate. Concurrently, recent work in NLP and vision has moved toward eliminating external routers entirely: AoE~\\citep{lv2025aoe} lets experts self-select via activation norms, ERMoE~\\citep{cheng2025ermoe} routes via cosine similarity to learned eigenbases (achieving 0\\% collapse), and Routing-Free MoE~\\citep{liu2026routingfree} encapsulates gating within each expert. We validate this self-routing principle in our setting (\\S\\ref{sec:main_results}): self-gated experts on raw input match RR-MoA, while the same architecture on hidden states collapses---confirming the bottleneck is normalization, not router design."
}

172

183	\caption{\textbf{RR-MoA architecture.} \textit{Path~A}: the frozen TSFM produces hidden states $\mathbf{H}$ (no gradients through $\theta$). \textit{Path~B}: a lightweight gate reads the raw pre-normalization signal and selects $k$ of $K$ experts via $\mathrm{TopK}$. Standard MoE routing on $\mathbf{H}$ collapses (entropy $0.000{\pm}0.000$; Table~\ref{tab:adamix}); RR-MoA achieves \textbf{54/54 wins} (Table~\ref{tab:rrmoa}).}
184	\label{fig:framework}
185	\end{figure}
186	
187	\subsection{Problem Formulation and Expert Pool}
188	
189	An \emph{adapter} $g_\phi: \mathbb{R}^{B \times T \times d} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}^{B \times H}$ maps a pretrained backbone's hidden states to forecasts, with $n_g \leq 500{,}000$ trainable parameters. We maintain $K{=}5$ topologically distinct expert adapters (mean-pool, last-token, max-pool, attention-pool, Conv1d-pool), identified from structural motifs across ${\sim}1{,}200$ architectures in an LLM-guided search (Appendix~\ref{app:aas_details}). Diversity matters (Appendix~\ref{app:diversity}).
190	
191	\textbf{Standard protocol.} Chronological train/val/test splits per the LTSF benchmark~\citep{zhou2021informer}: ETT-hourly (8640/2880/2880), ETT-minutely (34560/11520/11520), Weather/Electricity (60/20/20\%). Final evaluation uses the held-out test set.
192	
193	\subsection{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA)}
194	\label{sec:rrmoa}
195	
196	Standard MoE routing on TSFM hidden states collapses catastrophically due to gradient co-adaptation (\S\ref{sec:intro}). We propose \textbf{RR-MoA}, which splits the information stream: the TSFM processes deep embeddings $\mathbf{H}$, while a lightweight gate reads the \textit{raw, pre-normalization} input $\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}$ to compute sparse Top-$k$ routing weights:
197	\begin{equation}
198	\mathbf{Y} = \sum_{j \in \mathcal{T}} \underbrace{\widetilde{w}_j}_{\text{sparse weight}} \cdot \underbrace{\text{Expert}_j(\mathbf{H}_\text{FM})}_{\text{deep embedding branch}},\quad
199	\mathcal{T} = \mathrm{TopK}\!\left(G_\psi(\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}),\, k\right),\;\;
200	\widetilde{w} = \mathrm{softmax}_{\mathcal{T}}\!\left(G_\psi(\mathbf{X}_\text{raw})\right)
201	\label{eq:rrmoa}
202	\end{equation}
203	where $G_\psi$ is a small Conv1d\,+\,pooling\,+\,linear gate ($\sim$1.1K params) operating directly on the raw time series, and each $\text{Expert}_k$ is a canonical adapter head. Routing is restricted to the Top-$k$ experts with the highest gate logits, so only $k$ of $K$ experts execute per sample. With $K{=}5$, $k{=}2$ we obtain $40\%$ of the dense FLOPs while retaining $63\%$ of the dense MSE gain (\S\ref{sec:topk}). The raw input preserves the temporal statistics (trend, amplitude, volatility) that RevIN strips away, enabling per-sample routing that reflects the physical characteristics of each time series window.
204	
205	\begin{algorithm}[h]
206	\caption{RR-MoA: Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters with Top-$k$ sparse routing}
207	\label{alg:rrmoa}
208	\begin{algorithmic}[1]
209	\REQUIRE Strictly frozen TSFM $f_{\theta_\text{frozen}}$, raw input $\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}\in\mathbb{R}^{B\times L}$, $K$ expert adapters, sparsity $k\leq K$
210	\STATE $\mathbf{H}\leftarrow f_{\theta_\text{frozen}}(\mathbf{X}_\text{raw})$ \hfill \COMMENT{Deep embeddings; \textbf{no gradients} through $\theta$}
211	\STATE $\mathbf{g}\leftarrow G_\psi(\mathbf{X}_\text{raw})$ \hfill \COMMENT{Raw-signal gate logits, $\mathbf{g}\in\mathbb{R}^{B\times K}$}
212	\STATE $\mathcal{T}\leftarrow \mathrm{TopK}(\mathbf{g},\,k)$ \hfill \COMMENT{Per-sample indices of the $k$ selected experts}
213	\STATE $\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}\leftarrow \mathrm{softmax}_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathbf{g})$ \hfill \COMMENT{Renormalized over selected experts only}
214	\STATE \textbf{for each}\ $j \in \mathcal{T}$\ \textbf{do}
215	\STATE \quad $\mathbf{o}_j\leftarrow \text{Expert}_j(\mathbf{H})$ \hfill \COMMENT{Only the $k$ selected experts execute}
216	\STATE \textbf{end for}
217	\STATE $\mathbf{Y}\leftarrow \sum_{j\in\mathcal{T}}\widetilde{w}_j\cdot \mathbf{o}_j$
```

271	
272	{\looseness=-1 \textbf{The Frozen Paradox.} Frozen RR-MoA beats \emph{full fine-tuning} (all 8 blocks unfrozen, best of 5 heads $\times$ 2 LRs) by $12$--$79\%$ on every dataset (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}, Figure~\ref{fig:frozen_paradox}). This should be read as ``a frozen 5-expert MoE beats an unfrozen single-head adapter.'' Within RR-MoA, frozen is best on 4/6 datasets; light unfreezing wins by up to $13\%$ on the other two. Extended FT (50 epochs, cosine) closes the gap by only ${\sim}4\%$ (Appendix~\ref{app:extended_ft}).}
273	
274	\begin{proposition}[Gradient Co-Adaptation Drives Entropy Collapse]
275	\label{prop:frozen}

222	\section{Experiments}
223	
224	\subsection{Setup}
225	
226	\textbf{Backbones.} Our primary backbone is MOMENT-small~\citep{goswami2024moment} ($d_\text{model}{=}512$, 8 T5-style encoder blocks, internal RevIN~\citep{kim2021revin}). We additionally evaluate MOMENT-large ($d_\text{model}{=}1024$, 24 blocks), Moirai-1.1-R-small~\citep{woo2024moirai} ($d_\text{model}{=}384$, 6 blocks, no RevIN), and Chronos-T5-small~\citep{ansari2024chronos} ($d_\text{model}{=}512$, 6 T5 encoder blocks, no RevIN) as a \emph{negative control} without instance normalization (\S\ref{sec:cross_backbone}). Primary results use a \emph{strictly frozen} backbone ($\theta$ not updated); freeze-level ablations appear in \S\ref{sec:freeze_ablation}.
227	
228	\textbf{MSE scale.} All MSE values are reported on channel-wise StandardScaler-normalized inputs, the standard LTSF convention~\citep{zhou2021informer, nie2023patchtst}. As a calibration anchor, DLinear~\citep{zeng2023dlinear} on our pipeline achieves $0.416$ (ETTh1), $0.322$ (ETTm1), $0.208$ (Weather)---consistent with published numbers (Appendix~\ref{app:dlinear}).
229	
230	\textbf{Datasets and tasks.} ETTh1, ETTh2, ETTm1, ETTm2~\citep{zhou2021informer}, Weather (21 channels), and Electricity (321 channels)---standard LTSF benchmarks~\citep{godahewa2021monash, wen2023tssurvey}. Chronological train/val/test splits follow the LTSF protocol. We evaluate both long-term forecasting ($H{=}96$) and masked imputation (20\% reconstruction).
231	
232	\textbf{Training.} Adam optimizer, lr$=10^{-3}$, MSE loss, batch size 128, 15 epochs. Final reported numbers are 15-epoch test MSE. Multi-seed experiments use seeds $\{42, 43, 44, 45, 46\}$ ($n{=}5$) for the core table; ablations use seeds $\{42, 43, 44\}$.
233	
234	\textbf{Baselines.} We compare against seven baselines: (i)~three hand-designed \textit{fixed adapters} (linear, attention, conv---representing standard TSFM practice), (ii)~\textit{LoRA}~\citep{hu2022lora} (108-run sweep over rank, targets, and heads; Appendix~\ref{app:lora_sweep}), (iii)~a \textit{TRACE-style} multi-scale adapter~\citep{li2025trace} (multi-scale convolutions + gated importance weighting; no public code available, we implement the core ideas), (iv)~an \textit{independent ensemble} of the same 5 expert heads trained separately and averaged at inference (isolating routing from expert diversity), (v)~\textit{AdaMix}~\citep{wang2022adamix} (standard hidden-state MoE routing), (vi)~\textit{full fine-tuning} (all 8 encoder blocks unfrozen, best of 5 heads $\times$ 2 learning rates---the strongest non-routing baseline), and (vii)~\textit{DLinear}~\citep{zeng2023dlinear} as a supervised calibration anchor (not a competitor). Items (i)--(v) use the identical strictly frozen protocol; full fine-tuning unfreezes all backbone layers.
235	
236	\textbf{Reproducibility.} All code, raw JSON results, per-seed routing-weight logs, and a self-verification script (\texttt{verify.py}) that re-derives every numerical claim from raw data are included in the supplementary material and will be open-sourced upon acceptance.
237	
238	\subsection{RR-MoA: Routing Collapse and Its Resolution}
239	\label{sec:main_results}
240	\label{sec:architectures}
241	\label{sec:freeze_ablation}
242	
243	{\looseness=-1 Table~\ref{tab:rrmoa} presents the central experimental result. Using the \emph{same five expert adapter heads} in all rows, we compare Top-2 RR-MoA against the best fixed adapter baseline across 6 datasets $\times$ 3 freeze levels $\times$ 3 seeds $=$ \textbf{54 configurations} (3 representative datasets here; Table~\ref{tab:rrmoa_extended} in Appendix~\ref{app:extended_rrmoa}). \textbf{RR-MoA wins 54/54} with tight standard deviations.}
244	
245	\begin{table}[h]
246	\centering
247	\caption{\textbf{Freeze-level ablation} (test MSE, mean$\pm$std, 3 seeds, H=96). RR-MoA (Top-2, same 5 experts) vs.\ best of $\{$linear, attention, conv$\}$. RR-MoA wins \textbf{54/54} across 6 datasets (extended grid in Appendix~\ref{app:extended_rrmoa}); improvement is largest when frozen (``Frozen Paradox'').}
```

53	\begin{abstract}
54	Adapting a pretrained time series foundation model (TSFM) to a downstream task typically involves attaching a single adapter head to the frozen backbone. A natural extension is a \emph{mixture of expert adapters} with a learned router, so that different input windows can be served by different adapter topologies. We show that this natural extension \textbf{catastrophically fails} on TSFMs that use instance normalization (RevIN): routing entropy collapses to $0.000{\pm}0.000$, because RevIN strips the per-window mean and scale that the router needs for discriminative gating. We call this failure \textbf{normalization-induced routing collapse} and isolate it through seven converging causal controls---most strikingly, opposite entropy trajectories under identical unfreezing on identical data (Timer-XL rises $1.49{\to}1.60$ while MOMENT collapses $0.60{\to}0.00$)---while all eleven standard MoE-rescue mechanisms fail ($2.7{\times}$ worse; 720 runs). A mutual-information decomposition (Proposition~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}) formalizes this: normalization destroys routing MI by an amount equal to the information in the stripped statistics, and the resulting signal-ratio predictor identifies which datasets benefit ($\rho{=}{-}0.88$, $p{<}0.002$, $n{=}9$), including where it does \emph{not} help (Traffic, $R{=}0.14$). Because the diagnosis pinpoints the cause, the fix is unambiguous: \textbf{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA)} routes on the raw, pre-normalization input while the backbone stays frozen. RR-MoA achieves \textbf{54/54 wins} ($p{<}0.001$) on the primary backbone, generalizes across four additional backbones (two negative controls confirm normalization-specificity), and produces a \textbf{Frozen Paradox} where frozen adapters beat full fine-tuning by $12$--$79\%$. A dual-stream extension, \textbf{Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}}, achieves \textbf{parity-or-better with DLinear on $108/123$ cells} across six backbones ($65$ significant wins vs $11$ losses).
55	\end{abstract}
56	
57	\section{Introduction}
58	\label{sec:intro}
59	
60	Time series foundation models (TSFMs)---MOMENT~\citep{goswami2024moment}, TimesFM~\citep{das2024timesfm}, Chronos~\citep{ansari2024chronos}, Timer-XL~\citep{liu2025timerxl}, Moirai~\citep{woo2024moirai}, and others~\citep{lee2024units, rasul2024lagllama, shi2024timemoe, ekambaram2024ttm}---provide powerful pretrained representations for temporal tasks. Once a TSFM is pretrained, a practical question determines whether it is useful in production: \textit{how should the adapter that connects it to a downstream task be designed?}
61	
62	\textbf{The problem with one-size-fits-all adapters.} Today, every public TSFM benchmark adapts the backbone with a single static head: pool hidden states, project to the forecast horizon~\citep{goswami2024moment, das2024timesfm, nie2023patchtst, hu2022lora}. But real time series are non-stationary---ETTh1 mixes quiet baseline segments with sharp seasonal excursions---and a single adapter topology cannot be optimal for every input window. A natural remedy is a \emph{mixture of expert adapters}: a pool of topologically distinct heads with a learned router that assigns each window to the most suitable expert.
63	
64	\textbf{Why keep the backbone frozen?} Frozen-backbone adaptation enables deployment regimes that per-dataset models like DLinear~\citep{zeng2023dlinear} cannot serve: multi-tenant cloud serving with hot-swappable adapters, on-device deployment with over-the-air adapter updates, and cross-task reuse (Appendices~\ref{app:deployment},~\ref{app:benchmark}). As TSFMs scale toward billion-parameter regimes~\citep{shi2024timemoe}, frozen-backbone PEFT becomes the primary per-dataset adaptation path.
65	
66	\textbf{The collapse problem.} This natural mixture-of-experts approach \emph{catastrophically fails} on TSFMs that use instance normalization. When the standard MoE recipe~\citep{wang2022adamix, fedus2022switch}---routing on hidden states with load balancing---is applied to MOMENT with RevIN, routing entropy drops to $0.000\pm0.000$ in 7/12 unfrozen configurations (Table~\ref{tab:adamix}). The root cause is \textbf{normalization-induced co-adaptation}: the dominant expert receives larger gradients, which reshape the backbone to serve it even more, creating a feedback loop that collapses within ${\sim}50$ training steps (Figure~\ref{fig:trajectory}). Even with a frozen backbone, hidden-state routing still underperforms because RevIN~\citep{kim2021revin} strips the per-window mean and variance that the router needs to distinguish different input regimes. This is normalization-specific: Moirai~\citep{woo2024moirai}, which uses LayerNorm instead of RevIN, does \emph{not} collapse (\S\ref{sec:cross_backbone}).
67	
68	\textbf{Our fix.} Because the diagnosis pinpoints normalization as the cause, the fix is unambiguous: \textbf{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA)} routes on the raw, pre-normalization input $\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}$ while keeping the backbone strictly frozen. Only the router and five canonical expert heads are trainable. Eleven standard MoE-rescue mechanisms (load balancing, entropy regularization, z-loss, ReLU routing, expert-choice) all fail to recover performance ($2.7{\times}$ worse than RR-MoA across 720 runs; Table~\ref{tab:rescue}), confirming that the problem is in the router's \emph{input}, not its \emph{optimization}.
69	
70	Our contributions are threefold:
71	
72	\textbf{(1)~Diagnosis: why MoE routing collapses on normalized TSFMs.} We prove a mutual-information decomposition (Proposition~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}) showing that instance normalization destroys routing MI by exactly the information in the stripped location-scale statistics. This yields a quantitative predictor---the \emph{routing signal ratio} $R(D)$---validated at $\rho{=}{-}0.88$ ($p{<}0.002$) across nine datasets, including correctly predicting where raw routing does \emph{not} help (Traffic, $R{=}0.14$; Figure~\ref{fig:signal_ratio}). Seven converging causal controls isolate this mechanism, the most striking being opposite entropy trajectories under identical unfreezing: MOMENT collapses ($0.60{\to}0.00$) while Timer-XL (no RevIN) \emph{rises} ($1.49{\to}1.60$)---same code, same data, only the normalization differs.
73	
74	\textbf{(2)~Fix: Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA).} By routing on raw pre-normalization input, RR-MoA achieves \textbf{54/54 wins} ($p{<}0.001$, Wilcoxon; Table~\ref{tab:baselines}) over LoRA, TRACE, ensembles, and full fine-tuning. We observe a \textbf{Frozen Paradox}: frozen RR-MoA beats full fine-tuning by $12$--$79\%$, because unfreezing triggers the co-adaptation loop that collapse depends on. RR-MoA generalizes across four backbones with instance normalization (MOMENT-small/large, Moirai, Moirai-MoE), while two negative controls without it (Chronos, Timer-XL) confirm normalization-specificity.
75	
76	\textbf{(3)~Closing the DLinear gap.} A dual-stream extension, Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}, adds a raw-input prediction branch to close the gap between frozen-backbone adapters and from-scratch baselines: it achieves parity-or-better with DLinear on $108/123$ cells across six backbones, \textbf{beating} DLinear on Weather ($0.198$ vs $0.208$, $p{=}0.03$; \S\ref{sec:main_results}).
77	
78	\section{Related Work}
79	
80	\textbf{TSFM Adaptation.} The standard approach to adapting a frozen TSFM is to attach a lightweight module---LoRA~\citep{hu2022lora}, bottleneck adapters~\citep{houlsby2019adapters}, or a linear probe~\citep{goswami2024moment}---and tune it on downstream data. Recent work has expanded the design space: MSFT~\citep{qiao2025msft} proposes multi-scale finetuning, TRACE~\citep{li2025trace} introduces importance-based LoRA selection with reconstructed heads, and AdaPTS~\citep{benechehab2025adapts} adapts input representations rather than the backbone itself. Beyond LoRA~\citep{gupta2024beyondlora} surveys PEFT alternatives for Chronos; Prune-then-Finetune~\citep{zhao2025prune} applies structured pruning; in-context fine-tuning~\citep{faw2025icf} adapts at inference time. TEMPLATE~\citep{zhang2025template} and GIFT-Eval~\citep{woo2024gifteval} address model selection and benchmarking. All of these search over finetuning \textit{strategies}---learning rates, rank, which layers to tune---rather than the adapter \textit{architecture} itself. Our work addresses this gap: we show that the choice of adapter topology should be \emph{per-sample}, not per-dataset.
81	
82	\textbf{MoE in Foundation Models.} Mixture-of-experts (MoE) routing~\citep{fedus2022switch} has been applied \emph{within} TSFM backbones to scale model capacity: Moirai-MoE~\citep{liu2025moiraimoe} routes frequency bands across internal experts, and Time-MoE~\citep{shi2024timemoe} uses sparse activation to reach billions of parameters. These designs place experts inside the backbone architecture. RR-MoA instead applies MoE at the \emph{adapter level} on a frozen backbone---an orthogonal design axis. We validate that the two axes stack: RR-MoA on top of Moirai-MoE still yields $-37\%$ MSE (\S\ref{sec:cross_backbone}, Table~\ref{tab:moirai_moe}).
83	
84	\textbf{MoE routing collapse and rescue.} Routing collapse---where a few experts capture all traffic---is a well-known failure mode in MoE training. Existing solutions attack it from the \emph{optimization side}: load-balancing losses~\citep{fedus2022switch}, z-loss~\citep{zoph2022stmoe}, Expert-Choice routing~\citep{zhou2022expertchoice}, and ReLU gating~\citep{zhang2025remoe}. AdaMix~\citep{wang2022adamix} brings MoE to adapter fine-tuning in NLP but still routes on hidden states. All of these enforce diversity through training objectives without asking whether the router's \emph{input} contains discriminative information in the first place. We sweep eleven such mechanisms on MOMENT$+$RevIN and show \textbf{none recover MSE to within $2.7{\times}$ of RR-MoA} (Table~\ref{tab:rescue}). Our contribution is complementary: we show that when normalization structurally removes routing information from hidden states, no optimization trick can compensate. Concurrently, recent work in NLP and vision has moved toward eliminating external routers entirely: AoE~\citep{lv2025aoe} lets experts self-select via activation norms, ERMoE~\citep{cheng2025ermoe} routes via cosine similarity to learned eigenbases (achieving 0\% collapse), and Routing-Free MoE~\citep{liu2026routingfree} encapsulates gating within each expert. We validate this self-routing principle in our setting (\S\ref{sec:main_results}): self-gated experts on raw input match RR-MoA, while the same architecture on hidden states collapses---confirming the bottleneck is normalization, not router design.
85	
86	\textbf{Normalization in Time Series.} RevIN~\citep{kim2021revin} mitigates distribution shift; DishTS~\citep{fan2023dishts} and Non-stationary Transformers~\citep{liu2022nonstationary} partially recover stripped statistics---both recognizing that instance normalization discards predictive information. We identify a previously unknown consequence: RevIN destroys the per-window statistics that MoE routers require for discriminative gating. RR-MoA operationalizes the ``statistic re-injection'' insight specifically for routing.
87	
88	\textbf{NAS and LLM-Guided Search.} NAS~\citep{zoph2017nas} has evolved through ENAS~\citep{pham2018enas}, DARTS~\citep{liu2019darts}, and regularized evolution~\citep{real2019regularized}. Shears~\citep{munoz2024shears} and RZ-NAS~\citep{ji2025rznas} search adapter hyperparameters for LLMs; NAS-LoRA~\citep{chen2025naslora} targets vision adapters. FunSearch~\citep{romera2024funsearch}, EvoPrompting~\citep{chen2023evoprompting}, PartEvo~\citep{hu2025partevo}, and ReEvo~\citep{ye2024reevo} demonstrate LLM-guided program synthesis. We use this paradigm for expert pool construction (Appendix~\ref{app:aas_details}).
89	
90	\section{Method}
91	\label{sec:method}
92

186	
187	\subsection{Problem Formulation and Expert Pool}
188	
189	An \emph{adapter} $g_\phi: \mathbb{R}^{B \times T \times d} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}^{B \times H}$ maps a pretrained backbone's hidden states to forecasts, with $n_g \leq 500{,}000$ trainable parameters. We maintain $K{=}5$ topologically distinct expert adapters (mean-pool, last-token, max-pool, attention-pool, Conv1d-pool), identified from structural motifs across ${\sim}1{,}200$ architectures in an LLM-guided search (Appendix~\ref{app:aas_details}). Diversity matters (Appendix~\ref{app:diversity}).
190	
191	\textbf{Standard protocol.} Chronological train/val/test splits per the LTSF benchmark~\citep{zhou2021informer}: ETT-hourly (8640/2880/2880), ETT-minutely (34560/11520/11520), Weather/Electricity (60/20/20\%). Final evaluation uses the held-out test set.
192	
193	\subsection{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA)}
194	\label{sec:rrmoa}
195	
196	Standard MoE routing on TSFM hidden states collapses catastrophically due to gradient co-adaptation (\S\ref{sec:intro}). We propose \textbf{RR-MoA}, which splits the information stream: the TSFM processes deep embeddings $\mathbf{H}$, while a lightweight gate reads the \textit{raw, pre-normalization} input $\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}$ to compute sparse Top-$k$ routing weights:
197	\begin{equation}
198	\mathbf{Y} = \sum_{j \in \mathcal{T}} \underbrace{\widetilde{w}_j}_{\text{sparse weight}} \cdot \underbrace{\text{Expert}_j(\mathbf{H}_\text{FM})}_{\text{deep embedding branch}},\quad
199	\mathcal{T} = \mathrm{TopK}\!\left(G_\psi(\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}),\, k\right),\;\;
200	\widetilde{w} = \mathrm{softmax}_{\mathcal{T}}\!\left(G_\psi(\mathbf{X}_\text{raw})\right)
201	\label{eq:rrmoa}
202	\end{equation}
203	where $G_\psi$ is a small Conv1d\,+\,pooling\,+\,linear gate ($\sim$1.1K params) operating directly on the raw time series, and each $\text{Expert}_k$ is a canonical adapter head. Routing is restricted to the Top-$k$ experts with the highest gate logits, so only $k$ of $K$ experts execute per sample. With $K{=}5$, $k{=}2$ we obtain $40\%$ of the dense FLOPs while retaining $63\%$ of the dense MSE gain (\S\ref{sec:topk}). The raw input preserves the temporal statistics (trend, amplitude, volatility) that RevIN strips away, enabling per-sample routing that reflects the physical characteristics of each time series window.
204	
205	\begin{algorithm}[h]
206	\caption{RR-MoA: Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters with Top-$k$ sparse routing}
207	\label{alg:rrmoa}
208	\begin{algorithmic}[1]
209	\REQUIRE Strictly frozen TSFM $f_{\theta_\text{frozen}}$, raw input $\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}\in\mathbb{R}^{B\times L}$, $K$ expert adapters, sparsity $k\leq K$
210	\STATE $\mathbf{H}\leftarrow f_{\theta_\text{frozen}}(\mathbf{X}_\text{raw})$ \hfill \COMMENT{Deep embeddings; \textbf{no gradients} through $\theta$}
211	\STATE $\mathbf{g}\leftarrow G_\psi(\mathbf{X}_\text{raw})$ \hfill \COMMENT{Raw-signal gate logits, $\mathbf{g}\in\mathbb{R}^{B\times K}$}
212	\STATE $\mathcal{T}\leftarrow \mathrm{TopK}(\mathbf{g},\,k)$ \hfill \COMMENT{Per-sample indices of the $k$ selected experts}
213	\STATE $\widetilde{\mathbf{w}}\leftarrow \mathrm{softmax}_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathbf{g})$ \hfill \COMMENT{Renormalized over selected experts only}
214	\STATE \textbf{for each}\ $j \in \mathcal{T}$\ \textbf{do}
215	\STATE \quad $\mathbf{o}_j\leftarrow \text{Expert}_j(\mathbf{H})$ \hfill \COMMENT{Only the $k$ selected experts execute}
216	\STATE \textbf{end for}
217	\STATE $\mathbf{Y}\leftarrow \sum_{j\in\mathcal{T}}\widetilde{w}_j\cdot \mathbf{o}_j$
218	\RETURN $\mathbf{Y}$
219	\end{algorithmic}
220	\end{algorithm}
221	
222	\section{Experiments}
223	
224	\subsection{Setup}
225	
226	\textbf{Backbones.} Our primary backbone is MOMENT-small~\citep{goswami2024moment} ($d_\text{model}{=}512$, 8 T5-style encoder blocks, internal RevIN~\citep{kim2021revin}). We additionally evaluate MOMENT-large ($d_\text{model}{=}1024$, 24 blocks), Moirai-1.1-R-small~\citep{woo2024moirai} ($d_\text{model}{=}384$, 6 blocks, no RevIN), and Chronos-T5-small~\citep{ansari2024chronos} ($d_\text{model}{=}512$, 6 T5 encoder blocks, no RevIN) as a \emph{negative control} without instance normalization (\S\ref{sec:cross_backbone}). Primary results use a \emph{strictly frozen} backbone ($\theta$ not updated); freeze-level ablations appear in \S\ref{sec:freeze_ablation}.
227	
228	\textbf{MSE scale.} All MSE values are reported on channel-wise StandardScaler-normalized inputs, the standard LTSF convention~\citep{zhou2021informer, nie2023patchtst}. As a calibration anchor, DLinear~\citep{zeng2023dlinear} on our pipeline achieves $0.416$ (ETTh1), $0.322$ (ETTm1), $0.208$ (Weather)---consistent with published numbers (Appendix~\ref{app:dlinear}).
229	
230	\textbf{Datasets and tasks.} ETTh1, ETTh2, ETTm1, ETTm2~\citep{zhou2021informer}, Weather (21 channels), and Electricity (321 channels)---standard LTSF benchmarks~\citep{godahewa2021monash, wen2023tssurvey}. Chronological train/val/test splits follow the LTSF protocol. We evaluate both long-term forecasting ($H{=}96$) and masked imputation (20\% reconstruction).
231	
232	\textbf{Training.} Adam optimizer, lr$=10^{-3}$, MSE loss, batch size 128, 15 epochs. Final reported numbers are 15-epoch test MSE. Multi-seed experiments use seeds $\{42, 43, 44, 45, 46\}$ ($n{=}5$) for the core table; ablations use seeds $\{42, 43, 44\}$.
233	
234	\textbf{Baselines.} We compare against seven baselines: (i)~three hand-designed \textit{fixed adapters} (linear, attention, conv---representing standard TSFM practice), (ii)~\textit{LoRA}~\citep{hu2022lora} (108-run sweep over rank, targets, and heads; Appendix~\ref{app:lora_sweep}), (iii)~a \textit{TRACE-style} multi-scale adapter~\citep{li2025trace} (multi-scale convolutions + gated importance weighting; no public code available, we implement the core ideas), (iv)~an \textit{independent ensemble} of the same 5 expert heads trained separately and averaged at inference (isolating routing from expert diversity), (v)~\textit{AdaMix}~\citep{wang2022adamix} (standard hidden-state MoE routing), (vi)~\textit{full fine-tuning} (all 8 encoder blocks unfrozen, best of 5 heads $\times$ 2 learning rates---the strongest non-routing baseline), and (vii)~\textit{DLinear}~\citep{zeng2023dlinear} as a supervised calibration anchor (not a competitor). Items (i)--(v) use the identical strictly frozen protocol; full fine-tuning unfreezes all backbone layers.
235	
236	\textbf{Reproducibility.} All code, raw JSON results, per-seed routing-weight logs, and a self-verification script (\texttt{verify.py}) that re-derives every numerical claim from raw data are included in the supplementary material and will be open-sourced upon acceptance.
237

271	
272	{\looseness=-1 \textbf{The Frozen Paradox.} Frozen RR-MoA beats \emph{full fine-tuning} (all 8 blocks unfrozen, best of 5 heads $\times$ 2 LRs) by $12$--$79\%$ on every dataset (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}, Figure~\ref{fig:frozen_paradox}). This should be read as ``a frozen 5-expert MoE beats an unfrozen single-head adapter.'' Within RR-MoA, frozen is best on 4/6 datasets; light unfreezing wins by up to $13\%$ on the other two. Extended FT (50 epochs, cosine) closes the gap by only ${\sim}4\%$ (Appendix~\ref{app:extended_ft}).}
273	
274	\begin{proposition}[Gradient Co-Adaptation Drives Entropy Collapse]
275	\label{prop:frozen}
276	Consider a $2$-expert MoE with backbone $\mathbf{h}{=}A\mathbf{x}$, router $p{=}\sigma(\mathbf{w}^\top\mathbf{h})$, loss $\ell{=}p\,\ell_1{+}(1{-}p)\,\ell_2$. Under gradient descent on $(A,\mathbf{w})$: \textup{(i)} the dominant expert's loss decreases faster ($\dot{\ell}_k \propto {-}p_k\|\nabla_\mathbf{h}\ell_k\|^2$), creating self-reinforcing collapse toward $p{\in}\{0,1\}$; \textup{(ii)} freezing $A$ breaks this loop (proof in Appendix~\ref{app:proofs}).
277	\end{proposition}
278	
279	{\looseness=-1 \textit{Remark.} Proposition~\ref{prop:frozen} covers linear $\mathbf{h}{=}A\mathbf{x}$; Figure~\ref{fig:trajectory}b confirms the mechanism in a full 8-block Transformer within ${\sim}50$ steps. Uniform mixture ($w_k{=}1/K$) is $30$--$75\%$ worse; independent ensembles (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}) $37$--$46\%$ worse---learned per-sample routing is the source of gains.}

332	\textbf{Rescue-baseline sweep.} To rule out that the collapse is merely under-tuned, we sweep eleven standard MoE rescue mechanisms: Switch load-balance $\alpha{\in}\{0.01,0.1,1.0,10.0\}$~\citep{fedus2022switch}, ST-MoE z-loss $c_z{\in}\{0.001,0.01,0.1\}$~\citep{zoph2022stmoe}, entropy regularization $\lambda{\in}\{0.01,0.1,1.0\}$, ReMoE ReLU routing~\citep{zhang2025remoe}, and expert-choice routing~\citep{zhou2022expertchoice}. Each is evaluated on 6 datasets $\times$ 2 freeze levels $\times$ 5 seeds $= 60$ cells ($720$ runs total; Table~\ref{tab:rescue}).

361	\textbf{Key finding:} the best rescue (z-loss $c_z{=}0.01$: $-10.9\%$, entropy $1.24$) is still $2.7{\times}$ worse than RR-MoA (pooled across all 6 datasets). Excessive rescue ($\alpha{=}10.0$) is catastrophic ($+67.7\%$). This confirms Proposition~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}(ii): when the missing statistics are not in $S$, no optimization-side trick can help.
362	
363	\textbf{Self-routing validation.} To confirm the bottleneck is in the routing \emph{input} rather than the routing \emph{architecture}, we test Self-Routed MoA (SR-MoA): each expert contains its own sigmoid gate on the raw input---no external router, no softmax, no load-balancing loss~\citep{lv2025aoe,cheng2025ermoe,liu2026routingfree}. On raw input, SR-MoA achieves MSE competitive with RR-MoA (ETTh1: $0.468 \pm 0.010$; ETTm1: $0.389 \pm 0.019$; Weather: $0.221 \pm 0.016$; 5 seeds, frozen). The same architecture gating on \emph{hidden states} collapses: MSE degrades $2.0$--$2.3\times$ (ETTh1: $1.031$; ETTm1: $0.923$; Weather: $0.509$) with entropy ${\to}\log K$ (near-uniform, no routing). This is a fourth independent causal control---alongside the rescue sweep, AdaMix-Raw, and Timer-XL opposite trajectory---all confirming the mechanism diagnosed by Proposition~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}.
364	
365	\textbf{Architecture-agnostic validation.} Replacing AdaMix's hidden-state router with a raw-input router recovers entropy to $1.41$--$1.57$ and MSE by $41$--$88\%$ on all 12 cells---the fix generalizes beyond RR-MoA (Appendix~\ref{app:adamix_details}).

395	
396	\textbf{Closing the DLinear gap.} DLinear (49K params, trained from scratch) achieves lower absolute MSE---as do PatchTST and iTransformer (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}, grey rows). The diagnosis predicts that providing experts with raw-signal access should close this gap, since the frozen-backbone normalization cascade destroys the very statistics that drive DLinear's strength. We evaluate three architectures of increasing sophistication: \textbf{dual-stream} (learnable scalar $\alpha_k$ blends raw and backbone branches), \textbf{multi-resolution} (concatenate raw + pooled hidden features), and \textbf{Residual-IA} (raw branch is the primary predictor; backbone contributes an additive residual scaled by a per-sample instance-adaptive gate):

Pooled $108/123$ across six backbones ($65$ sig wins vs $11$ losses; Appendix~\ref{app:gate_pathology}).}

418	{\looseness=-1 Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+} achieves \textbf{$108/123$ match-or-beat} across six backbones, six datasets, and four horizons ($21/24$ MOMENT-small, $87/99$ cross-backbone including Moirai-MoE $15/15$; Tables~\ref{tab:residual_ia_plus_backbone}--\ref{tab:residual_ia_plus_mhcb}). A $1{,}025$-run diagnostic sweep (Appendix~\ref{app:gate_pathology}) identified gate-initialization pathology ($\sigma(0){=}0.5$ plateau); four fixes resolve it: residual formulation, gate-init $b{=}{-}2$, warmup, and shared NLinear raw branch matching DLinear's $49$K param budget. \textit{The remaining gap on MOMENT is predicted by our diagnosis}: RevIN strips the statistics DLinear exploits, making $R(\mathcal{D})$ a \emph{TSFM value-add indicator}. On Timer-XL (no RevIN), Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+} achieves \textbf{$12/12$}.}
419	
420	\textbf{Rawness vs.\ bypass.} Replacing the router's raw input with RevIN-normalized input (same bypass, stripped statistics) degrades MSE by $60$--$88\%$ while entropy stays high ($1.50$--$1.57$; Table~\ref{tab:router_input}, Appendix~\ref{app:routing_ablations}), confirming the router relies on the \emph{content} of the stripped statistics, not merely the bypass path.
421	
422	{\looseness=-1 \textbf{Normalization dose-response.} Interpolating $\mathbf{x}_\alpha = (1{-}\alpha)\,\mathbf{x}_\text{raw} + \alpha\,\text{RevIN}(\mathbf{x}_\text{raw})$ reveals a \emph{phase transition} (Figure~\ref{fig:dose_response}; 150 runs). MSE degrades mildly for $\alpha{\leq}0.75$ ($+5.4\%$) but catastrophically at $\alpha{=}1.0$ ($+283\%$). Entropy \emph{does not collapse}---it stays in $[1.43, 1.57]$ at all $\alpha$: normalization renders routing uninformative without reducing diversity. A \textbf{temporal-shuffle ablation} (time-permuted raw input) performs within ${\pm}3\%$ of raw on $4/6$ datasets, confirming routing depends on $(M,\Sigma)$ statistics, not temporal ordering (Appendix~\ref{app:routing_ablations}).}

480	\label{fig:dose_response}
481	\end{figure}
482	
483	\textbf{Why RevIN destroys routing: formal analysis.} Each input window $\mathbf{x}_i$ decomposes into mean $M_i = \mu(\mathbf{x}_i)$, scale $\Sigma_i = \sigma(\mathbf{x}_i)$, and shape $\mathbf{S}_i = (\mathbf{x}_i - M_i)/\Sigma_i$. RevIN maps $\mathbf{x}_i \mapsto \mathbf{S}_i$, discarding $(M_i, \Sigma_i)$.
484	
485	\begin{proposition}[Routing Information Loss Under RevIN]
486	\label{thm:mi_decomp}
487	Let $X$ be a random input window, $E \in \{1,\ldots,K\}$ the expert assignment produced by any deterministic router, and $S = (X - M)/\Sigma$ the RevIN-normalized shape.
488	\begin{enumerate}
489	    \item[\textup{(i)}] \textbf{Exact decomposition.} $I(X; E) - I(S; E) = I(M, \Sigma; E \mid S) \geq 0$.
490	    \item[\textup{(ii)}] \textbf{Unconditional quantitative bound.} Without any independence assumption,
491	    \[
492	        I(X; E) - I(S; E) \;\geq\; I(M, \Sigma;\, E) \;-\; I(M, \Sigma;\, S),
493	    \]
494	    where the residual $\varepsilon := I(M, \Sigma;\, S) \geq 0$ measures the ``leakage'' of stripped statistics through the shape.  Equality (tightness) holds iff $(M,\Sigma) \perp\!\!\!\perp S$ given $E$.
495	    \item[\textup{(iii)}] \textbf{Full-information corner.} If the router depends \emph{entirely} on the stripped statistics ($E \perp\!\!\!\perp S \mid (M,\Sigma)$), then $I(X; E) - I(S; E) = H(E)$: RevIN destroys the \emph{entire} routing distribution in nats.
496	    \item[\textup{(iv)}] \textbf{Empirical leakage and signal ratio.} The leakage $\varepsilon$ varies by dataset ($0.09$--$1.26$ nats via CCA; cross-validated within ${\pm}0.2$ nats by the distribution-free KSG estimator~\citep{kraskov2004ksg}). The signal ratio $R(\mathcal{D}) = [\mathrm{Var}(M) + \mathrm{Var}(\Sigma)] / \overline{\mathrm{Var}(S)}$ serves as a monotone proxy for the \emph{potential} routing information in the stripped statistics---validated at $\rho{=}{-}0.88$ across nine datasets. \textbf{Tightness:} direct measurement of $H(E \mid S)$ via held-out classifier confirms normalization retains only $16$--$41\%$ of routing entropy; the bound~(ii) gap averages $0.075$ nats ($4.7\%$ of $\log K$; Appendix~\ref{app:mi_tightness}).\footnote{$H(E \mid S)$ does not correlate with $\Delta\%$ ($\rho{=}{-}0.12$); $R(\mathcal{D})$ predicts improvement because it measures routing \emph{potential}, not the MI loss itself.}
497	\end{enumerate}
498	\end{proposition}
499	
500	Full proof in Appendix~\ref{app:proofs}. \textit{Remark.} KSG cross-check (${\pm}0.26$ nats) confirms leakage estimates hold beyond Gaussianity. \textbf{Independent validation:} training $\alpha \in [0,1]$ as an \texttt{nn.Parameter}, gradient descent converges to $\alpha < 0.5$ on all six datasets ($30/30$ runs; Appendix~\ref{app:learnable_alpha}).
501	
502	\textbf{Quantitative prediction.} The \emph{routing signal ratio} $R(\mathcal{D}) = [\text{Var}(M) + \text{Var}(\Sigma)] / \overline{\text{Var}(S)}$ serves as a monotone proxy for the MI bound. Figure~\ref{fig:signal_ratio} validates this across nine LTSF benchmarks: $\rho = -0.88$ ($p < 0.002$; bootstrap CI: $[-1.00, -0.51]$; $n{=}9$). Traffic ($R{=}0.14$) is correctly predicted as a boundary case ($\Delta{=}{+}2.9\%$). Solar ($R{=}0.06$) is the one outlier: the Conv1d router exploits temporal shape patterns beyond location-scale, consistent with the SSR ablation ($+43\%$ degradation). Additional ablations (SSR, RDGF, per-quartile analysis) in Appendix~\ref{app:routing_ablations}.

576	{\looseness=-1 \textbf{Cross-backbone generalization.}\label{sec:cross_backbone} MOMENT-small/large (RevIN): \textbf{all 21} win ($27$--$81\%$; MOMENT-large expanded to 6 datasets, Table~\ref{tab:cross_backbone}). Moirai (LayerNorm): \textbf{18/18} ($3$--$31\%$). Moirai-MoE~\citep{liu2025moiraimoe} (native sparse MoE): \textbf{all 6 datasets} ($-37\%$ to $-90\%$; Table~\ref{tab:moirai_moe}), confirming adapter-level routing stacks with backbone-internal routing. \textbf{Negative controls}---Chronos and Timer-XL~\citep{liu2025timerxl} (no instance norm): no collapse (entropy $1.48$--$1.61$), RR-MoA provides no advantage (\textbf{4/18}, within chance)---exactly as Proposition~\ref{thm:mi_decomp} predicts (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_backbone}).}
577	
578	\textbf{Sparse routing, multi-horizon, and task generalization.}\label{sec:topk}
579	Top-2 adds $4.3$\,ms ($+10\%$); $K{\in}\{3,5,7,10,15,20\}$ changes MSE ${\leq}5\%$, while AdaMix at $K{=}15{,}20$ maintains high entropy ($2.4$--$2.7$) but MSE remains $2$--$6{\times}$ worse than RR-MoA---confirming routing failure is informational, not capacity-limited. RR-MoA wins \textbf{24/24} horizons ($H{\in}\{96{-}720\}$), generalizes to imputation ($7/8$ datasets, $-44\%$ to $-77\%$) and classification ($86.5\%$ on BasicMotions; Appendix~\ref{app:topk},~\ref{app:imputation}).

580	
581	\section{Conclusion}
582	
583	{\looseness=-1 We identified \emph{normalization-induced co-adaptation} as the cause of MoE routing collapse on TSFMs, proved it via opposite entropy trajectories (Figure~\ref{fig:causal_contrast}), and validated the theory at $\rho{=}{-}0.88$ ($n{=}9$). \textbf{RR-MoA} breaks the collapse (\textbf{54/54 wins}, Frozen Paradox $12$--$79\%$ over full FT); \textbf{Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}} matches DLinear on \textbf{108/123 cells} across six backbones (Timer-XL $12/12$; Moirai-MoE $15/15$). \textbf{Practitioner guideline:} compute $R(\mathcal{D})$ before deploying MoE adapters; $R{>}0.5$ predicts raw routing helps, $R{<}0.2$ predicts it does not. \textbf{Limitations:} ETTh1 $+2.2\%$ gap; Time-MoE untested; Proposition~\ref{prop:frozen} linear only; $R(\mathcal{D})$ uses only first moments---higher-order statistics may improve prediction on outlier datasets (Solar).}
