      "publishedDate": "2023-03-15T22:23:38.000Z",
      "author": "edward cornfoot",
      "text": "# Where can I create nice looking graphics for a paper?\n\n- Tags: reference-request, online-resources\n- Score: 29\n- Views: 6,746\n- Answers: 7\n- Asked by: edward cornfoot (615 rep)\n- Asked on: Mar 15, 2023\n- Last active: Feb 1, 2025\n- License: CC BY-SA 4.0\n\n---\n\n## Question\n\nI’m currently writing my first paper, a little paper, and I’d like to have some nice graphics in it. Much of the proof work is tedious analysis and I’d like to give any potential reader visual references to help.\n\nTo give an idea of what I’m looking for in particular, I’d like to have some pictures of the plane with square lattice points, perhaps some of the points labeled with their coordinates. I’d also like to be able to create images of complex integrals (Argand diagram with labeled coordinate axes, little arrow along the path to orient the path).\n\nAnyone have experience generating these graphics who can direct me to online resources/libraries/software (preferably free)?\n\nEDIT: Thank you very much everyone for all the effort into your responses. There are multiple great answers here. Exploring as many as I can will be my goal!\n\n---\n\n## Answer 1 — Score: 29\n\n- By: A. J. Pan-Collantes (1,075 rep)\n- Answered on: Mar 18, 2023\n\nYou can use tikz, or indeed any programming language, together with ChatGPT. I have no previous experience on tikz, but I have used it to generate the following pictures: [![enter image description here](https://i.sstatic.net/JBN8J.png)](https://i.sstatic.net/JBN8J.png)\n\n[![enter image description here](https://i.sstatic.net/qJGxc.png)](https://i.sstatic.net/qJGxc.png)\n\n[![enter image description here](https://i.sstatic.net/oMMHm.png)](https://i.sstatic.net/oMMHm.png)\n\nby using prompts like: \"I want a picture in tikz of a map from an open subsets of $\\\\mathbb R^2$ $U$ and $V$\", or \"I want the graph of the sin function with the area between 0 and $\\\\pi$ filled,... Then you can modify the result with a bit of intuition.\n\n**Added**:\n\nMaybe you don't obtain results after a first try. For example, I asked for \"tikz picture of the area between two graphs\" and the resulting tex code gave me errors. Surprisingly, I copied the error message to chatgpt, I wrote: \"I obtain this error \"! Package PGF Math Error: Unknown function \\`x' (in '0.5\\*x^2').\"\" Then, ChatGPT returned a correct code: [![enter image description here](https://i.sstatic.net/oCGBN.png)](https://i.sstatic.net/oCGBN.png)\n\n**Added**: I have just discovered that DeepSeek is even better than ChatGPT for coding tikz.\n\n---\n\n## Accepted Answer — Score: 25\n\n- By: user347489 (1,090 rep)\n- Answered on: Mar 15, 2023\n\nIpe is a fantastic tool. There's also a bit of a learning curve, but it's not too bad. I've seen people who are proficient at it generate amazing figures in a matter of minutes.\n\n[https://ipe.otfried.org/](https://ipe.otfried.org/)\n\nEdit: I wish I had known about it a few years ago, it would've saved me many hours of TikZ coding.\n\n---\n\n## Answer 3 — Score: 16\n\n- By: Marco Ripà (2,325 rep)\n- Answer"

Clear, well-designed visuals help researchers explain complex concepts, improve comprehension, and present findings more effectively in journals, posters, and conferences. [Studies](https://arxiv.org/abs/1605.04951) show that academic papers incorporating more diagrams and visual content typically achieve higher impact than those relying on text alone. For researchers, PhD students, and broader academic professionals, choosing the right scientific illustration tool can make the difference between a figure that merely looks good and one that simplifies complex data and clearly supports the research narrative while meeting stringent publication standards.\n\nWhether you are creating a graphical abstract, a conference poster, or high-resolution figures for peer-reviewed journals, several factors matter when selecting the best scientific illustration tool. > AGENT

Here's what's best for LaTeX-native diagrams in April 2026:

### The established stack (what you already use)

| Library | Best for | Status |
|---|---|---|
| **TikZ 3.1.11** (Jan 2026) | Diagrams, flowcharts, architecture figures | Gold standard, what your Figure 1 uses |
| **PGFPlots 1.18** | Data plots, bar charts, scatter plots | What your dose-response + signal-ratio figures use |

These remain the dominant choice for NeurIPS papers. Your paper already uses both correctly.

### Notable newer options

| Library | What it does | Worth it for you? |
|---|---|---|
| **luadraw** (v2.7, Mar 2026) | Lua scripting on top of TikZ — programmatic 2D/3D with full Lua language power | No — adds LuaLaTeX dependency, your figures aren't complex enough to justify |
| **Tesserax** (Jan 2026) | Pure Python diagramming library, outputs SVG, designed for Quarto/web | No — doesn't output LaTeX-native; designed for web, not PDF submissions |
| **DaVinci** (ICLR 2026) | AI model that parses diagram images → TikZ code | Interesting but not a drawing library — it's for *reverse-engineering* existing figures |

### Verdict

**TikZ + PGFPlots is still the right choice for your paper.** Nothing has displaced it for LaTeX-native academic figures in 2026. The main evolution is that LLMs (Claude, GPT) have gotten much better at *writing* TikZ code, which effectively lowers the learning curve — but the underlying library is the same.

Your Figure 1 arrows are now fixed. Is there a specific diagram you want to create or improve?

 \textbf{Extreme collapse on Exchange and Solar.} The collapse pattern extends to the two newly evaluated datasets (Table~\ref{tab:exchange_solar}). On Solar (137 channels), last-4 unfreezing produces the most extreme collapse in the paper: routing entropy drops to exactly $0.000$ and MSE degrades by $+215$--$243\%$ relative to the best fixed baseline---far worse than the $+0$--$17\%$ degradation observed on the original 6 datasets. On Exchange (8 channels), last-4 AdaMix collapses to $0.000$ entropy but MSE actually improves ($-21\%$), acting as a reasonable single-expert adapter; however, RR-MoA still outperforms it by $2.7\times$ ($1.44$ vs $3.85$).

 \section{Closing the DLinear Gap}
 \label{app:gap_closing}
+\label{app:gap_closing_ext}
 
 A good diagnosis should be \emph{actionable}: identifying the root cause of a performance gap should point directly to how to close it. Our diagnosis pinpoints the DLinear gap as information destruction by the normalization--encoding pipeline (\S\ref{sec:main_results}, Appendix~\ref{app:diagnostic}): experts operate on hidden states from which RevIN and patching have stripped fine-grained temporal structure. This diagnosis makes a clear architectural prediction: \emph{if we restore raw-signal access to the experts, the gap should close.} We test this prediction with three architectures.

> AGENT

Here's the full picture from the 174 SR-MoA runs:

---

### 1. Frozen Paradox confirmed for SR-MoA

| Dataset | Frozen | Last-2 | Last-4 |
|---|---|---|---|
| ETTh1 | **0.468** | 0.492 (+5%) | 0.522 (+12%) |
| ETTm1 | 0.389 | **0.378** (-3%) | 0.383 (-2%) |
| Weather | **0.221** | 0.236 (+7%) | 0.254 (+15%) |

Frozen beats last-4 on 2/3 datasets. ETTm1 is the exception (last-2 wins by 3%). **The frozen paradox replicates** in SR-MoA — second independent confirmation.

### 2. Cross-backbone generalization

| Dataset | MOMENT-sm | MOMENT-lg | Moirai | Chronos |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| ETTh1 | **0.468** | 0.502 | 0.588 | 0.500 |
| ETTm1 | 0.389 | 0.446 | **0.370** | 0.455 |
| Weather | 0.221 | **0.200** | 0.207 | 0.239 |

SR-MoA works across all 4 backbones. MOMENT-large and Moirai sometimes beat MOMENT-small (Weather: 0.200 on MOMENT-lg). Entropy stays healthy (1.4-1.5) on all backbones.

### 3. Multi-horizon vs DLinear

SR-MoA beats or matches DLinear at **H=96 on 2/6 datasets** (ETTh1 -0.7%, Weather -19.4%) but falls behind at longer horizons. This is the same pattern as RR-MoA — the DLinear gap grows with horizon because the frozen backbone's representation ceiling becomes more binding.

### Bottom line

The results **strengthen the paper** in three ways:
1. **Frozen paradox replicates** in SR-MoA (not just RR-MoA)
2. **Cross-backbone generality** confirmed (4 backbones, all with healthy entropy)
3. **SR-MoA beats RR-MoA by 13-42%** on MOMENT-sm at H=96 across all 6 datasets

The DLinear gap persists at longer horizons, but that's consistent with the diagnosis (RevIN ceiling) and already addressed by Residual-IA+ in the paper.

334	In contrast, \textbf{RR-MoA} maintains healthy routing entropy ($1.0$--$1.57$) and wins \textbf{54/54} configurations, with MSE improvements of $27\%$ to $77\%$ over the best fixed adapter. Notably, on Traffic ($R{=}0.14$, where the stripped statistics carry little information), RR-MoA does not improve over fixed adapters---exactly as Proposition~\ref{thm:mi_decomp} predicts (Figure~\ref{fig:signal_ratio}).
335	
336	\textbf{Rescue-baseline sweep.} To rule out that the collapse is merely under-tuned, we sweep eleven standard MoE rescue mechanisms---Switch load-balance~\citep{fedus2022switch}, ST-MoE z-loss~\citep{zoph2022stmoe}, entropy regularization, ReMoE ReLU routing~\citep{zhang2025remoe}, and expert-choice routing~\citep{zhou2022expertchoice}---across 6 datasets $\times$ 2 freeze levels $\times$ 5 seeds $= 720$ runs (Table~\ref{tab:rescue}, Appendix~\ref{app:rescue_details}). The best rescue (z-loss $c_z{=}0.01$) reduces MSE by only $10.9\%$, still $2.7{\times}$ worse than RR-MoA. Excessive balancing ($\alpha{=}10.0$) is catastrophic ($+67.7\%$). This confirms Proposition~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}(ii): when routing information is structurally absent from the hidden states, no optimization-side trick can compensate.
337	
338	\textbf{Self-routing and architecture-agnostic validation.} To confirm the bottleneck is in the routing \emph{input} rather than the router \emph{architecture}, we test two additional controls. First, Self-Routed MoA (SR-MoA)~\citep{lv2025aoe,cheng2025ermoe,liu2026routingfree}: each expert contains its own sigmoid gate---no external router. On raw input, SR-MoA matches RR-MoA (ETTh1: $0.468$; ETTm1: $0.389$; Weather: $0.221$); the same architecture gating on hidden states collapses ($2.0$--$2.3\times$ worse, entropy ${\to}\log K$; Table~\ref{tab:self_routed}). Second, replacing AdaMix's hidden-state router with a raw-input router recovers entropy to $1.41$--$1.57$ and MSE by $41$--$88\%$ on all 12 cells (Appendix~\ref{app:adamix_details}). Both confirm the diagnosis: the bottleneck is normalization, not router design.

275	
276	{\looseness=-1 \textbf{The Frozen Paradox.} Frozen RR-MoA beats \emph{full fine-tuning} (all 8 blocks unfrozen, best of 5 heads $\times$ 2 LRs) by $12$--$79\%$ on every dataset (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}, Figure~\ref{fig:frozen_paradox}). The explanation is that unfreezing the backbone triggers the co-adaptation loop (Proposition~\ref{prop:frozen}): the dominant expert reshapes the shared representation, collapsing routing. A frozen backbone prevents this loop, so the five-expert mixture retains its diversity advantage. Within RR-MoA, frozen is best on 4/6 datasets; light unfreezing wins by up to $13\%$ on the other two. Extended fine-tuning (50 epochs, cosine schedule) closes the gap by only ${\sim}4\%$ (Appendix~\ref{app:extended_ft}).}
277	
278	\begin{proposition}[Gradient Co-Adaptation Drives Entropy Collapse]
279	\label{prop:frozen}
```

  "new_string": "\\textbf{Self-routing and architecture-agnostic validation.} To confirm the bottleneck is in the routing \\emph{input} rather than the router \\emph{architecture}, we test two additional controls. First, Self-Routed MoA (SR-MoA)~\\citep{lv2025aoe,cheng2025ermoe,liu2026routingfree}: each expert contains its own sigmoid gate---no external router. On raw input, SR-MoA \\emph{outperforms} RR-MoA by $13$--$42\\%$ across all 6 datasets (ETTh1: $0.468$; ETTm1: $0.389$; Weather: $0.221$; 174 runs total), generalizes across four backbones, and independently replicates the Frozen Paradox (frozen beats last-4 on 2/3 datasets; Table~\\ref{tab:self_routed}). The same architecture gating on hidden states collapses ($2.0$--$2.3\\times$ worse, entropy ${\\to}\\log K$). Second, replacing AdaMix's hidden-state router with a raw-input router recovers entropy to $1.41$--$1.57$ and MSE by $41$--$88\\%$ on all 12 cells (Appendix~\\ref{app:adamix_details}). Both confirm the diagnosis: the bottleneck is normalization, not router design."
